~~~text
\section{Generalized synthetic control: preserve the panel target across a wrapper}
\label{exec21:gsynth-scope}

A branch manager evaluates a staggered rollout of a servicing policy. Some
branches adopt early, some late, and some remain untreated. A useful analysis
must say whether its answer concerns a typical branch, a typical treated
branch-month, or a portfolio weighted by an explicitly chosen exposure.
These targets differ before any model is fitted. A package wrapper can change
the answer by losing a weight or substituting an estimator even when the
underlying numerical engine is otherwise appropriate.

The selected pre-cutoff \texttt{gsynth}~1.4.0 source is a compatibility
wrapper around \texttt{fect}. Its maintainers describe the wrapper as retained
solely for backward compatibility. The reviewed dependency candidate is
\texttt{fect}~2.4.5, declared on 29~May~2026 and published through the selected
CRAN revision on 30~May. Matching pinned upstream and CRAN wrapper, manual,
license and change-log files agree byte for byte. This establishes a concrete
source comparison; it does not establish an installed combination. The
wrapper's declared lower bound, \texttt{fect >= 2.0.0}, permits multiple engine
versions with potentially different behavior. \citep{exec21_gsynth_wrapper,exec21_gsynth_engine}

\subsection{Weights and exposure duration define the business quantity}
\label{exec21:gsynth-target}

Let $i$ index branches and $t$ calendar periods. Let $D_{it}$ indicate an
active intervention, $\mathcal S$ denote the retained treated cells, and
$\delta_{it}=Y_{it}(1)-Y_{it}(0)$ the corresponding effect in stated monetary
units. With positive prespecified weights $w_{it}$, two possible targets are
\begin{equation}
\label{exec21:gsynth-weights}
 \tau_{\rm cell}=\frac{1}{|\mathcal S|}\sum_{(i,t)\in\mathcal S}\delta_{it},
 \qquad
 \tau_w=\frac{\sum_{(i,t)\in\mathcal S}w_{it}\delta_{it}}
                  {\sum_{(i,t)\in\mathcal S}w_{it}}.
\end{equation}
If $\mathcal I$ is the retained treated-unit set and $T_i^+$ its observed
treated periods, a third target gives each unit equal weight:
\begin{equation}
\label{exec21:gsynth-units}
 \tau_{\rm unit}=\frac{1}{|\mathcal I|}\sum_{i\in\mathcal I}
       \frac{1}{T_i^+}\sum_{t:(i,t)\in\mathcal S}\delta_{it}.
\end{equation}
Identification of the missing $Y_{it}(0)$ is a separate question from these
definitions. A sample's retained cells need not represent the original
portfolio when observation or unit-removal rules depend on risk or outcome.

The selected \texttt{gsynth} manual promises that \texttt{weight} specifies
the weighting variable for the weighted average effect. The complete
95-line wrapper declares that argument but never reads it again and never
passes it to \texttt{fect}. Its documented weighted interface therefore has
a concrete source-level defect. A successful call or the stored call string
would not demonstrate that the requested weights reached estimation.
This is a source finding; the current review has not executed the native
wrapper. \citep{exec21_gsynth_wrapper}

For an exact illustration, two equally represented treated cells have effects
$-6$ and $10$ dollars. Their unweighted mean is $2$, whereas weights $9$ and
$1$ give $(-54+10)/10=-22/5=-4.4$. If the decision assumes a cost of USD~1
on the corresponding average-exposure basis, the two calculated margins are
USD~1 and USD~$-5.4$. A weighting omission can reverse the indicated decision;
these are authored arithmetic examples, not measured branch results or
observed outputs of either package. \citep{exec21_gsynth_reference}

Exposure duration creates another distinction. Suppose one unit experiences
effect $10$ in each of three treated periods, while another experiences
effect $-6$ in one treated period. Equation~\eqref{exec21:gsynth-weights}
gives $\tau_{\rm cell}=6$ and equation~\eqref{exec21:gsynth-units} gives
$\tau_{\rm unit}=2$. At an assumed per-period cost of $4$, averaging the
net effects using the same respective measures gives margins $2$ and $-2$.
A longer observed treatment history should not silently make one branch
three times as influential in an analysis intended to describe a typical
branch. \citep{exec21_gsynth_reference}

The selected engine exposes both treated-cell and unit-average calculations.
It also distinguishes weights for outcome-model fitting from weights for
aggregation. Supplying \texttt{W} fills both roles; supplying only
\texttt{W.agg} is intended to change aggregation, while \texttt{W.est}
alone leaves canonical aggregation unweighted. The inspected source rejects
distinct columns for the two roles, despite an error message referring to
earlier versions and a future release. Its executed conditional, rather than
that stale version message, determines the limitation. Zero-weight rows are
dropped, and weighted aggregates are routed into the ordinary output slots.
A proposed adapter must therefore record both the requested role and the
actual retained population; blindly multiplying supplied weights is not a
general substitute for missing role support. \citep{exec21_gsynth_engine}

\subsection{A factor model needs information in the relevant directions}
\label{exec21:gsynth-rank}

A useful working model for an untreated outcome is
\begin{equation}
\label{exec21:gsynth-factor}
 Y_{it}(0)=\alpha_i+\xi_t+x_{it}^{\mathsf T}\beta
                     +\lambda_i^{\mathsf T}f_t+\varepsilon_{it},
\end{equation}
where $x_{it}$ contains covariates with a justified intervention-time meaning,
$f_t$ represents common latent factors and $\lambda_i$ is the unit's loading.
Even if donor observations reveal $f_t$ exactly, a treated unit's pre-period
outcomes must determine the component of $\lambda_i$ that matters after
treatment. Covariates caused by the intervention, anticipation, spillovers
to donors, or changing loadings require additional arguments. A low prediction
error on past observations cannot establish those substantive conditions.

For a precise obstruction, suppress the intercepts, observed covariates and
errors. Let the factors in two pre-periods and one post-period be
\begin{equation}
\label{exec21:gsynth-witness}
 f_1=(1,0)^{\mathsf T},\quad f_2=(2,0)^{\mathsf T},\quad
 f_3=(1,1)^{\mathsf T}.
\end{equation}
Donor loadings $(0,1)$ and $(1,1)$ generate observed paths $(0,0,1)$ and
$(1,2,2)$. A treated loading of either $(2,0)$ or $(2,4)$ gives the same
pre-treatment path $(2,4)$. Hold its observed post-treatment outcome at $8$.
The two compatible untreated post outcomes are $2$ and $6$, giving effects
$6$ and $2$. With an assumed intervention cost of $4$, the net values are $2$
and $-2$. The complete observed panels agree between these two worlds.
\citep{exec21_gsynth_reference}

Writing $F_{\rm pre}$ for the pre-period factor matrix, the direction
$v=(0,1)^{\mathsf T}$ satisfies
\begin{equation}
\label{exec21:gsynth-null}
 F_{\rm pre}v=0,\qquad f_3^{\mathsf T}v=1.
\end{equation}
The pre-period factor rank is one while the full factor rank is two.
Repeating the two pre-factor rows over six distinct calendar periods does
not change that rank. A third donor with loading $(2,1)$ can raise the donor
count without resolving this missing treated-unit information. Thus minimum
period and donor counts are useful guards, but are not proofs of the relevant
rank condition. This example lies outside full pre-factor-rank qualification;
it is not evidence that an estimator valid under that qualification fails.
\citep{exec21_gsynth_reference}

The inspected engine includes count/rank restrictions and, in one unbounded
treated-loading branch, solves using the inverse of
$F_{\rm pre}^{\mathsf T}F_{\rm pre}$. A failed solve returns unavailable
effect fields. Near singularity can remain problematic even when the solve
returns. Its inspected rank-selection paths aggregate cross-validation
scores and can apply a minimum-error, one-standard-error or legacy
one-percent selection rule. The final rule may override an intermediate
rank update. These are predictive selection procedures, not tests proving
counterfactual identification. A restricted loading model, an informative
additional pre-period, an external loading restriction or a prospective
experiment could address a particular ambiguity, but each changes the
information or assumptions of the problem. \citep{exec21_gsynth_engine}

\subsection{Defaults, missingness and uncertainty must travel together}
\label{exec21:gsynth-inference}

In the selected wrapper, the default estimator is \texttt{gsynth}. Legacy
\texttt{EM=TRUE} selects \texttt{ife} only if the estimator argument was
omitted. The default requested inference is parametric for the gsynth route
and bootstrap for the IFE or matrix-completion routes. A parametric request
for the latter routes is changed to bootstrap with a warning; the legacy
name \texttt{nonparametric} maps to bootstrap. The wrapper also sets unit
fixed effects, five CV folds and tolerance $10^{-3}$, whereas the direct
engine's inspected defaults include two-way effects, twenty folds and
tolerance $10^{-5}$. Direct-engine adoption therefore requires explicit
configuration rather than assuming identical defaults.
\citep{exec21_gsynth_wrapper,exec21_gsynth_engine}

The engine's gsynth route forces a never-treated donor pool and rejects
treatment reversals. Too few pre-treatment observations or excessive
missingness can remove units; the returned result must disclose this change
in population. A package family's support for switching treatments does not
override the restrictions of this selected method. Missing-cell masks also
enter fitting and effect aggregation, so reporting only the number of
input rows conceals the denominator actually used. \citep{exec21_gsynth_engine}

For parametric uncertainty, the selected engine requires the isolated
never-treated pool. Its automatic error mode uses an autoregressive strategy
when cells are missing and an empirical strategy otherwise; explicitly
requested empirical or wild modes require a complete panel. In inspected
case-bootstrap code, units or cluster identifiers are resampled, with
support-conditioned retries in unit-resampling branches. Ordinary jackknife
with a cluster argument emits a warning that clustering is ignored. These
are different sampling mechanisms; a generic confidence-interval label
cannot make them interchangeable. \citep{exec21_gsynth_engine}

The inspected average-effect uncertainty uses the standard deviation of
bootstrap draws with missing values removed and, by default, a normal
interval. The basic-pivot option has a different construction. Any future
qualification must preserve attempted, valid and failed draws, resampling
units, selection/refitting behavior and reasons for unavailable output.
Dropping failed draws from a standard-deviation calculation is not evidence
that inference remains valid. Dependence across branches, common calendar
shocks, model misspecification and post-selection uncertainty each require a
declared law and a specific argument. This source comparison has executed
neither native package and makes no empirical coverage claim.
\citep{exec21_gsynth_engine}

\subsection{Reuse through an explicit panel contract}
\label{exec21:gsynth-integration}

The proposal retains the pinned wrapper as a compatibility reference and
favors evaluating a direct \texttt{fect} worker if a prototype is funded.
The ignored weight and implicit remapping make an unrestricted wrapper
pass-through unsuitable for a new interface. Porting the engine immediately

Before admission, a public reference comparison should reproduce the
wrapper's weight omission and compare an explicitly configured engine on

durations, missingness, reversals, donor shortages, weak factor support,
rank resets, failed solves, cluster warnings and bounded runtime failures.
Known-law factor panels can test selected calibration claims before any

These public comparisons can establish whether a panel prototype merits
~~~

Before:
~~~text
\chapter{Panel effects, event studies and synthetic controls}
\label{sw:panels}

The preceding chapters developed panel contrasts, event-path uncertainty and
comparison trajectories in detail. This chapter brings their consequences
back to the banking request: whose effect is being estimated, at which event
time, under which comparison law, and with what uncertainty? The examples
below show why a common reporting interface must preserve those distinctions.
The CausalImpact study adds a Bayesian prediction approach whose causal
interpretation depends on an unaffected comparison process.

\section{Population averages and treated-group averages}

A bank-wide policy evaluation can target all eligible accounts, the accounts
actually treated, or a particular rollout cohort at a particular horizon.
Even perfectly computed effects can support opposite decisions when the target
population is silently changed.

In a project example, half the eligible accounts belong to a high-response
baseline stratum \(H\). Treatment is given with probability \(0.8\) in \(H\)
and \(0.2\) outside it. Suppose the incremental gross contribution of treatment
over the declared horizon is CNY 400 in \(H\) and CNY 100 outside it. These
are stipulated stratum effects, with no estimation noise. The population
average treatment effect is
\[
 \operatorname{ATE}=0.5(400)+0.5(100)=250.
\]
Bayes' rule gives
\[
 \Pr(H\mid A=1)
 =\frac{0.5(0.8)}{0.5(0.8)+0.5(0.2)}=0.8,
\]
so the average effect in the treated group is
\[
 \operatorname{ATT}=0.8(400)+0.2(100)=340.
\]
At a hypothetical CNY 300 treatment cost, average net benefit is \(-50\)
for the eligible population and \(+40\) for a group with the treated-group
composition. The example does not authorize future targeting using an observed
treatment label; it shows why a result's population is a necessary part of its
meaning.

DRDID's selected documentation explicitly targets ATT and distinguishes panel
data from stationary repeated cross sections. A shared API must retain both
facts. A repeated cross section does not supply the same account-level changes
as a panel, even if its columns have the same names. A result labeled only
``average effect'' would hide the target distinction illustrated above.
Double robustness also operates within the procedure's identification and
nuisance-model framework; it does not establish parallel trends or assignment
truth. \cite{v12_sw_drdid}

\section{Staggered timing and generated outcomes}

A rollout across branches or customer groups creates treatment cohorts and
relative event times. A useful request must state the first treatment time,
whether treatment is absorbing, which units serve as controls, which relative
times are supported, and how effects are aggregated. Otherwise two event-study
plots can represent different populations at the same displayed lag.

did2s documents a first stage fitted on untreated observations and a second
stage using the full sample. The standard-error calculation is adjusted for
that procedure and its clustering contract. Passing the residualized outcome
to an ordinary second regression and reporting that regression's default
standard error would not preserve the documented method. The proposed adapter
must retain the first-stage sample, generated outcome, omitted periods,
treatment timing and inference settings. \cite{v12_sw_did2s}

eventstudyinteract is community Stata code. Its selected help file describes
an interaction-weighted estimator: estimate cohort-by-relative-time effects,
estimate cohort shares, then aggregate with those shares. It permits
never-treated or last-treated control cohorts; when the last-treated cohort
is used, periods in which that cohort receives treatment must be excluded.
The help file warns that the cited validity result covers balanced panels
without covariates, while the implementation also accepts unbalanced data.
Software acceptance of that input is therefore not the same as an inspected
validity argument for it. \cite{v12_sw_eventstudyinteract}

The package stores the interaction-weighted estimates and covariance in
\texttt{e(b\_iw)} and \texttt{e(V\_iw)}. The ordinary \texttt{e(b)} and
\texttt{e(V)} entries belong to the underlying interacted regression. An
adapter exporting the familiar default slots would risk returning the wrong
object. This is a concrete reason to qualify the statistical handoff rather
than merely test that Stata can launch.

The same documentation describes pointwise intervals. Simultaneous coverage
of an entire event path is a different property. As a project probability
calculation, ten independent intervals each with exactly 0.95 coverage cover
all ten targets with probability
\[
 0.95^{10}=0.5987369\ldots.
\]
Actual event-study estimates need not be independent, and this number is not
a measurement of eventstudyinteract. It demonstrates that a collection of
pointwise labels cannot itself establish a simultaneous guarantee. A report
must identify the family of targets and the inference method supporting any
joint statement.

The Stata runtime introduces a commercial dependency, but it does not make
eventstudyinteract a built-in vendor capability. The pinned wrapper's MIT
license is established in Section~\ref{sec:exec24-eventstudy-reuse}; runtime,
dependency and intended-distribution obligations remain separate. A prospective
Stata bridge consequently needs a licensed runtime and a review of the complete
distribution. Comparing that bridge with a vendor's own estimator
requires the vendor's dated technical documentation, not inference from this
community package.

\section{Synthetic controls and the source of uncertainty}

When a policy changes for one region, branch group or business line, a donor
combination can form a counterfactual trajectory. The donor definition,
pre-treatment period, scaling, fitting criterion and post-treatment horizon
are part of the scientific design. The weight optimizer is one component of
that design. A small fitting loss does not by itself explain why post-treatment
prediction error should have a particular distribution.

The inspected Synth interface takes preprocessed predictor and outcome
matrices and reports donor/predictor weights and optimization losses. This
makes it a candidate for a controlled weight-construction operation. The
selected passages do not supply a universal placebo or confidence-coverage
guarantee. The product should report fitting loss and donor dependence as
diagnostics and attach only an uncertainty procedure whose assumptions and
target have been separately stated. \cite{v12_sw_synth}

augsynth's selected dispatcher distinguishes single-outcome, multiple-outcome
and staggered settings, with distinct balance and regularization controls.
Selecting the family name is therefore insufficient to select a method.
Section~\ref{sec:exec24-augsynth-reuse} develops the selected ridge, tuning and
uncertainty paths. Its findings do not qualify every staggered or
multiple-outcome request, and native admission still requires matched tests.
The author-repository snapshot was available after a CRAN
mirror lookup failed; that establishes source access for the inspected
revision, not a CRAN release history. \cite{v12_sw_augsynth}

scpi's selected documentation explicitly distinguishes in-sample and
out-of-sample uncertainty and exposes donor constraint sets, solvers and
simulation choices. A generic scalar ``standard error'' field would compress
these components into a misleadingly uniform object. The proposed integration
retains the constraint set, solver outcome, simulation identity and the
assumptions used for prediction error. It also records failures: an infeasible
or numerically failed solve does not become a zero effect, and changing the
constraint set to obtain a solution changes the reported procedure.
\cite{v12_sw_scpi}

For banking examples, donor contamination is a substantive concern to assess:
a nominally untreated branch may be affected by shared pricing, national
communications or customer movement. This is a proposed design diagnostic,
not an observed defect in a package or dataset. The software should make donor
membership and exclusions reviewable. It cannot verify lack of spillovers
solely from a donor weight vector.

A Bayesian structural time-series analysis offers a different account of
counterfactual prediction and its uncertainty. The next study explains what
its posterior draws represent and why a precise predictive difference can
still fail to identify the effect of the banking action.


~~~

~~~text
revision, not a CRAN release history. \citep{v12_sw_augsynth}

scpi's selected documentation explicitly distinguishes in-sample and
out-of-sample uncertainty and exposes donor constraint sets, solvers and
simulation choices. A generic scalar ``standard error'' field would compress
these components into a misleadingly uniform object. The proposed integration
retains the constraint set, solver outcome, simulation identity and the
assumptions used for prediction error. It also records failures: an infeasible
or numerically failed solve does not become a zero effect, and changing the
constraint set to obtain a solution changes the reported procedure.
\citep{v12_sw_scpi}

For banking examples, donor contamination is a substantive concern to assess:
a nominally untreated branch may be affected by shared pricing, national
communications or customer movement. This is a proposed design diagnostic,
not an observed defect in a package or dataset. The software should make donor
membership and exclusions reviewable. It cannot verify lack of spillovers
solely from a donor weight vector.

A Bayesian structural time-series analysis offers a different account of
counterfactual prediction and its uncertainty. The next study explains what
its posterior draws represent and why a precise predictive difference can
still fail to identify the effect of the banking action.


~~~

## Edit 158
docs/release/2026-10-29/source/docs/writing-repair/2026-10-29/src/6c3b0c7efc6a0e09.tex

Use parenthetical author-year syntax for sentence-final citations in the complete panel synthesis and CausalImpact comparison. Exact segment 2 of two around the unchanged nested input; split for source-context replay.

Substantive destination: All original words, definitions, equations, adverse examples, assumptions, scientific limitations, alternatives, labels and citation keys survive unchanged in the same source.

Before:
~~~text


\section{Fitting weights and target weights}

fect's selected documentation distinguishes weights used in counterfactual
fitting from weights used to aggregate treatment effects. The latter do not
automatically implement inverse-probability or doubly robust confounding
correction. A fitting weight changes the optimization or fitting population;
an aggregation weight changes the reported average. The two can be numerically
similar vectors and still represent different operations. \cite{v12_sw_fect}

This distinction is especially important when a bank moves between
account-weighted, exposure-weighted and monetary-loss-weighted effects.
If effects \(\tau_i\) are reported as
\[
 \tau_w=\frac{\sum_i w_i\tau_i}{\sum_i w_i},
\]
then the definition and availability time of \(w_i\) are part of the target.
A realized post-treatment balance weight can change the population being
averaged and may itself be affected by treatment. The formula alone does not
justify its causal interpretation. The interface should retain the weight's
meaning and clock, including whether it was fixed before action.

fect also documents restrictions on its parametric bootstrap. Its 2.4.5
manual retains some language about earlier version roadmaps. The implementation
review in Section~\ref{sec:exec24-fect-reuse} resolves selected method gates,
reversal restrictions and resampling paths while retaining termination and
native-validation limits. The proposed trial therefore selects an exact fitting
method, aggregation rule and resampling law and checks them together. A
successful counterfactual fit cannot substitute for that complete procedure.

The resulting panel strategy is plural: evaluate ATT procedures, staged
regression, interaction-weighted event studies, donor weighting and
counterfactual factor approaches for the questions they support. The product
may offer a coherent report across them while preserving incompatible targets
and assumptions. Matched comparisons should align populations, horizons and
information, and they should retain a losing method or an unsupported case
instead of forcing every candidate into one ranking.

These comparisons concern effects of declared interventions and trajectories
under declared counterfactual laws. A lag coefficient in a dynamic panel or
a response to a structural funding shock requires a further account of
endogeneity and shock identification. The next chapter develops that
additional distinction.


~~~

## Edit 159
docs/release/2026-10-29/source/docs/writing-repair/2026-10-29/src/8f312b9d3e958a97.tex

Use parenthetical author-year syntax for sentence-final citations in the complete panel synthesis and CausalImpact comparison.

Substantive destination: All original words, definitions, equations, adverse examples, assumptions, scientific limitations, alternatives, labels and citation keys survive unchanged in the same source.

Before:
~~~text
\section{Bayesian intervention analysis for branch campaigns}
\label{exec13:causalimpact-reuse}

A bank that launches a campaign in one branch group may want the daily path of
additional applications, rather than a single coefficient pooled over all
branches. Google CausalImpact is a substantive candidate for this question.
Its Bayesian structural time-series approach models how the treated series
would have evolved without the intervention, using its earlier history and,
when available, contemporaneous control series. The proposed reuse decision
is to retain the R implementation as a reference and prospective baseline,
with an isolated R adapter conditional on a compatible environment and
reviewed behavior. No such adapter or fitted package comparison has been
executed here. This disposition preserves a useful alternative without
turning a forecasting model into a general identification engine.
\cite{exec13_ci_reuse,exec13_ci_r}

Here the package name denotes \texttt{google/CausalImpact}. It does not denote
the aGrUM symbolic object discussed in the identification survey. The
selected R source is version 1.4.1 at the commit beginning
\texttt{0f5bf8d97cfb}, committed on 31 March 2026. Its DESCRIPTION retains a
24 September 2025 package date, but the later commit raises the required R
version to 4.5.0. An unchanged package version is consequently insufficient
to reproduce the environment. The repository contains a three-platform
release-R check workflow and substantive input, model and inference tests;
inspection of those files establishes intended checks, not that the checks
passed on the proposed platform. \cite{exec13_ci_r,exec13_ci_semantics}

\subsection{The target and the identifying restriction}

Let the intervention begin after date \(T_0\), let \(H\) be the declared
post-intervention horizon, and write \(Y_t(0)\) for the outcome that the
treated branch group would have produced without the campaign. The realized
path contrast is \(\Delta_t=Y_t-Y_t(0)\). A posterior for this missing path
uses the observed pre-intervention response and the control history
\(X_{1:T_0+H}\). In the state-space formulation of Brodersen and colleagues,
\begin{align}
 Y_t(0)&=Z_t^{\mathsf T}\alpha_t+\varepsilon_t,\\
 \alpha_{t+1}&=T_t\alpha_t+R_t\eta_t.
\end{align}
Here \(\alpha_t\) is a latent state, \(Z_t\) maps it to the scalar response,
\(T_t\) evolves that state, and \(R_t\) maps state innovations into it.
The observation and state innovations have the specified Gaussian laws;
trend, seasonality and regression components determine the matrices and
their parameters. Posterior simulation integrates over the stated parameter,
state and variable-selection uncertainty. The inherited method supplies a
joint predictive law for the entire missing response path, not merely
separate forecasts at each date. \cite{exec13_ci_brodersen2015}

For causal attribution, the control series must remain unaffected by the
intervention, and the pre-period relationship must continue to describe the
untreated post-period response. Neither an accurate pre-period fit nor a
posterior excluding zero proves these restrictions. National advertising,
shared customer acquisition channels, a branch closure or a simultaneous
pricing change can make the proposed donor history inappropriate. A
post-period control may be available for retrospective analysis while being
unavailable when a campaign is first planned. The proposal must distinguish
these two information sets before using retrospective fit to justify a
prospective decision. \cite{exec13_ci_r,exec13_ci_brodersen2015}

An exact construction shows the consequence. Let \(A_t=0\) for eight
pre-period days and \(A_t=1\) for four campaign days. Set
\(x_t^0=10+t\) and suppose the observed daily application count and control
index satisfy
\[
 X_t=x_t^0+15A_t,\qquad
 Y_t=100+2x_t^0+20A_t.
\]
In this first law, the campaign increases applications by 20 per day and
also changes the control index by 15. The pre-period relationship is exactly
\(Y_t=100+2X_t\). Continuing that relationship into the post-period gives
\[
 Y_t-(100+2X_t)=20-2(15)=-10.
\]
The same twelve observed rows arise under a second law in which the
exogenous control path is the observed \(X_t\), the campaign leaves it
unchanged, and the response equation is
\(Y_t=100+2X_t-10A_t\). Under that law the effect is genuinely negative.
The exogenous control path is allowed to shift on the campaign date; no
random timing assumption excludes that possibility in this construction.
Thus the identical observations, including perfect pre-period fit, support
opposite effects under different control-invariance assumptions. This is a
project counterexample to automatic attribution, not a fitted CausalImpact
result or a claim that its algorithm returns the exact linear forecast.
\cite{exec13_cimpact_examples}

More generally, with pre-period slope \(\beta\), donor contamination
\(\gamma A_t\) and response effect \(\tau A_t\), the continued linear
contrast is \(\tau-\beta\gamma\). This decomposition makes a sensitivity
analysis interpretable: an owner can state a plausible range for donor
spillover and inspect when the sign changes. It does not estimate that range
from the same ambiguous history. Similarly, an unmodeled untreated
post-period shift \(\delta\) would enter the contrast as
\(\tau+\delta-\beta\gamma\). A richer state model can represent some
changes, but cannot learn from missing untreated outcomes which part of a
coincident break was caused by the campaign.

For an investment illustration, stipulate a net value of USD 1 per additional
application and a four-day campaign cost of USD 20. The two laws imply
\(4(20)-20=60\) and \(4(-10)-20=-60\) dollars. These are deliberately
specified values, not measured bank economics. Their role is to show why
obtaining a defensible unaffected control or a randomized comparison can
matter more than reducing Monte Carlo error around the wrong counterfactual.
\cite{exec13_cimpact_examples}

\subsection{What the selected implementation actually summarizes}

The R data path takes the response in its first column and controls in later
columns. It checks numeric input, requires more than three total rows and a
pre-period spanning at least three row positions, and rejects missing
covariates. Some out-of-range period endpoints are clipped with warnings.
The automatic path standardizes using pre-period moments and masks the
response after the pre-period before fitting. The supplied-model path instead
requires a separately fitted \texttt{bsts} model with the post-treatment
response missing. These details are part of the computation's information
boundary; an adapter must not silently train on the response it is supposed
to predict counterfactually. \cite{exec13_ci_semantics}

The automatic model includes a local level and optionally seasonality and
regression. The inspected defaults include 1,000 iterations, standardization,
local-level prior scale 0.01, no seasonal component and static regression.
The static regression construction also supplies an expected model size of
three, expected \(R^2=0.8\), and prior degrees of freedom 50. These are
modeling choices to expose and examine, rather than bank-independent
constants that need no justification. The source passes \texttt{seed=1} to
its \texttt{bsts} calls, while predictive observation-noise simulation uses R
random generation. Reproducibility therefore needs the full runtime and
random-state contract; preserving only a displayed seed is insufficient.
The number of iterations is not a convergence certificate.
\cite{exec13_ci_semantics}

The distinction between latent-state means and predictive draws matters.
The implementation averages state contributions for point predictions, but
uses trajectories including simulated observation noise for predictive
quantiles. For draw \(b\), the reported effect transformation is
\[
 \Delta_t^{(b)}=Y_t-\widetilde Y_t^{(b)},\qquad
 C_h^{(b)}=\sum_{t=T_0+1}^{T_0+h}\Delta_t^{(b)}.
\]
Quantiles of \(C_h^{(b)}\) retain dependence between dates. They are not
obtained by adding the marginal lower and upper bounds. The source also
forces cumulative impact to zero before the post-period begins; that
convention must not be misreported as an empirical pre-treatment causal
test. \cite{exec13_ci_semantics,exec13_ci_brodersen2015}

For example, consider four artificial two-day predictive draws:
\[
(80,120),\quad(120,80),\quad(80,120),\quad(120,80).
\]
Each marginal central 95 percent
empirical interval is \([80,120]\), whereas every path sums to 200. The
interval of sums is \([200,200]\); adding marginal endpoints gives
\([160,240]\). If the observed two-day total is 220, the corresponding
draw-wise cumulative effect is always 20. This finite arithmetic checks the
aggregation rule only. It is neither an MCMC experiment nor a coverage result.
\cite{exec13_cimpact_examples}

Relative effects require another distinction. The inspected summary computes
draw-wise relative totals
\[
 R^{(b)}=\frac{\sum_tY_t}{\sum_t\widetilde Y_t^{(b)}}-1
\]
and then summarizes those ratios. A ratio of posterior means can differ
sharply. With actual total 10 and three artificial counterfactual totals
\(-1,1,2\), the ratios are \(-11,9,4\), whose mean is \(2/3\); dividing
10 by the mean counterfactual total and subtracting one gives 14. Zero
denominators are undefined, and small or negative denominators can make a
percentage difficult to interpret. The proposed report retains absolute
effects and explicit denominator diagnostics rather than clipping such
values into a plausible-looking percentage. \cite{exec13_ci_semantics,exec13_cimpact_examples}

The field called \texttt{p} is a one-sided simulation tail-area calculation.
The inspected implementation appends the observed total to the predictive
totals and takes the smaller inclusive tail proportion. For \(B\) predictive
draws its smallest positive value is \(1/(B+1)\); the four-draw construction
above gives \(1/5\), not zero. Its meaning depends on the specified predictive
model and simulation. It is not a design-based randomization probability, a
proof that the causal assumptions hold, or a posterior probability that a
point-null model is true. An interface should preserve the source field and
explain this definition instead of converting every uncertainty measure into
the same generic significance label. \cite{exec13_ci_semantics,exec13_cimpact_examples}

Finally, units determine useful aggregation. Brodersen and colleagues
distinguish a cumulative flow effect from an average stock effect. Daily
additional applications can be summed over the campaign. An extra USD 10,000
of deposit balance observed on each of three dates is USD 10,000 at the last
date; summing those snapshots produces USD 30,000-days under a daily exposure
convention, not USD 30,000 of new deposits or revenue. Funding value would
need a specified balance-time and margin calculation. The adapter request
therefore includes whether the response is a flow, a stock, a rate or an
index, its units and its aggregation rule.
\cite{exec13_ci_brodersen2015,exec13_cimpact_examples}

\subsection{Calendars, failures and a proposed adapter boundary}

The source saves the original time labels and then replaces the internal
time index with sequential row numbers. Its own comment notes that this
precludes automatic filling of missing time points. The missing-date tests
check that omitted labels remain omitted from the output. A Friday and the
following Monday may therefore be neighboring model steps in a sparse input.
For a bank, business-day dynamics and calendar-day accrual are different
choices. Before fitting, the proposed adapter validates a declared calendar,
row uniqueness, time-zone treatment and period boundaries. Filling omitted
rows, carrying forward balances or aggregating transactions would require
an explicit transformation; each can change the estimand or state evolution.
\cite{exec13_ci_semantics}

Constant, entirely missing or insufficiently observed response series cause
the selected model builder to warn and return no fitted model. The wrapper
can still return an object with the CausalImpact class and unavailable
inferences. Checking only the object's class would misclassify that failure
as success. The proposed output boundary instead requires a fitted model,
usable draws and the requested summaries, while retaining warnings and
missing-value reasons. Missing controls, inadmissible periods, failed
inference, unavailable relative summaries and an unsupported scientific
request need distinguishable responses. A treatment-affected donor is a
scientific restriction to assess; it is not equivalent to a numerical error.
\cite{exec13_ci_semantics,exec13_ci_reuse}

For a future R worker, the request should bind the response definition,
units, calendar, pre/post periods, donor identities, supplied causal
restrictions, data provenance, missingness policy, model specification,
priors, iteration and random-state settings, and a resource limit. The
response should retain source/runtime identities, effective periods,
warnings, posterior draw identity or a verifiable draw artifact, point and
cumulative summaries with their uncertainty type, denominator diagnostics
and execution status. Neither the generated prose report nor a generic
``success'' field substitutes for these quantities. Inputs and outputs would
cross a versioned file or process boundary; the existing CLI and MCP service
does not acquire this behavior merely because the contract is specified.
\cite{exec13_ci_reuse}

The first behavioral comparison should run the same frozen fixture directly
in R and through the adapter in the same environment. It should compare
effective periods, row labels, warnings, draw dimensions and numerical
summaries, not only a plotted effect. Separate fixtures should cover a missing
date, missing controls, a constant response, a one-day post-period, a gap
between periods, out-of-range endpoints and a supplied model with incorrectly
retained post-treatment outcomes. The exact constructions above provide
independent checks of aggregation and reporting; they do not replace a
matched native run. A subsequent prospectively designed simulation can
assess recovery under an explicit observation law and retain contaminated
donors and structural breaks as failures. None of these future fitted
comparisons is claimed as completed. \cite{exec13_ci_reuse}

\subsection{Language paths, licenses and the reuse decision}

The R package declares Apache 2.0. Its license requires preserving the
applicable license and attribution notices, marking modifications and
carrying forward any applicable NOTICE material when distributing derivative
work. Its dependency stack is not thereby assigned the same license. The
inspected cutoff CRAN mirrors contain \texttt{bsts} 0.9.11,
\texttt{Boom} 0.9.17 and \texttt{BoomSpikeSlab} 1.2.7. Their descriptions
declare compilation requirements, R constraints and, for the latter two,
LGPL 2.1 alternatives tied to their license files. The \texttt{bsts}
declaration is \texttt{LGPL-2.1 | MIT + file LICENSE}; its companion file
supplies the copyright holder and year. The inspected Boom description also
identifies incorporated third-party code. Any distributed build consequently
needs its own dependency and notice inventory, including the applicable
LGPL source or relinking route. These declarations are source evidence, not
a solved dependency lock or blanket redistribution clearance.
\cite{exec13_ci_r,exec13_ci_dependencies}

The selected R dependency requirement is material: the observed workspace
has R 4.1.2 and none of the seven queried CausalImpact/direct or modeling
packages installed. Replacing the pin with an older release would change
the reviewed candidate. A separate R 4.5-or-later environment with pinned
compatible compiled dependencies is the concrete future route. This
environment cost, data/calendar preparation, donor justification, Monte
Carlo diagnostics, adapter fixtures and maintenance belong in the prototype
budget. No measured runtime or monetary integration estimate is available
from this source review. \cite{exec13_ci_reuse,exec13_ci_dependencies}

For Python, the R README recommends \texttt{google/tfp-causalimpact}.
At the separately selected 5 October 2026 commit beginning
\texttt{2bd0a64f7836}, its exported version is 0.2.0, its distribution name
is \texttt{tfp-causalimpact}, and its import namespace is
\texttt{causalimpact}. It exports \texttt{fit\_causalimpact} and distinct
data, model and inference option types. The project declares Python 3.9 or
later, TensorFlow and TensorFlow Probability among its dependencies, and
Apache 2.0 licensing. The declarations do not freeze compatible backend
versions. Its README describes a lineage involving the R package and Dafiti's
Python implementation and explicitly says it is not an officially supported
Google product. The R README aims for close results while allowing
randomized differences. No interchangeability, backend compatibility or
numerical equivalence has been demonstrated here.
\cite{exec13_ci_python,exec13_ci_r}

The preferred proposal path is therefore to reference the R implementation
and evaluate a narrow native wrapper before considering a port. Directly
adopting its estimation internals into the current library would import a
runtime and output contract that has not been exercised. Porting the R
wrapper and its sampler stack into Python or C++ would add responsibility
for numerical behavior, priors, random generation, failure handling and
dependency terms. The existing TFP implementation is a separate candidate
that should be compared before undertaking such a port. Its backend source,
license closure and native comparison remain deferred. Shared import names
are insufficient package identity. \cite{exec13_ci_reuse,exec13_ci_python}

Alternatives should be selected by the question. A simple seasonal forecast
is a transparent predictive baseline; a donor-weight procedure emphasizes
an explicitly restricted comparison group; a panel design is preferable
when its assignment and population assumptions match the decision; and a
randomized rollout can address causal ambiguity that predictive refinement
leaves intact. The earlier survey describes the respective packages. A
comparison should align the branch group, outcome units, horizon, available
information and uncertainty meaning before ranking losses. A package that
answers a different estimand is not a losing implementation of the same
request.

The research owner must justify the donor and stability restrictions and
declare the target. The adapter owner must supply the compatible environment,
dependency notices, calendar and conversion checks, resource handling and
complete failure outputs. A reviewer must assess native-versus-adapter
behavior and the prospectively specified study before any fitted performance
claim is made. The present decision grants no integration capability.
Reconsider it when a compatible pinned environment and matched native oracle
exist, or when a changed scientific question, dependency revision or target
platform changes the appropriate baseline. Unavailable native execution is
local to this candidate and does not prevent work on other proposal branches.
\cite{exec13_ci_reuse}
\label{exec13:causalimpact-reuse-end}

~~~

~~~text
local-level prior scale 0.01, no seasonal component and static regression.
The static regression construction also supplies an expected model size of
three, expected \(R^2=0.8\), and prior degrees of freedom 50. These are
modeling choices to expose and examine, rather than bank-independent
constants that need no justification. The source passes \texttt{seed=1} to
its \texttt{bsts} calls, while predictive observation-noise simulation uses R
random generation. Reproducibility therefore needs the full runtime and
random-state contract; preserving only a displayed seed is insufficient.
The number of iterations is not a convergence certificate.
\citep{exec13_ci_semantics}

The distinction between latent-state means and predictive draws matters.
The implementation averages state contributions for point predictions, but
uses trajectories including simulated observation noise for predictive
quantiles. For draw \(b\), the reported effect transformation is
\[
 \Delta_t^{(b)}=Y_t-\widetilde Y_t^{(b)},\qquad
 C_h^{(b)}=\sum_{t=T_0+1}^{T_0+h}\Delta_t^{(b)}.
\]
Quantiles of \(C_h^{(b)}\) retain dependence between dates. They are not
obtained by adding the marginal lower and upper bounds. The source also
forces cumulative impact to zero before the post-period begins; that
convention must not be misreported as an empirical pre-treatment causal
test. \citep{exec13_ci_semantics,exec13_ci_brodersen2015}

For example, consider four artificial two-day predictive draws:
\[
(80,120),\quad(120,80),\quad(80,120),\quad(120,80).
\]
Each marginal central 95 percent
empirical interval is \([80,120]\), whereas every path sums to 200. The
interval of sums is \([200,200]\); adding marginal endpoints gives
\([160,240]\). If the observed two-day total is 220, the corresponding
draw-wise cumulative effect is always 20. This finite arithmetic checks the
aggregation rule only. It is neither an MCMC experiment nor a coverage result.
\citep{exec13_cimpact_examples}

Relative effects require another distinction. The inspected summary computes
draw-wise relative totals
\[
 R^{(b)}=\frac{\sum_tY_t}{\sum_t\widetilde Y_t^{(b)}}-1
\]
and then summarizes those ratios. A ratio of posterior means can differ
sharply. With actual total 10 and three artificial counterfactual totals
\(-1,1,2\), the ratios are \(-11,9,4\), whose mean is \(2/3\); dividing
10 by the mean counterfactual total and subtracting one gives 14. Zero
denominators are undefined, and small or negative denominators can make a
percentage difficult to interpret. The proposed report retains absolute
effects and explicit denominator diagnostics rather than clipping such
values into a plausible-looking percentage. \citep{exec13_ci_semantics,exec13_cimpact_examples}

The field called \texttt{p} is a one-sided simulation tail-area calculation.
The inspected implementation appends the observed total to the predictive
totals and takes the smaller inclusive tail proportion. For \(B\) predictive
draws its smallest positive value is \(1/(B+1)\); the four-draw construction
above gives \(1/5\), not zero. Its meaning depends on the specified predictive
model and simulation. It is not a design-based randomization probability, a
proof that the causal assumptions hold, or a posterior probability that a
point-null model is true. An interface should preserve the source field and
explain this definition instead of converting every uncertainty measure into
the same generic significance label. \citep{exec13_ci_semantics,exec13_cimpact_examples}

Finally, units determine useful aggregation. Brodersen and colleagues
distinguish a cumulative flow effect from an average stock effect. Daily
additional applications can be summed over the campaign. An extra USD 10,000
of deposit balance observed on each of three dates is USD 10,000 at the last
date; summing those snapshots produces USD 30,000-days under a daily exposure
convention, not USD 30,000 of new deposits or revenue. Funding value would
need a specified balance-time and margin calculation. The adapter request
therefore includes whether the response is a flow, a stock, a rate or an
index, its units and its aggregation rule.
\citep{exec13_ci_brodersen2015,exec13_cimpact_examples}

\subsection{Calendars, failures and a proposed adapter boundary}

The source saves the original time labels and then replaces the internal
time index with sequential row numbers. Its own comment notes that this
precludes automatic filling of missing time points. The missing-date tests
check that omitted labels remain omitted from the output. A Friday and the
following Monday may therefore be neighboring model steps in a sparse input.
For a bank, business-day dynamics and calendar-day accrual are different
choices. Before fitting, the proposed adapter validates a declared calendar,
row uniqueness, time-zone treatment and period boundaries. Filling omitted
rows, carrying forward balances or aggregating transactions would require
an explicit transformation; each can change the estimand or state evolution.
\citep{exec13_ci_semantics}

Constant, entirely missing or insufficiently observed response series cause
the selected model builder to warn and return no fitted model. The wrapper
can still return an object with the CausalImpact class and unavailable
inferences. Checking only the object's class would misclassify that failure
as success. The proposed output boundary instead requires a fitted model,
usable draws and the requested summaries, while retaining warnings and
missing-value reasons. Missing controls, inadmissible periods, failed
inference, unavailable relative summaries and an unsupported scientific
request need distinguishable responses. A treatment-affected donor is a
scientific restriction to assess; it is not equivalent to a numerical error.
\citep{exec13_ci_semantics,exec13_ci_reuse}

For a future R worker, the request should bind the response definition,
units, calendar, pre/post periods, donor identities, supplied causal
restrictions, data provenance, missingness policy, model specification,
priors, iteration and random-state settings, and a resource limit. The
response should retain source/runtime identities, effective periods,
warnings, posterior draw identity or a verifiable draw artifact, point and
cumulative summaries with their uncertainty type, denominator diagnostics
and execution status. Neither the generated prose report nor a generic
``success'' field substitutes for these quantities. Inputs and outputs would
cross a versioned file or process boundary; the existing CLI and MCP service
does not acquire this behavior merely because the contract is specified.
\citep{exec13_ci_reuse}

The first behavioral comparison should run the same frozen fixture directly
in R and through the adapter in the same environment. It should compare
effective periods, row labels, warnings, draw dimensions and numerical
summaries, not only a plotted effect. Separate fixtures should cover a missing
date, missing controls, a constant response, a one-day post-period, a gap
between periods, out-of-range endpoints and a supplied model with incorrectly
retained post-treatment outcomes. The exact constructions above provide
independent checks of aggregation and reporting; they do not replace a
matched native run. A subsequent prospectively designed simulation can
assess recovery under an explicit observation law and retain contaminated
donors and structural breaks as failures. None of these future fitted
comparisons is claimed as completed. \citep{exec13_ci_reuse}

\subsection{Language paths, licenses and the reuse decision}

The R package declares Apache 2.0. Its license requires preserving the
applicable license and attribution notices, marking modifications and
carrying forward any applicable NOTICE material when distributing derivative
work. Its dependency stack is not thereby assigned the same license. The
inspected cutoff CRAN mirrors contain \texttt{bsts} 0.9.11,
\texttt{Boom} 0.9.17 and \texttt{BoomSpikeSlab} 1.2.7. Their descriptions
declare compilation requirements, R constraints and, for the latter two,
LGPL 2.1 alternatives tied to their license files. The \texttt{bsts}
declaration is \texttt{LGPL-2.1 | MIT + file LICENSE}; its companion file
supplies the copyright holder and year. The inspected Boom description also
identifies incorporated third-party code. Any distributed build consequently
needs its own dependency and notice inventory, including the applicable
LGPL source or relinking route. These declarations are source evidence, not
a solved dependency lock or blanket redistribution clearance.
\citep{exec13_ci_r,exec13_ci_dependencies}

The selected R dependency requirement is material: the observed workspace
has R 4.1.2 and none of the seven queried CausalImpact/direct or modeling
packages installed. Replacing the pin with an older release would change
the reviewed candidate. A separate R 4.5-or-later environment with pinned
compatible compiled dependencies is the concrete future route. This
environment cost, data/calendar preparation, donor justification, Monte
Carlo diagnostics, adapter fixtures and maintenance belong in the prototype
budget. No measured runtime or monetary integration estimate is available
from this source review. \citep{exec13_ci_reuse,exec13_ci_dependencies}

For Python, the R README recommends \texttt{google/tfp-causalimpact}.
At the separately selected 5 October 2026 commit beginning
\texttt{2bd0a64f7836}, its exported version is 0.2.0, its distribution name
is \texttt{tfp-causalimpact}, and its import namespace is
\texttt{causalimpact}. It exports \texttt{fit\_causalimpact} and distinct
data, model and inference option types. The project declares Python 3.9 or
later, TensorFlow and TensorFlow Probability among its dependencies, and
Apache 2.0 licensing. The declarations do not freeze compatible backend
versions. Its README describes a lineage involving the R package and Dafiti's
Python implementation and explicitly says it is not an officially supported
Google product. The R README aims for close results while allowing
randomized differences. No interchangeability, backend compatibility or
numerical equivalence has been demonstrated here.
\citep{exec13_ci_python,exec13_ci_r}

The preferred proposal path is therefore to reference the R implementation
and evaluate a narrow native wrapper before considering a port. Directly
adopting its estimation internals into the current library would import a
runtime and output contract that has not been exercised. Porting the R
wrapper and its sampler stack into Python or C++ would add responsibility
for numerical behavior, priors, random generation, failure handling and
dependency terms. The existing TFP implementation is a separate candidate
that should be compared before undertaking such a port. Its backend source,
license closure and native comparison remain deferred. Shared import names
are insufficient package identity. \citep{exec13_ci_reuse,exec13_ci_python}

Alternatives should be selected by the question. A simple seasonal forecast
is a transparent predictive baseline; a donor-weight procedure emphasizes
an explicitly restricted comparison group; a panel design is preferable
when its assignment and population assumptions match the decision; and a
randomized rollout can address causal ambiguity that predictive refinement
leaves intact. The earlier survey describes the respective packages. A
comparison should align the branch group, outcome units, horizon, available
information and uncertainty meaning before ranking losses. A package that
answers a different estimand is not a losing implementation of the same
request.

The research owner must justify the donor and stability restrictions and
declare the target. The adapter owner must supply the compatible environment,
dependency notices, calendar and conversion checks, resource handling and
complete failure outputs. A reviewer must assess native-versus-adapter
behavior and the prospectively specified study before any fitted performance
claim is made. The present decision grants no integration capability.
Reconsider it when a compatible pinned environment and matched native oracle
exist, or when a changed scientific question, dependency revision or target
platform changes the appropriate baseline. Unavailable native execution is
local to this candidate and does not prevent work on other proposal branches.
\citep{exec13_ci_reuse}
\label{exec13:causalimpact-reuse-end}
~~~

## Edit 160
docs/release/2026-10-29/source/docs/writing-repair/2026-10-29/src/d0fab65084afebc1.tex

Use parenthetical author-year syntax for sentence-final citations after complete contextual reading.

Substantive destination: Every original word, equation, label, citation key, assumption, adverse finding, alternative and qualification remains in the same source.

Before:
~~~text
\section{Commercial missing-data workflows and transfer between bank populations}
\label{exec21:transport-scope}

A bank may obtain a credible intervention estimate in a pilot and still make a
poor decision when it extends the intervention to another branch. Customers may
differ, follow-up outcomes may be missing, and the software used to complete
the records may report uncertainty for a different quantity from the proposed
decision. A useful commercial comparison therefore follows the complete
question: which population receives which action, when is the outcome measured,
why is it unobserved, and which assumptions connect the observed records to the
decision? Imputation, uncertainty combination and population transfer are
separate steps. A feature called ``missing values'' establishes none of those
connections by its name alone.

The selected primary documentation provides concrete comparators. Stata's
\texttt{mi} family manages multiple completed datasets and their analysis;
SAS/STAT's MI and MIANALYZE procedures separate generation and combination;
IBM SPSS Missing Values exposes imputation and output-specific pooling.
The inspected IBM edition is 31.0.2. The SAS HTML identifies version 14.1,
whereas the Stata manuals contain release-19 example-data paths and July 2026
PDF metadata. These identities delimit the comparison; later retrieval does
not authenticate every byte as the edition available at the literature cutoff.
Exact licensed builds, current entitlements and broader commercial coverage
remain unresolved. EViews, Mplus and commercial graphical tools remain leads
requiring matched technical originals for this particular target.
\cite{exec21_transport_stata_mi,exec21_transport_sas_mi_intro,exec21_transport_spss_missing}

\subsection{Missingness assumptions precede record completion}
\label{exec21:transport-missingness}

Let $A$ be a binary action, $X$ covariates recorded before that action, $Y(a)$
the monetary outcome under action $a$, and $R$ an indicator that the outcome is
observed. The record contains $(X,A,R,RY)$, with a separate missingness mask so
that an absent outcome is never mistaken for a zero. Consistency connects an
observed outcome to $Y(A)$; treatment exchangeability and positive treatment
probability are additional causal assumptions. For the particular problem in
which only $Y$ is missing, a conditional mean restriction sufficient for response
adjustment is
\begin{equation}
 E[Y\mid X,A,R=1]=E[Y\mid X,A],\qquad
 P(R=1\mid X,A)>0.
 \label{exec21:transport-mar}
\end{equation}
The stronger familiar MAR restriction makes response independent of the missing
outcome conditional on the observed variables. When covariates are also missing,
the pattern-specific observed information and imputation model must be specified
again; equation~\eqref{exec21:transport-mar} is not a general multivariate recipe.
Administrative censoring, a variable undefined for a customer, and an outcome
lost through nonresponse need distinct observation models.

Stata's substantive manual and the SAS assumptions chapter distinguish MAR
from MCAR and describe ignorability using MAR together with distinct parameters
for the data and missingness models. They also explain why an assumption about
unobserved values cannot be verified by inspecting only the observed values.
The SAS profile adds distributional restrictions for particular regression and
MCMC procedures. These restrictions must accompany a procedure name. A good
fit to observed records, a plausible-looking imputed distribution or rejection
of an MCAR diagnostic does not establish MAR.
\cite{exec21_transport_stata_substantive,exec21_transport_sas_assumptions}

An exact example makes the investment consequence visible. Let a latent response
type $H$ be equally likely to be zero or one, randomize $A$ independently of
$H$, and set $R=H$. All customers have $Y(0)=0$. Responders have $Y(1)=10$.
In one complete-data law, nonresponders have $Y(1)=-10$; in a second they have
$Y(1)=10$. The observable joint distribution of $(A,R,RY)$ is identical in
both laws: each action-response cell has probability $1/4$, and observed treated
outcomes equal 10. Yet the complete-population effects are respectively 0 and
10. At an assumed action cost of USD 4, the margins are USD $-4$ and USD 6.
More imputation draws from one selected model cannot distinguish these worlds.
The project reference enumerates the complete laws and verifies equality of
their observed distributions. It does not assert that either law describes
actual bank records. \cite{exec21_transport_reference}

A transparent sensitivity parameter writes the unobserved treated mean as
$10+\delta$, keeping the control mean zero. The resulting target is
\begin{equation}
 \tau(\delta)=10+\tfrac12\delta,\qquad
 V(\delta)=6+\tfrac12\delta.
 \label{exec21:transport-sensitivity}
\end{equation}
For the illustrative restriction $\delta\in[-20,0]$, value ranges from $-4$
to 6 and changes sign at $\delta=-12$. The interval is supplied as a sensitivity
restriction, not learned from the missing outcomes. The SAS sensitivity chapter
documents a pattern-mixture approach in MI and explicitly says that the
selection-model approach is not supplied by that procedure profile. A result
that changes under a plausible MNAR scenario establishes dependence of the
decision on assumptions; it does not establish which scenario is true. Exact
syntax and later-release capabilities need their own evidence.
\cite{exec21_transport_sas_sensitivity,exec21_transport_reference}

\subsection{What the three documented workflows preserve}
\label{exec21:transport-workflows}

Stata offers four MI storage styles while retaining the original data as
$m=0$ and the completed datasets as $m=1,\ldots,M$. Variables can be regular,
imputed or passive, with passive variables representing algebraic functions
of other variables. The introduction also states that extended missing values
\texttt{.a} through \texttt{.z} persist as hard missing in the documented
profile. An interchange layer that maps every absent value to an ordinary
numeric null could therefore change what the engine is asked to impute.
Maintaining the missingness reason and role is part of the statistical contract.
\cite{exec21_transport_stata_mi}

For chained equations, Stata documents a default burn-in of ten iterations,
ordering from the most to the least observed variable, configurable conditional
models and saved trace summaries. Perfect prediction produces an error unless
an explicit augmentation option is requested. The manual discusses
incompatible conditionals and dependence of results on specification, order
and burn-in. These are useful controls, but a chosen default is not a proof of
convergence or of adequacy for a causal estimand. For example, an imputer that
omits a treatment interaction needed by the analyst can erase the heterogeneity
needed for the branch-transfer decision even if marginal summaries look
reasonable. \cite{exec21_transport_stata_chained,exec21_transport_stata_substantive}

There is a stronger reason to retain the conditional specifications themselves.
Consider binary $U,V$ with proposed conditionals
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 P(U=1\mid V=0)&=P(U=1\mid V=1)=\tfrac12,\\
 P(V=1\mid U=0)&=\tfrac14,\qquad P(V=1\mid U=1)=\tfrac34.
 \end{aligned}
 \label{exec21:transport-conditionals}
\end{equation}
For any positive binary joint distribution, the odds ratio calculated from
either pair of conditionals must agree. Here the first specification requires
odds ratio 1 and the second requires 9, so no such joint distribution exists.
Nevertheless, alternating draws can have stable distributions. In the cell
order $(0,0),(0,1),(1,0),(1,1)$, a scan updating $U$ then $V$ has stationary
law $(3,1,1,3)/8$; reversing the scan has stationary law $(1,1,1,1)/4$.
Direct multiplication verifies each invariant distribution. Thus an apparently
stable trace does not demonstrate compatibility of the supplied conditionals.
This exact constructed example is a diagnostic design, not an observed failure
of a particular vendor's implementation. \cite{exec21_transport_reference}

SAS supplies a related but separately versioned workflow. Its MI overview
distinguishes sequential methods for monotone patterns from multivariate-normal
MCMC and fully conditional specification for arbitrary patterns. FCS uses
separate conditional models rather than beginning with an explicit common
multivariate model. The completed datasets are analyzed using the relevant
complete-data procedures, and MIANALYZE reads parameter estimates together with
standard errors or covariance matrices. It notes an additional assumption for
multivariate Wald inference and special treatment for quantities such as
correlations and ratios. The proposal therefore needs a typed collection of
estimates and covariance matrices, not just a filled table. The recovered HTML
contains equations as images that are outside this text inspection; the numerical
pooling formulas below are grounded in the readable Stata manual.
\cite{exec21_transport_sas_mi_intro,exec21_transport_sas_mianalyze_intro,exec21_transport_sas_combining}

IBM's manual makes another important distinction. Multiple Imputation accepts
analysis weights in summaries and imputation models, but it does not explicitly
handle strata, clusters or other complex sampling structures. The manual also
says that Complex Samples procedures do not automatically analyze MI datasets
in this profile. Frequency weights and analysis weights have different
semantics; noninteger frequency weights are rounded. Passing a final sampling
weight therefore does not establish a cluster-aware covariance calculation.
Stata documents a survey-prefix analysis route, but that alone also leaves
the imputation model's treatment of the sampling design to be justified.
\cite{exec21_transport_spss_missing,exec21_transport_stata_estimate}

SPSS chooses a monotone method when the pattern permits it and otherwise uses
FCS. Automatic FCS uses ten iterations; the manual provides history output
for convergence assessment. Main effects are the automatic specification,
with optional categorical two-way interactions under selected settings.
Scale variables can use linear regression or predictive mean matching, whose
documented default donor count is five; categorical models use logistic
regression. Constraints can cause redraws, and excluding highly incomplete
variables removes them from both imputation and prediction. Reproducing a run
requires the same seed, case order, variable order and settings. These details
affect the implied procedure and belong in an adapter request.
\cite{exec21_transport_spss_missing}

The output variable \texttt{Imputation\_} distinguishes original data from
the $M$ completed versions, and split-file settings control how those versions
are analyzed. IBM distinguishes naive combination, which supplies a pooled
parameter, from univariate combination, which can also supply standard errors,
effective degrees of freedom, intervals and missing-information diagnostics.
Support is specific to each output; an unsupported procedure returns no pooled
output. An integration must preserve that unavailability rather than attach a
generic confidence interval to every pooled number.
\cite{exec21_transport_spss_missing}

\subsection{Combine uncertainty on the declared estimand scale}
\label{exec21:transport-pooling}

For a fixed scalar estimand $Q$, let $\widehat Q_m$ and $U_m$ be the estimate
and its completed-data variance from imputation $m$. The documented combination
rule is
\begin{align}
 \overline Q&=M^{-1}\sum_{m=1}^M\widehat Q_m,&
 \overline U&=M^{-1}\sum_{m=1}^M U_m,\nonumber\\
 B&=(M-1)^{-1}\sum_{m=1}^M(\widehat Q_m-\overline Q)^2,&
 T&=\overline U+(1+M^{-1})B.
 \label{exec21:transport-poolformula}
\end{align}
The between-imputation term reflects missing-data uncertainty under the
specified imputation model, and $B/M$ reflects using finitely many imputations.
Neither accounts automatically for uncertainty about an unverified MAR or
population-invariance assumption. Valid completed-data inference and suitable
imputation remain premises. For vector parameters the covariance matrices,
including their off-diagonal terms and common coefficient identities, must be
preserved. \cite{exec21_transport_stata_estimate,exec21_transport_stata_substantive}

For the authored values $(\widehat Q_1,\widehat Q_2,\widehat Q_3)=(0,2,4)$
and $U_m=1$, equation~\eqref{exec21:transport-poolformula} gives
$\overline Q=2$, $B=4$ and $T=19/3$. Retaining only the within variance gives
1. Incorrectly treating the three completed-data estimates as independent
replicate measurements would give $\overline U/M=1/3$. Neither is the MI
variance. The documented large-complete-sample degrees-of-freedom approximation
uses $r=(1+M^{-1})B/\overline U$ and
$\nu=(M-1)(1+1/r)^2$, giving $361/128$ here. A small complete-data sample
requires the further adjustment documented by the manual; the arithmetic is
not a universal coverage result. \cite{exec21_transport_stata_estimate,exec21_transport_reference}

The estimand scale also matters. If two log-odds estimates correspond to odds
ratios 1 and 9, exponentiating their average coefficient gives 3, whereas
averaging the odds ratios gives 5. Stata documents coefficient-scale pooling
followed by an optional exponentiated display; it also has explicit requests
for transformed coefficients. A declared transformed estimand needs its own
completed-data uncertainty and suitable pooling justification. Neither 3 nor
5 is a bank's average profit or a marginal treatment effect merely because it
was returned after imputation. \cite{exec21_transport_stata_estimate,exec21_transport_reference}

Failure handling is part of the same argument. Stata normally stops when an
imputation's estimation fails or the estimation sample changes. Its
\texttt{errorok} and \texttt{esampvaryok} options permit different behavior.
An automatic wrapper must expose such choices and retain the failing
imputation and sample identities. Otherwise it can silently pool different
populations or select only successful fits. The appropriate number of
imputations should be chosen using stability and Monte Carlo error diagnostics
for the requested inference; high relative efficiency of a point estimator
does not guarantee stable intervals or decision thresholds.
\cite{exec21_transport_stata_estimate,exec21_transport_sas_mi_intro}

\subsection{Transport needs an explicit population bridge}
\label{exec21:transport-target}

Let $S=1$ denote the source pilot and $S=0$ the target branch, and define
$p_s(x)=P(X=x\mid S=s)$ and
$\tau(x)=E[Y(1)-Y(0)\mid X=x,S=1]$. One sufficient route assumes consistency,
source treatment exchangeability, positive source treatment and response
probabilities, valid response adjustment within source covariates, and equality
of conditional potential-outcome means across $S$. It also requires source
support wherever $p_0(x)>0$. Under these restrictions, iterated expectation gives
\begin{equation}
 \tau_0=\sum_x p_0(x)\tau(x)
       =E\left[\left.\frac{p_0(X)}{p_1(X)}\tau(X)\right|S=1\right].
 \label{exec21:transport-standardize}
\end{equation}
This is a project derivation for a declared finite-covariate regime. Equality of
conditional means is extra scientific information; it is not established by
the existence of a weight column or by the imputation procedure.
\cite{exec21_transport_reference}

For clarity, write $e_a(x)=P(A=a\mid X=x,S=1)$ and
$r_a(x)=P(R=1\mid A=a,X=x,S=1)$. Under the same response restriction and
positivity, each source conditional mean can be written
\begin{equation}
 \mu_a(x)=E\left[\left.
       \frac{\mathbb{1}\{A=a\}RY}{e_a(x)r_a(x)}
       \right|X=x,S=1\right].
 \label{exec21:transport-response}
\end{equation}
Condition first on $A=a$ and $R=1$, cancel the treatment and response
probabilities, then apply the mean restriction and source causal assumptions.
Substitution into equation~\eqref{exec21:transport-standardize} gives a
combined population, treatment and response-weight expression. It is not a
variance estimator: estimated nuisance models, sampling the target covariates,
clustering and any use of MI require a jointly justified inference procedure.
Weights for these different purposes must remain separately named.

Suppose the source proportions of two customer strata are $(4/5,1/5)$ and the
target proportions are $(1/5,4/5)$. Conditional intervention effects are USD
10 and USD $-10$. The pilot's average effect is 6, the target's is $-6$, and
the population weights are $1/4$ and 4. At an assumed cost of USD 2, the pilot
margin is USD 4 and the target margin is USD $-8$. The exact reference also
enumerates randomized treatment and four positive response rates, verifying
that the combined weighting expression recovers the stipulated target.
No software error is needed for the naive deployment decision to reverse:
the wrong population suffices. \cite{exec21_transport_reference}

If the source instead contains only the first stratum, the second effect is
unobserved and no finite reweighting creates its information. With the explicit
restriction that the second effect lies in $[-10,10]$, the target effect is
$2+(4/5)\tau(1)\in[-6,10]$, giving margin $[-8,8]$ at cost 2. Without such
a justified bound, even that interval cannot be asserted. An adapter should
report the support defect and any defensible identified set. Silently trimming
the second stratum changes the target population; silently predicting into it
adds extrapolation assumptions. \cite{exec21_transport_reference}

\subsection{Competitive position and a public qualification route}
\label{exec21:transport-integration}

The documented commercial systems already provide substantive imputation,
analysis and diagnostic workflows. The proposal has not demonstrated a native
replacement or superior estimation performance. Its proposed contribution is
to preserve the causal question and its restrictions across those workflows:
source and target populations, observation clocks, missingness reasons,
imputation identity, covariance semantics and explicit refusal. Licensed
isolated execution is a candidate integration route; public manuals do not
grant redistribution rights for the engines. EViews, Mplus and graphical-tool
profiles cannot be ranked without the missing originals, and prices remain
unknown. \cite{exec21_transport_stata_estimate,exec21_transport_sas_mianalyze_intro,exec21_transport_spss_missing}

A useful first prototype would compare a selected licensed procedure and an
already reviewed open implementation on the same generated population,
missingness law and estimand. Complete-record analysis, complete-case analysis,
single imputation, proper multiple imputation and an explicitly specified
response-weight procedure supply substantive alternatives. Fix the causal
target, sample law, interaction structure, number of imputations, diagnostic
and refusal rules before evaluation. Include MAR and MNAR cases, complete and
missing source support, compatible and incompatible imputation models, cluster
sampling, changing coefficient sets and unsuccessful fits. Report bias,
coverage under the claimed regime, interval width, unavailable requests,
simulation error and full workflow cost. A sensitivity grid evaluates
robustness; it is not a device for choosing whichever assumption makes a
preferred intervention profitable.

The library, CLI and MCP interfaces should carry the same request and result
meaning. Original and imputed data need stable unit and imputation identifiers,
measurement types, category references, passive transformations and sampling
design. Results need per-imputation estimates and covariance, pooled scale and
degrees of freedom, convergence diagnostics, population support and retained
failures. An assistant can help locate technical passages, propose models and
explain sensitivity; it cannot infer the unobserved outcomes or authorize a
scientific assumption from fluent text. These public comparisons can establish
whether the proposed integration merits a more expensive bank experiment.


\subsection{What must be preserved from the audit to the decision}

The chapter's alternatives answer different information problems. Joint audits
can distinguish relationship laws that separate-edge labels cannot. Coordinated
actions can sometimes avoid an unresolved graph moment. Population adjustment
uses justified invariance and support; imputation uses an observation and
analysis model; historical retrieval uses identity and availability rules.
Neither a completed table nor a precise conditional calculation establishes
all of those premises.

A defensible recommendation therefore retains the missing information and its
monetary consequence. It may call for an additional joint audit, a different
allocation, a restricted target population, sensitivity analysis or refusal.
The software choices matter because their score, covariance, mask, clock and
failure behavior can preserve or change that recommendation. The next chapter
examines hidden-state and Bayesian computations as further conditional tools,
with their assumptions carried into the comparison.
~~~
