~~~text
\section{Commercial missing-data workflows and transfer between bank populations}
\label{exec21:transport-scope}

A bank may obtain a credible intervention estimate in a pilot and still make a
poor decision when it extends the intervention to another branch. Customers may
differ, follow-up outcomes may be missing, and the software used to complete
the records may report uncertainty for a different quantity from the proposed
decision. A useful commercial comparison therefore follows the complete
question: which population receives which action, when is the outcome measured,
why is it unobserved, and which assumptions connect the observed records to the
decision? Imputation, uncertainty combination and population transfer are
separate steps. A feature called ``missing values'' establishes none of those
connections by its name alone.

The selected primary documentation provides concrete comparators. Stata's
\texttt{mi} family manages multiple completed datasets and their analysis;
SAS/STAT's MI and MIANALYZE procedures separate generation and combination;
IBM SPSS Missing Values exposes imputation and output-specific pooling.
The inspected IBM edition is 31.0.2. The SAS HTML identifies version 14.1,
whereas the Stata manuals contain release-19 example-data paths and July 2026
PDF metadata. These identities delimit the comparison; later retrieval does
not authenticate every byte as the edition available at the literature cutoff.
Exact licensed builds, current entitlements and broader commercial coverage
remain unresolved. EViews, Mplus and commercial graphical tools remain leads
requiring matched technical originals for this particular target.
\citep{exec21_transport_stata_mi,exec21_transport_sas_mi_intro,exec21_transport_spss_missing}

\subsection{Missingness assumptions precede record completion}
\label{exec21:transport-missingness}

Let $A$ be a binary action, $X$ covariates recorded before that action, $Y(a)$
the monetary outcome under action $a$, and $R$ an indicator that the outcome is
observed. The record contains $(X,A,R,RY)$, with a separate missingness mask so
that an absent outcome is never mistaken for a zero. Consistency connects an
observed outcome to $Y(A)$; treatment exchangeability and positive treatment
probability are additional causal assumptions. For the particular problem in
which only $Y$ is missing, a conditional mean restriction sufficient for response
adjustment is
\begin{equation}
 E[Y\mid X,A,R=1]=E[Y\mid X,A],\qquad
 P(R=1\mid X,A)>0.
 \label{exec21:transport-mar}
\end{equation}
The stronger familiar MAR restriction makes response independent of the missing
outcome conditional on the observed variables. When covariates are also missing,
the pattern-specific observed information and imputation model must be specified
again; equation~\eqref{exec21:transport-mar} is not a general multivariate recipe.
Administrative censoring, a variable undefined for a customer, and an outcome
lost through nonresponse need distinct observation models.

Stata's substantive manual and the SAS assumptions chapter distinguish MAR
from MCAR and describe ignorability using MAR together with distinct parameters
for the data and missingness models. They also explain why an assumption about
unobserved values cannot be verified by inspecting only the observed values.
The SAS profile adds distributional restrictions for particular regression and
MCMC procedures. These restrictions must accompany a procedure name. A good
fit to observed records, a plausible-looking imputed distribution or rejection
of an MCAR diagnostic does not establish MAR.
\citep{exec21_transport_stata_substantive,exec21_transport_sas_assumptions}

An exact example makes the investment consequence visible. Let a latent response
type $H$ be equally likely to be zero or one, randomize $A$ independently of
$H$, and set $R=H$. All customers have $Y(0)=0$. Responders have $Y(1)=10$.
In one complete-data law, nonresponders have $Y(1)=-10$; in a second they have
$Y(1)=10$. The observable joint distribution of $(A,R,RY)$ is identical in
both laws: each action-response cell has probability $1/4$, and observed treated
outcomes equal 10. Yet the complete-population effects are respectively 0 and
10. At an assumed action cost of USD 4, the margins are USD $-4$ and USD 6.
More imputation draws from one selected model cannot distinguish these worlds.
The project reference enumerates the complete laws and verifies equality of
their observed distributions. It does not assert that either law describes
actual bank records. \citep{exec21_transport_reference}

A transparent sensitivity parameter writes the unobserved treated mean as
$10+\delta$, keeping the control mean zero. The resulting target is
\begin{equation}
 \tau(\delta)=10+\tfrac12\delta,\qquad
 V(\delta)=6+\tfrac12\delta.
 \label{exec21:transport-sensitivity}
\end{equation}
For the illustrative restriction $\delta\in[-20,0]$, value ranges from $-4$
to 6 and changes sign at $\delta=-12$. The interval is supplied as a sensitivity
restriction, not learned from the missing outcomes. The SAS sensitivity chapter
documents a pattern-mixture approach in MI and explicitly says that the
selection-model approach is not supplied by that procedure profile. A result
that changes under a plausible MNAR scenario establishes dependence of the
decision on assumptions; it does not establish which scenario is true. Exact
syntax and later-release capabilities need their own evidence.
\citep{exec21_transport_sas_sensitivity,exec21_transport_reference}

\subsection{What the three documented workflows preserve}
\label{exec21:transport-workflows}

Stata offers four MI storage styles while retaining the original data as
$m=0$ and the completed datasets as $m=1,\ldots,M$. Variables can be regular,
imputed or passive, with passive variables representing algebraic functions
of other variables. The introduction also states that extended missing values
\texttt{.a} through \texttt{.z} persist as hard missing in the documented
profile. An interchange layer that maps every absent value to an ordinary
numeric null could therefore change what the engine is asked to impute.
Maintaining the missingness reason and role is part of the statistical contract.
\citep{exec21_transport_stata_mi}

For chained equations, Stata documents a default burn-in of ten iterations,
ordering from the most to the least observed variable, configurable conditional
models and saved trace summaries. Perfect prediction produces an error unless
an explicit augmentation option is requested. The manual discusses
incompatible conditionals and dependence of results on specification, order
and burn-in. These are useful controls, but a chosen default is not a proof of
convergence or of adequacy for a causal estimand. For example, an imputer that
omits a treatment interaction needed by the analyst can erase the heterogeneity
needed for the branch-transfer decision even if marginal summaries look
reasonable. \citep{exec21_transport_stata_chained,exec21_transport_stata_substantive}

There is a stronger reason to retain the conditional specifications themselves.
Consider binary $U,V$ with proposed conditionals
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 P(U=1\mid V=0)&=P(U=1\mid V=1)=\tfrac12,\\
 P(V=1\mid U=0)&=\tfrac14,\qquad P(V=1\mid U=1)=\tfrac34.
 \end{aligned}
 \label{exec21:transport-conditionals}
\end{equation}
For any positive binary joint distribution, the odds ratio calculated from
either pair of conditionals must agree. Here the first specification requires
odds ratio 1 and the second requires 9, so no such joint distribution exists.
Nevertheless, alternating draws can have stable distributions. In the cell
order $(0,0),(0,1),(1,0),(1,1)$, a scan updating $U$ then $V$ has stationary
law $(3,1,1,3)/8$; reversing the scan has stationary law $(1,1,1,1)/4$.
Direct multiplication verifies each invariant distribution. Thus an apparently
stable trace does not demonstrate compatibility of the supplied conditionals.
This exact constructed example is a diagnostic design, not an observed failure
of a particular vendor's implementation. \citep{exec21_transport_reference}

SAS supplies a related but separately versioned workflow. Its MI overview
distinguishes sequential methods for monotone patterns from multivariate-normal
MCMC and fully conditional specification for arbitrary patterns. FCS uses
separate conditional models rather than beginning with an explicit common
multivariate model. The completed datasets are analyzed using the relevant
complete-data procedures, and MIANALYZE reads parameter estimates together with
standard errors or covariance matrices. It notes an additional assumption for
multivariate Wald inference and special treatment for quantities such as
correlations and ratios. The proposal therefore needs a typed collection of
estimates and covariance matrices, not just a filled table. The recovered HTML
contains equations as images that are outside this text inspection; the numerical
pooling formulas below are grounded in the readable Stata manual.
\citep{exec21_transport_sas_mi_intro,exec21_transport_sas_mianalyze_intro,exec21_transport_sas_combining}

IBM's manual makes another important distinction. Multiple Imputation accepts
analysis weights in summaries and imputation models, but it does not explicitly
handle strata, clusters or other complex sampling structures. The manual also
says that Complex Samples procedures do not automatically analyze MI datasets
in this profile. Frequency weights and analysis weights have different
semantics; noninteger frequency weights are rounded. Passing a final sampling
weight therefore does not establish a cluster-aware covariance calculation.
Stata documents a survey-prefix analysis route, but that alone also leaves
the imputation model's treatment of the sampling design to be justified.
\citep{exec21_transport_spss_missing,exec21_transport_stata_estimate}

SPSS chooses a monotone method when the pattern permits it and otherwise uses
FCS. Automatic FCS uses ten iterations; the manual provides history output
for convergence assessment. Main effects are the automatic specification,
with optional categorical two-way interactions under selected settings.
Scale variables can use linear regression or predictive mean matching, whose
documented default donor count is five; categorical models use logistic
regression. Constraints can cause redraws, and excluding highly incomplete
variables removes them from both imputation and prediction. Reproducing a run
requires the same seed, case order, variable order and settings. These details
affect the implied procedure and belong in an adapter request.
\citep{exec21_transport_spss_missing}

The output variable \texttt{Imputation\_} distinguishes original data from
the $M$ completed versions, and split-file settings control how those versions
are analyzed. IBM distinguishes naive combination, which supplies a pooled
parameter, from univariate combination, which can also supply standard errors,
effective degrees of freedom, intervals and missing-information diagnostics.
Support is specific to each output; an unsupported procedure returns no pooled
output. An integration must preserve that unavailability rather than attach a
generic confidence interval to every pooled number.
\citep{exec21_transport_spss_missing}

\subsection{Combine uncertainty on the declared estimand scale}
\label{exec21:transport-pooling}

For a fixed scalar estimand $Q$, let $\widehat Q_m$ and $U_m$ be the estimate
and its completed-data variance from imputation $m$. The documented combination
rule is
\begin{align}
 \overline Q&=M^{-1}\sum_{m=1}^M\widehat Q_m,&
 \overline U&=M^{-1}\sum_{m=1}^M U_m,\nonumber\\
 B&=(M-1)^{-1}\sum_{m=1}^M(\widehat Q_m-\overline Q)^2,&
 T&=\overline U+(1+M^{-1})B.
 \label{exec21:transport-poolformula}
\end{align}
The between-imputation term reflects missing-data uncertainty under the
specified imputation model, and $B/M$ reflects using finitely many imputations.
Neither accounts automatically for uncertainty about an unverified MAR or
population-invariance assumption. Valid completed-data inference and suitable
imputation remain premises. For vector parameters the covariance matrices,
including their off-diagonal terms and common coefficient identities, must be
preserved. \citep{exec21_transport_stata_estimate,exec21_transport_stata_substantive}

For the authored values $(\widehat Q_1,\widehat Q_2,\widehat Q_3)=(0,2,4)$
and $U_m=1$, equation~\eqref{exec21:transport-poolformula} gives
$\overline Q=2$, $B=4$ and $T=19/3$. Retaining only the within variance gives
1. Incorrectly treating the three completed-data estimates as independent
replicate measurements would give $\overline U/M=1/3$. Neither is the MI
variance. The documented large-complete-sample degrees-of-freedom approximation
uses $r=(1+M^{-1})B/\overline U$ and
$\nu=(M-1)(1+1/r)^2$, giving $361/128$ here. A small complete-data sample
requires the further adjustment documented by the manual; the arithmetic is
not a universal coverage result. \citep{exec21_transport_stata_estimate,exec21_transport_reference}

The estimand scale also matters. If two log-odds estimates correspond to odds
ratios 1 and 9, exponentiating their average coefficient gives 3, whereas
averaging the odds ratios gives 5. Stata documents coefficient-scale pooling
followed by an optional exponentiated display; it also has explicit requests
for transformed coefficients. A declared transformed estimand needs its own
completed-data uncertainty and suitable pooling justification. Neither 3 nor
5 is a bank's average profit or a marginal treatment effect merely because it
was returned after imputation. \citep{exec21_transport_stata_estimate,exec21_transport_reference}

Failure handling is part of the same argument. Stata normally stops when an
imputation's estimation fails or the estimation sample changes. Its
\texttt{errorok} and \texttt{esampvaryok} options permit different behavior.
An automatic wrapper must expose such choices and retain the failing
imputation and sample identities. Otherwise it can silently pool different
populations or select only successful fits. The appropriate number of
imputations should be chosen using stability and Monte Carlo error diagnostics
for the requested inference; high relative efficiency of a point estimator
does not guarantee stable intervals or decision thresholds.
\citep{exec21_transport_stata_estimate,exec21_transport_sas_mi_intro}

\subsection{Transport needs an explicit population bridge}
\label{exec21:transport-target}

Let $S=1$ denote the source pilot and $S=0$ the target branch, and define
$p_s(x)=P(X=x\mid S=s)$ and
$\tau(x)=E[Y(1)-Y(0)\mid X=x,S=1]$. One sufficient route assumes consistency,
source treatment exchangeability, positive source treatment and response
probabilities, valid response adjustment within source covariates, and equality
of conditional potential-outcome means across $S$. It also requires source
support wherever $p_0(x)>0$. Under these restrictions, iterated expectation gives
\begin{equation}
 \tau_0=\sum_x p_0(x)\tau(x)
       =E\left[\left.\frac{p_0(X)}{p_1(X)}\tau(X)\right|S=1\right].
 \label{exec21:transport-standardize}
\end{equation}
This is a project derivation for a declared finite-covariate regime. Equality of
conditional means is extra scientific information; it is not established by
the existence of a weight column or by the imputation procedure.
\citep{exec21_transport_reference}

For clarity, write $e_a(x)=P(A=a\mid X=x,S=1)$ and
$r_a(x)=P(R=1\mid A=a,X=x,S=1)$. Under the same response restriction and
positivity, each source conditional mean can be written
\begin{equation}
 \mu_a(x)=E\left[\left.
       \frac{\mathbb{1}\{A=a\}RY}{e_a(x)r_a(x)}
       \right|X=x,S=1\right].
 \label{exec21:transport-response}
\end{equation}
Condition first on $A=a$ and $R=1$, cancel the treatment and response
probabilities, then apply the mean restriction and source causal assumptions.
Substitution into equation~\eqref{exec21:transport-standardize} gives a
combined population, treatment and response-weight expression. It is not a
variance estimator: estimated nuisance models, sampling the target covariates,
clustering and any use of MI require a jointly justified inference procedure.
Weights for these different purposes must remain separately named.

Suppose the source proportions of two customer strata are $(4/5,1/5)$ and the
target proportions are $(1/5,4/5)$. Conditional intervention effects are USD
10 and USD $-10$. The pilot's average effect is 6, the target's is $-6$, and
the population weights are $1/4$ and 4. At an assumed cost of USD 2, the pilot
margin is USD 4 and the target margin is USD $-8$. The exact reference also
enumerates randomized treatment and four positive response rates, verifying
that the combined weighting expression recovers the stipulated target.
No software error is needed for the naive deployment decision to reverse:
the wrong population suffices. \citep{exec21_transport_reference}

If the source instead contains only the first stratum, the second effect is
unobserved and no finite reweighting creates its information. With the explicit
restriction that the second effect lies in $[-10,10]$, the target effect is
$2+(4/5)\tau(1)\in[-6,10]$, giving margin $[-8,8]$ at cost 2. Without such
a justified bound, even that interval cannot be asserted. An adapter should
report the support defect and any defensible identified set. Silently trimming
the second stratum changes the target population; silently predicting into it
adds extrapolation assumptions. \citep{exec21_transport_reference}

\subsection{Competitive position and a public qualification route}
\label{exec21:transport-integration}

The documented commercial systems already provide substantive imputation,
analysis and diagnostic workflows. The proposal has not demonstrated a native
replacement or superior estimation performance. Its proposed contribution is
to preserve the causal question and its restrictions across those workflows:
source and target populations, observation clocks, missingness reasons,
imputation identity, covariance semantics and explicit refusal. Licensed
isolated execution is a candidate integration route; public manuals do not
grant redistribution rights for the engines. EViews, Mplus and graphical-tool
profiles cannot be ranked without the missing originals, and prices remain
unknown. \citep{exec21_transport_stata_estimate,exec21_transport_sas_mianalyze_intro,exec21_transport_spss_missing}

A useful first prototype would compare a selected licensed procedure and an
already reviewed open implementation on the same generated population,
missingness law and estimand. Complete-record analysis, complete-case analysis,
single imputation, proper multiple imputation and an explicitly specified
response-weight procedure supply substantive alternatives. Fix the causal
target, sample law, interaction structure, number of imputations, diagnostic
and refusal rules before evaluation. Include MAR and MNAR cases, complete and
missing source support, compatible and incompatible imputation models, cluster
sampling, changing coefficient sets and unsuccessful fits. Report bias,
coverage under the claimed regime, interval width, unavailable requests,
simulation error and full workflow cost. A sensitivity grid evaluates
robustness; it is not a device for choosing whichever assumption makes a
preferred intervention profitable.

The library, CLI and MCP interfaces should carry the same request and result
meaning. Original and imputed data need stable unit and imputation identifiers,
measurement types, category references, passive transformations and sampling
design. Results need per-imputation estimates and covariance, pooled scale and
degrees of freedom, convergence diagnostics, population support and retained
failures. An assistant can help locate technical passages, propose models and
explain sensitivity; it cannot infer the unobserved outcomes or authorize a
scientific assumption from fluent text. These public comparisons can establish
whether the proposed integration merits a more expensive bank experiment.


\subsection{What must be preserved from the audit to the decision}

The chapter's alternatives answer different information problems. Joint audits
can distinguish relationship laws that separate-edge labels cannot. Coordinated
actions can sometimes avoid an unresolved graph moment. Population adjustment
uses justified invariance and support; imputation uses an observation and
analysis model; historical retrieval uses identity and availability rules.
Neither a completed table nor a precise conditional calculation establishes
all of those premises.

A defensible recommendation therefore retains the missing information and its
monetary consequence. It may call for an additional joint audit, a different
allocation, a restricted target population, sensitivity analysis or refusal.
The software choices matter because their score, covariance, mask, clock and
failure behavior can preserve or change that recommendation. The next chapter
examines hidden-state and Bayesian computations as further conditional tools,
with their assumptions carried into the comparison.
~~~

~~~text
\chapter{Customer histories, heterogeneous effects and economic units}
\label{v131:histories-forests}

This chapter returns to the customer-level request after the network comparisons.
The earlier longitudinal, forest and dose studies supply the detailed methods;
the question here is what must remain unchanged when their outputs are reused.
Whole histories, within-period event order, the population being averaged and
the units of a treatment density can each change the economic answer. The
examples retain those distinctions without treating a common interface as a
common estimand.

\section{Resampling a sequence requires a sampling model}

Consider a bank evaluating a sequence of servicing decisions over twelve
months. The data contain repeated observations of each borrower, interventions
that depend on previous balances and competing events such as account closure.
The statistical unit might be a borrower history, a household, a branch or an
independent economic calendar. These choices imply different resampling laws.
A routine with ``bootstrap'' in its name does not determine the appropriate law.

LiNGAM's generic bootstrap resamples rows and refits the model. VARLiNGAM has
a distinct path: it fits a lag model, applies a contemporaneous LiNGAM model to
the residuals, resamples residual vectors and reconstructs a sequence using the
fitted autoregressive coefficients. The inspected loop temporarily sets the
lag-selection criterion to \texttt{None}. Consequently, one must inspect the
initial lag/coefficient settings and the loop's refit behavior before claiming
that it propagates model-selection uncertainty. \cite{v131_sw_lingam}

Residual resampling can be a sensible candidate under an appropriate innovation
law. It does not automatically preserve persistent residual dependence,
unmodeled common shocks or a different sampling calendar. Row resampling is a
different candidate suitable for a different observational unit. A banking
comparison should include a correctly specified innovation model and an adverse
case with persistent residuals, preserving the uncertainty target and failures
in both. Bootstrap edge frequencies should not be reported as a calibrated
effect confidence region without an additional argument.

pygformula's inspected bootstrap draws observed subject identifiers with
replacement, copies each subject's complete history and assigns a fresh replicate
identifier to each draw. It updates histories and refits covariate and outcome
models. Expanding to the requested simulation population occurs in a separate
step. These operations distinguish the observed sampling unit from the Monte
Carlo population used to evaluate a fitted regime. \cite{v131_sw_pygformula}

The fresh identifiers matter. Drawing borrower 17 twice should produce two
bootstrap histories, not concatenate them into one impossible person with two
records at every date. An integration test needs duplicate draws, unequal
history lengths and multiple interventions. It must also preserve the original
calendar when that calendar is part of the estimand. Subject resampling alone
does not account for a market shock shared by every subject on the same date.

Failed fits require equally explicit handling. The inspected bootstrap helper
catches an exception, warns and returns a dictionary whose
\texttt{boot\_results} is \texttt{None}. The caller filters such entries when
assembling one result list, but subsequently constructs \texttt{bootests} by
indexing every original entry. A failed entry therefore exposes a static
inconsistency in that caller path. This observation requires reproduction in
the pinned runtime before attributing an observed frequency or impact to it.
The integration must count attempted, successful and failed replications and
preserve the failure reason, irrespective of whether the eventual repair
aborts, returns an explicit unavailable interval or uses a preregistered rule.
\cite{v13_sw_pygformula,v131_sw_pygformula}

\section{The intervention clock and competing-event target}

The inspected simulation path applies an intervention to the current simulated
frame and then refreshes history variables before outcome prediction. Static
schedules index their values by the absolute time index \texttt{int\_values[t]}.
Threshold interventions bound the current action between the supplied limits.
Custom interventions receive the current frame, available pool and time index.
The API permits useful regimes, but does not itself certify that a custom
function uses only information available at the decision time.
\cite{v131_sw_pygformula}

For example, ``contact once delinquency has persisted for two months'' refers
to the observed history before the action, whereas ``contact borrowers who
will be delinquent next month'' uses future information. Both can be expressed
as executable code if future fields have been made available. A valid interface
must enforce the declared information clock and treat prohibited reads as a
contract violation. Thresholds should also retain their monetary units and
whether they change an action, a measurement or an eligibility condition.

Competing events alter the target even when the same outcome model is used.
In a stipulated discrete-time model, let $d_t$ be the probability of a competing
event in period $t$ among those still at risk, and let $y_t$ be the outcome
hazard conditional on avoiding that competing event. If competing events occur
before outcomes within a period, the cumulative incidence through $T$ is
\[
 F_Y(T)=\sum_{t=1}^T
 \left\{\prod_{s<t}(1-d_s)(1-y_s)\right\}(1-d_t)y_t.
\]
This formula follows by partitioning on the period of the first outcome event.
It differs from a hypothetical law that eliminates the competing event.

For two periods with $y=(0.1,0.2)$ and $d=(0.05,0.1)$, the first contribution
is $0.095$ and the second is $0.9(0.95)(0.9)(0.2)=0.1539$. Total incidence is
$0.2489$. Removing competing events while holding these outcome hazards fixed
gives $1-(0.9)(0.8)=0.28$. At a stipulated 10,000 accounts and CNY 100 per event,
the corresponding expected-loss difference is CNY 31,100. This is exact
illustrative arithmetic. It does not identify the effect of eliminating a
real bank's competing event or establish that the outcome hazard would remain
fixed under that intervention. \cite{v131_project_semantics}

The inspected pygformula source separately computes survival risks with and
without a competing-event path, using subject-level cumulative products and
then population means. That code provides a concrete place to check the
within-period ordering, history alignment and target definition. It does not
settle whether observed censoring or competing-event assumptions are valid in
the proposed banking application. \cite{v131_sw_pygformula}

\section{Following an R forest operation into its C++ implementation}

For grf, the causal-forest binding assigns the same variable index to treatment
and instrument, then calls an instrumental predictor. The predictor factory
constructs \texttt{InstrumentalPredictionStrategy}. The native prediction uses
a ratio of centered outcome--instrument and treatment--instrument moments.
When instrument and treatment coincide, this corresponds to a weighted local
covariance divided by a weighted local treatment variance. This traced route
supports the interpretation of continuous-treatment output as a local slope
under its functional assumptions. \cite{v131_sw_grf}

It does not identify an arbitrary finite change in a nonlinear dose-response
curve. A change from an offer rate of one unit to two units needs the appropriate
counterfactual contrast and exposure support. A slope, an average slope and a
finite contrast can coincide in a linear model and diverge otherwise. The bank
must choose the target before comparing software outputs.

The native variance routine forms influence-like quantities across tree
groups, separates between-group variation from group noise and calls a Bayes
debiaser. These groups concern the forest's computation. They are distinct
from the observed borrower clusters used by the R average-effect calculation.
Inspecting both paths avoids the false inference that a tree-group variance
option automatically accounts for a persistent banking calendar. The debiaser,
forest sampling, serialization and numerical safeguards require qualification
before a direct native interface is admitted. Section~\ref{exec13:grf-adapter}
develops the selected native representation, variance-field and persistence
contracts; source inspection does not replace their runtime tests. \cite{v131_sw_grf}

The maintained R interface is therefore a reasonable first integration
candidate, subject to a complete license/dependency and behavioral review.
A direct C++ port would have to preserve the forest representation, prediction
strategy, variance method, randomization and exceptional outcomes across version
changes. Avoiding one language boundary does not by itself justify that
maintenance commitment. The open-source decision should compare the operational
cost of an R process or library bridge with the full scientific and technical
cost of a maintained port.

\section{Population averaging, support and unavailable combinations}

In grf's inspected all-population AIPW path, the mean is a weighted average of
\texttt{get\_scores} output. The variance calculation sums centered weighted
scores within the declared clusters, squares these group contributions and
applies a group-count correction. For binary treatment, the score uses the
usual propensity residual divided by $\widehat e(1-\widehat e)$. For continuous
treatment, an auxiliary regression forest estimates the conditional variance
of the treatment; the debiasing weight divides the treatment residual by that
estimate. \cite{v131_sw_grf}

Small conditional variance is an information problem. Borrowers with essentially
one available offer rate do not support precise estimation of a local rate
response simply because a forest can fit the outcome. Qualification should
retain weak-support cases and identify whether the operation returns a
warning, an extreme weight, an unavailable result or a misleading finite value.
The inspected formula alone supplies no finite-sample guarantee for such cases.

The all, treated, control and overlap populations must remain distinct. In the
overlap path the code fits a residualized outcome on a residualized treatment
and uses cluster or heteroskedastic covariance as applicable. Moving to an
overlap target may improve stability while changing which banking population
the result describes. A wrapper must not silently make that choice on the
analyst's behalf. \cite{v131_sw_grf}

Option support must be determined from reachable control flow. The inspected
average-effect routine rejects TMLE when clusters or sample weights are supplied
before reaching later formulas that contain cluster-related expressions. Seeing
those later formulas would not justify advertising clustered TMLE at this entry
point. Likewise, only specified combinations support continuous treatment.
The adapter's capability matrix needs explicit accepted combinations and
typed refusal for the others. \cite{v131_sw_grf}

\section{A generalized propensity score has physical units}

Let a continuous exposure follow a location-scale law
$A=\mu(X)+s(X)R$, with $s(X)>0$. Conditional change of variables gives
\[
 f_{A\mid X}(a\mid x)
 =\frac{1}{s(x)}f_{R\mid X}
       \left(\frac{a-\mu(x)}{s(x)}\middle|x\right).
\]
The Jacobian $1/s(x)$ is necessary for a density with respect to the original
exposure measure. If a common residual density is used instead of
$f_{R\mid X}$, an additional residual-distribution assumption is being made.

CausalGPS's inspected \texttt{compute\_resid} returns the standardized residual
$(a-b)/c$. Its \texttt{compute\_density} helper calls R's density estimate and
interpolates it with \texttt{rule=2}. In the inspected kernel branch of
\texttt{estimate\_gps}, the standardized-residual density is assigned to the
GPS field without division by the predicted scale in that branch. The normal
branch instead evaluates a normal density using its fitted mean and scale.
These observations establish a concrete scale question for downstream matching,
weighting and dose-response construction. Section~\ref{sec:exec24-dose-response-reuse}
traces the selected weighting ratio and outcome frontend. Its scale witness
narrows the outstanding question without qualifying every downstream operation. \cite{v13_sw_causalgps,v131_sw_causalgps}

At a standardized residual of zero, a standard normal density is about
$0.3989423$. Two strata with scales one and two have original-exposure densities
$0.3989423$ and $0.1994711$ at their respective means. Retaining only the common
standardized-residual value loses that difference. If the exposure is measured
in units one hundred times smaller, its numerical value is multiplied by one
hundred and the corresponding density must be divided by one hundred. The
probability of any physical exposure interval remains unchanged. These exact
transformations provide a reference for a future unit-consistency test.
\cite{v131_project_semantics}

Some downstream procedures might deliberately use a transformed balancing
quantity, while others require a density in the original units. The integration decision therefore remains conditional on a declared use of
the returned field and a justified interpretation of its scale. The selected
weighting path has been traced; its remaining scale and native-conformance
questions must be resolved before admission. Silently changing the helper
could alter package behavior without resolving the scientific contract. A
matched comparison should examine heterogeneous scales, near-zero scales,
counterfactual dose evaluation and an explicit unit conversion, retaining any
unsupported cases.

The causaldrf comparison has a separate source obligation. Its accompanying
license notice identifies the year and copyright holder, supplementing the
package's license declaration. That resolves the availability of that notice;
it does not settle transitive licensing or qualify its interval routines.
Section~\ref{sec:exec24-dose-response-reuse} develops the selected interval-index
and pointwise-versus-simultaneous restrictions, which remain part of the
proposed comparison. Source availability, permission to distribute and justified
uncertainty remain separate questions. \cite{v131_sw_causaldrf}

\section{Implications for reusable causal operations}

These examples support a common semantic contract with explicit graph
orientation, query/failure type, independent observational unit, decision clock,
target population, dose units and uncertainty construction. A business-facing
assistant can elicit these fields and explain unresolved choices, while a
deterministic validator checks the declared operation against its supported
combinations. An LLM's plausible description is not evidence that a package
uses the intended covariance or intervention target.

The relevant next qualification compares complete causal requests. It retains
the same estimand and information across packages, separates sampling from
Monte Carlo error, includes failed fits and preserves unbounded sets. Numerical
agreement in a benign case is useful evidence about implementation, but cannot
establish assignment truth, identification or useful precision in adverse
banking regimes. Those remain scientific and institutional requirements.


The commercial comparison that follows applies the same criteria to documented
vendor capabilities and to this proposal's actual evidence. A product feature
is useful only for a specified request; a proposed preserving interface is not
yet demonstrated superiority over an existing engine.
~~~

After:
~~~text
\chapter{Customer histories, heterogeneous effects and economic units}
\label{v131:histories-forests}

This chapter returns to the customer-level request after the network comparisons.
The earlier longitudinal, forest and dose studies supply the detailed methods;
the question here is what must remain unchanged when their outputs are reused.
Whole histories, within-period event order, the population being averaged and
the units of a treatment density can each change the economic answer. The
examples retain those distinctions without treating a common interface as a
common estimand.

\section{Resampling a sequence requires a sampling model}

Consider a bank evaluating a sequence of servicing decisions over twelve
months. The data contain repeated observations of each borrower, interventions
that depend on previous balances and competing events such as account closure.
The statistical unit might be a borrower history, a household, a branch or an
independent economic calendar. These choices imply different resampling laws.
A routine with ``bootstrap'' in its name does not determine the appropriate law.

LiNGAM's generic bootstrap resamples rows and refits the model. VARLiNGAM has
a distinct path: it fits a lag model, applies a contemporaneous LiNGAM model to
the residuals, resamples residual vectors and reconstructs a sequence using the
fitted autoregressive coefficients. The inspected loop temporarily sets the
lag-selection criterion to \texttt{None}. Consequently, one must inspect the
initial lag/coefficient settings and the loop's refit behavior before claiming
that it propagates model-selection uncertainty. \citep{v131_sw_lingam}

Residual resampling can be a sensible candidate under an appropriate innovation
law. It does not automatically preserve persistent residual dependence,
unmodeled common shocks or a different sampling calendar. Row resampling is a
different candidate suitable for a different observational unit. A banking
comparison should include a correctly specified innovation model and an adverse
case with persistent residuals, preserving the uncertainty target and failures
in both. Bootstrap edge frequencies should not be reported as a calibrated
effect confidence region without an additional argument.

pygformula's inspected bootstrap draws observed subject identifiers with
replacement, copies each subject's complete history and assigns a fresh replicate
identifier to each draw. It updates histories and refits covariate and outcome
models. Expanding to the requested simulation population occurs in a separate
step. These operations distinguish the observed sampling unit from the Monte
Carlo population used to evaluate a fitted regime. \citep{v131_sw_pygformula}

The fresh identifiers matter. Drawing borrower 17 twice should produce two
bootstrap histories, not concatenate them into one impossible person with two
records at every date. An integration test needs duplicate draws, unequal
history lengths and multiple interventions. It must also preserve the original
calendar when that calendar is part of the estimand. Subject resampling alone
does not account for a market shock shared by every subject on the same date.

Failed fits require equally explicit handling. The inspected bootstrap helper
catches an exception, warns and returns a dictionary whose
\texttt{boot\_results} is \texttt{None}. The caller filters such entries when
assembling one result list, but subsequently constructs \texttt{bootests} by
indexing every original entry. A failed entry therefore exposes a static
inconsistency in that caller path. This observation requires reproduction in
the pinned runtime before attributing an observed frequency or impact to it.
The integration must count attempted, successful and failed replications and
preserve the failure reason, irrespective of whether the eventual repair
aborts, returns an explicit unavailable interval or uses a preregistered rule.
\citep{v13_sw_pygformula,v131_sw_pygformula}

\section{The intervention clock and competing-event target}

The inspected simulation path applies an intervention to the current simulated
frame and then refreshes history variables before outcome prediction. Static
schedules index their values by the absolute time index \texttt{int\_values[t]}.
Threshold interventions bound the current action between the supplied limits.
Custom interventions receive the current frame, available pool and time index.
The API permits useful regimes, but does not itself certify that a custom
function uses only information available at the decision time.
\citep{v131_sw_pygformula}

For example, ``contact once delinquency has persisted for two months'' refers
to the observed history before the action, whereas ``contact borrowers who
will be delinquent next month'' uses future information. Both can be expressed
as executable code if future fields have been made available. A valid interface
must enforce the declared information clock and treat prohibited reads as a
contract violation. Thresholds should also retain their monetary units and
whether they change an action, a measurement or an eligibility condition.

Competing events alter the target even when the same outcome model is used.
In a stipulated discrete-time model, let $d_t$ be the probability of a competing
event in period $t$ among those still at risk, and let $y_t$ be the outcome
hazard conditional on avoiding that competing event. If competing events occur
before outcomes within a period, the cumulative incidence through $T$ is
\[
 F_Y(T)=\sum_{t=1}^T
 \left\{\prod_{s<t}(1-d_s)(1-y_s)\right\}(1-d_t)y_t.
\]
This formula follows by partitioning on the period of the first outcome event.
It differs from a hypothetical law that eliminates the competing event.

For two periods with $y=(0.1,0.2)$ and $d=(0.05,0.1)$, the first contribution
is $0.095$ and the second is $0.9(0.95)(0.9)(0.2)=0.1539$. Total incidence is
$0.2489$. Removing competing events while holding these outcome hazards fixed
gives $1-(0.9)(0.8)=0.28$. At a stipulated 10,000 accounts and CNY 100 per event,
the corresponding expected-loss difference is CNY 31,100. This is exact
illustrative arithmetic. It does not identify the effect of eliminating a
real bank's competing event or establish that the outcome hazard would remain
fixed under that intervention. \citep{v131_project_semantics}

The inspected pygformula source separately computes survival risks with and
without a competing-event path, using subject-level cumulative products and
then population means. That code provides a concrete place to check the
within-period ordering, history alignment and target definition. It does not
settle whether observed censoring or competing-event assumptions are valid in
the proposed banking application. \citep{v131_sw_pygformula}

\section{Following an R forest operation into its C++ implementation}

For grf, the causal-forest binding assigns the same variable index to treatment
and instrument, then calls an instrumental predictor. The predictor factory
constructs \texttt{InstrumentalPredictionStrategy}. The native prediction uses
a ratio of centered outcome--instrument and treatment--instrument moments.
When instrument and treatment coincide, this corresponds to a weighted local
covariance divided by a weighted local treatment variance. This traced route
supports the interpretation of continuous-treatment output as a local slope
under its functional assumptions. \citep{v131_sw_grf}

It does not identify an arbitrary finite change in a nonlinear dose-response
curve. A change from an offer rate of one unit to two units needs the appropriate
counterfactual contrast and exposure support. A slope, an average slope and a
finite contrast can coincide in a linear model and diverge otherwise. The bank
must choose the target before comparing software outputs.

The native variance routine forms influence-like quantities across tree
groups, separates between-group variation from group noise and calls a Bayes
debiaser. These groups concern the forest's computation. They are distinct
from the observed borrower clusters used by the R average-effect calculation.
Inspecting both paths avoids the false inference that a tree-group variance
option automatically accounts for a persistent banking calendar. The debiaser,
forest sampling, serialization and numerical safeguards require qualification
before a direct native interface is admitted. Section~\ref{exec13:grf-adapter}
develops the selected native representation, variance-field and persistence
contracts; source inspection does not replace their runtime tests. \citep{v131_sw_grf}

The maintained R interface is therefore a reasonable first integration
candidate, subject to a complete license/dependency and behavioral review.
A direct C++ port would have to preserve the forest representation, prediction
strategy, variance method, randomization and exceptional outcomes across version
changes. Avoiding one language boundary does not by itself justify that
maintenance commitment. The open-source decision should compare the operational
cost of an R process or library bridge with the full scientific and technical
cost of a maintained port.

\section{Population averaging, support and unavailable combinations}

In grf's inspected all-population AIPW path, the mean is a weighted average of
\texttt{get\_scores} output. The variance calculation sums centered weighted
scores within the declared clusters, squares these group contributions and
applies a group-count correction. For binary treatment, the score uses the
usual propensity residual divided by $\widehat e(1-\widehat e)$. For continuous
treatment, an auxiliary regression forest estimates the conditional variance
of the treatment; the debiasing weight divides the treatment residual by that
estimate. \citep{v131_sw_grf}

Small conditional variance is an information problem. Borrowers with essentially
one available offer rate do not support precise estimation of a local rate
response simply because a forest can fit the outcome. Qualification should
retain weak-support cases and identify whether the operation returns a
warning, an extreme weight, an unavailable result or a misleading finite value.
The inspected formula alone supplies no finite-sample guarantee for such cases.

The all, treated, control and overlap populations must remain distinct. In the
overlap path the code fits a residualized outcome on a residualized treatment
and uses cluster or heteroskedastic covariance as applicable. Moving to an
overlap target may improve stability while changing which banking population
the result describes. A wrapper must not silently make that choice on the
analyst's behalf. \citep{v131_sw_grf}

Option support must be determined from reachable control flow. The inspected
average-effect routine rejects TMLE when clusters or sample weights are supplied
before reaching later formulas that contain cluster-related expressions. Seeing
those later formulas would not justify advertising clustered TMLE at this entry
point. Likewise, only specified combinations support continuous treatment.
The adapter's capability matrix needs explicit accepted combinations and
typed refusal for the others. \citep{v131_sw_grf}

\section{A generalized propensity score has physical units}

Let a continuous exposure follow a location-scale law
$A=\mu(X)+s(X)R$, with $s(X)>0$. Conditional change of variables gives
\[
 f_{A\mid X}(a\mid x)
 =\frac{1}{s(x)}f_{R\mid X}
       \left(\frac{a-\mu(x)}{s(x)}\middle|x\right).
\]
The Jacobian $1/s(x)$ is necessary for a density with respect to the original
exposure measure. If a common residual density is used instead of
$f_{R\mid X}$, an additional residual-distribution assumption is being made.

CausalGPS's inspected \texttt{compute\_resid} returns the standardized residual
$(a-b)/c$. Its \texttt{compute\_density} helper calls R's density estimate and
interpolates it with \texttt{rule=2}. In the inspected kernel branch of
\texttt{estimate\_gps}, the standardized-residual density is assigned to the
GPS field without division by the predicted scale in that branch. The normal
branch instead evaluates a normal density using its fitted mean and scale.
These observations establish a concrete scale question for downstream matching,
weighting and dose-response construction. Section~\ref{sec:exec24-dose-response-reuse}
traces the selected weighting ratio and outcome frontend. Its scale witness
narrows the outstanding question without qualifying every downstream operation. \citep{v13_sw_causalgps,v131_sw_causalgps}

At a standardized residual of zero, a standard normal density is about
$0.3989423$. Two strata with scales one and two have original-exposure densities
$0.3989423$ and $0.1994711$ at their respective means. Retaining only the common
standardized-residual value loses that difference. If the exposure is measured
in units one hundred times smaller, its numerical value is multiplied by one
hundred and the corresponding density must be divided by one hundred. The
probability of any physical exposure interval remains unchanged. These exact
transformations provide a reference for a future unit-consistency test.
\citep{v131_project_semantics}

Some downstream procedures might deliberately use a transformed balancing
quantity, while others require a density in the original units. The integration decision therefore remains conditional on a declared use of
the returned field and a justified interpretation of its scale. The selected
weighting path has been traced; its remaining scale and native-conformance
questions must be resolved before admission. Silently changing the helper
could alter package behavior without resolving the scientific contract. A
matched comparison should examine heterogeneous scales, near-zero scales,
counterfactual dose evaluation and an explicit unit conversion, retaining any
unsupported cases.

The causaldrf comparison has a separate source obligation. Its accompanying
license notice identifies the year and copyright holder, supplementing the
package's license declaration. That resolves the availability of that notice;
it does not settle transitive licensing or qualify its interval routines.
Section~\ref{sec:exec24-dose-response-reuse} develops the selected interval-index
and pointwise-versus-simultaneous restrictions, which remain part of the
proposed comparison. Source availability, permission to distribute and justified
uncertainty remain separate questions. \citep{v131_sw_causaldrf}

\section{Implications for reusable causal operations}

These examples support a common semantic contract with explicit graph

target population, dose units and uncertainty construction. A business-facing

deterministic validator checks the declared operation against its supported
combinations. An LLM's plausible description is not evidence that a package
uses the intended covariance or intervention target.

The relevant next qualification compares complete causal requests. It retains
the same estimand and information across packages, separates sampling from

agreement in a benign case is useful evidence about implementation, but cannot

banking regimes. Those remain scientific and institutional requirements.
~~~

After:
~~~text
The proposal should therefore retain expert-guided averaging as a candidate
for generating and reviewing diagrams. A public prototype comparison can use
known generated mechanisms, withheld domains, correlated component failures,
wrong expert assertions, budget exhaustion and incompatible graph endpoints.
It should compare static aggregation, selective expert queries, a numerical
method without expert input and a workflow that preserves unresolved graph

review cost. Graph scores remain useful diagnostics, but qualification of an
~~~

~~~text
\section{When an end-to-end graph workflow still lacks calibrated effects}
\label{rel64:graph-confidence}

The preceding example leaves a practical question. Can an assistant quantify
its uncertainty about each arrow, draw many graphs and pass those graphs to a
flexible generative model? Such a workflow would be useful if its final
intervals retained the mechanisms relevant to the bank's decision. Each step,
however, changes the object whose uncertainty is being described. A probability
assigned to a sentence, a distribution over graphs and a sampling interval for
an intervention effect require different justifications.

Molero P{\'e}rez's February 2026 thesis develops a concrete instance of this
workflow. Pairwise language judgments, optionally augmented by retrieval,
produce proposed causal relations. The study compares self-reported
confidence, selected-answer token probabilities and a HallBayes refinement
based on weakened prompts. Its chosen procedure converts the refinement's
risk score into an edge probability, averages three runs, samples 100 graphs
and fits causal neural spline flows. This is a substantive implementation
proposal with reported biomedical comparisons. Its availability permits a
more precise assessment than either accepting the integration as calibrated
or dismissing it merely because language models propose the graphs. The
following assessment concerns the printed method and results; it does not
reproduce the upstream software or its model calls.\citep{rel64molero}

\subsection{A success ceiling gives an error floor}

Write the Bernoulli relative entropy, in nats, as
\[
 d(p,q)=p\log\frac pq+(1-p)\log\frac{1-p}{1-q},
 \qquad 0<q<1.
\]
The thesis's equations (14)--(16) average clipped positive log-probability
lifts for the selected answer under original and masked prompts. It then uses
this quantity as an information budget in a Bernoulli inequality and defines
the largest compatible success probability. For any stipulated nonnegative
budget $\Delta$, the logical implication of that inequality is
\begin{equation}
 \begin{split}
 d(p,q)\leq\Delta,\qquad
 p_{\max}(\Delta,q)&=\sup\{u\in[0,1]:d(u,q)\leq\Delta\},\\
 p\leq p_{\max}(\Delta,q)&\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 1-p\geq1-p_{\max}(\Delta,q).
 \end{split}
 \label{rel64:error-floor}
\end{equation}
The complement is therefore a lower bound on error under this premise. The
thesis calls it an upper bound and subsequently uses it to set graph-sampling
probabilities. That interpretation does not follow from the displayed
inequality. With $q=.1$ and $\Delta=.5$, the upper endpoint is approximately
$.49505$, giving complement $.50495$. Yet $p=.1$ satisfies the inequality
and has error $.9$. This exact counterexample concerns the direction of the
bound; it does not assert that every estimated graph in the thesis is wrong.
\citep{rel64molero}

There is a further distinction between the score and the premise. Evaluating
a clipped log-lift at one selected answer does not generally evaluate an
expectation over the full answer distribution. Nor does masking a prompt
establish the probability that its causal assertion is true in the intended
bank population. A separate calibration argument is needed before the score
can stand for the information budget or the causal correctness probability
in~\eqref{rel64:error-floor}. Averaging repeated calls reduces some
Monte Carlo variation conditional on the response mechanism; it cannot by
itself supply either missing interpretation.

\subsection{The information inequality remains useful in its stated direction}

The thesis cites the 2025 version of Chlon and colleagues' information-budget
paper. Its Theorem 5 follows from convexity and reduction to an event. Let
$P$ be a reference distribution, $S_k$ predictive distributions, and
$\bar S=\mathbb E_k S_k$ their mixture. For an event $A$, put
$p=P(A)$ and $q=\bar S(A)$. Under absolute continuity and finite expected
relative entropy,
\begin{equation}
 \mathbb E_k\operatorname{KL}(P\Vert S_k)
 \geq\operatorname{KL}(P\Vert\bar S)
 \geq d(p,q).
 \label{rel64:necessary-information}
\end{equation}
The first step is Jensen's inequality in the second distribution; the second
is the information lost when the outcome is reduced to membership of $A$.
Equality for the entire chain requires equality in both steps. Sufficiency
of the event for distinguishing $P$ from the mixture addresses the second
step; it does not eliminate a positive mixture Jensen gap.
\citep{rel64chlonone,rel64chlonthree}

A necessary information requirement is not a success guarantee. Consider the
project's four-outcome construction
\[
 S=(.05,.05,.001,.899),\qquad
 P=(.05,.05,.899,.001),\qquad A=\{1,2\}.
\]
Use the same $S$ for every prompt. Both event probabilities are $.1$, but
\[
 \operatorname{KL}(P\Vert S)=.898\log899\simeq6.10755
 >d(.95,.1)\simeq1.99421.
\]
The extra divergence lies within the failure event. Exceeding the information
requirement associated with a target success probability of $.95$ has not
raised actual reference-event probability above $.1$. This is a failure of a
sufficiency interpretation, not a counterexample to
inequality~\eqref{rel64:necessary-information}.

The June 2026 version changes the title, author list and deployment
description. It explicitly requires a specified Bernoulli predicate and a
reference supplied by gold labels, a deterministic verification primitive or
an audited calibrated verifier. Unrestricted generation without such a
reference is outside its stated regime. Its reported held-out audit remains
one particular evaluation, not an intervention-coverage result. These
qualifications improve the interpretation of the proposal, but they do not
turn the necessary inequality into a sufficient guarantee. Even with a
binary reference, $d(.05,.5)=d(.95,.5)$: without a justified branch
restriction, inversion does not distinguish low from high event probability.
With constant predictive probability $.5$ and target $.95$, the printed
budget ratio is one for either reference probability, before clipping matters.
The event, reference and direction of the bound must therefore remain visible
when adapting the planner. A later version's verifier assumptions also cannot
be silently attributed to the thesis's earlier masking procedure.
\citep{rel64chlonthree}

\subsection{Independent edge draws need a coherent graph law}

For an accepted edge the thesis uses one minus the risk score; for a rejected
edge it uses the risk score. It then samples edges independently and applies
topological sorting. Sorting represents an already acyclic graph in an
appropriate order; it cannot make every independently drawn graph acyclic.
The treatment of a failed sort is part of the probability law. The following
example diagnoses the general construction and does not claim that the
thesis's particular draws contained a cycle.\citep{rel64molero}

Suppose the only possible arrows are $A\to B$, $B\to C$ and $C\to A$,
each assigned probability $4/5$. Under independent draws,
\[
 \Pr(\text{cycle})=(4/5)^3=64/125,\qquad
 \Pr(A\to B\mid\text{acyclic})=\frac{36}{61}\simeq.59016.
\]
The conditional numerator is $(4/5)[1-(4/5)^2]$ and the denominator
is $1-(4/5)^3$. Rejecting cyclic draws changes the edge probabilities;
deleting an edge instead creates another distribution determined by the
deletion rule. Indeed, no distribution supported on DAGs can retain all
three proposed marginals: a DAG contains at most two of these arrows, whereas
their proposed expected count is $12/5$. This strengthens the majority-cycle
example in Section~\ref{exec20:expert-votes}: structural validity and
marginal probability interpretation cannot be checked independently.

A usable alternative defines a joint law on an explicitly admissible model
class, records its prior and likelihood interpretation, and tests sensitivity
to that class. When those probabilities cannot be justified, retain compatible
graphs and propagate their effect sets instead. Neither route assumes that a
linguistic probability score is already a posterior over banking mechanisms.

\subsection{An interval comparison needs an intervention reference}

The thesis trains four groups of 100 flow models: an expert reference graph,
the sampled language-derived graphs, the expert graph's ordering without its
adjacency restrictions, and random orderings without those restrictions.
Within each group, the 2.5th and 97.5th percentiles of the fitted effect
estimates define an interval. The reported interval-overlap means are .723,
.712 and .301 for the last three groups compared with the fitted expert-graph
group. The first two differ by .011. That contrast describes these fitted
model intervals; the table does not provide a paired uncertainty interval for
the difference. It is not evidence that language assistance generally
outperforms knowledge of the causal ordering.\citep{rel64molero}

Agreement with a fitted expert reference is useful when the task is to
reconstruct that reference. It is not observed intervention truth. If two
methods return the identical interval $[.5,.7]$ while the true effect is zero,
their interval overlap is one and both miss the effect. More generally,
percentiles across graph choices and training runs describe variation under
that procedure. A 95\% repeated-sampling coverage claim needs a defined
sampling experiment, valid treatment of graph uncertainty and an identified
target. A calibrated Bayesian interpretation instead needs a justified joint
posterior and corresponding model assumptions. Repetition alone supplies
neither interpretation.

The numerical comparator also needs matched information and a suitable test.
The thesis specifies chi-square conditional-independence tests for its PC
baseline while prioritizing continuous clinical variables. Its language
workflow additionally draws on outside knowledge. A fair follow-up would
resolve the continuous-variable testing and coding choices, compare methods
given the same admissible knowledge, and keep a numerical-only arm. The
thesis itself acknowledges calibration problems, lost valid edges and the
absence of hidden-confounder handling. Those limitations belong alongside
the favorable reported scores.\citep{rel64molero}

For the bank, the investment conclusion follows from the retained example in
Section~\ref{exec20:expert-identification}. Its observationally identical
mechanisms imply margins of USD~20 and USD~$-40$ under the same stated
valuation and cost. A narrow distribution of repeated fits cannot resolve
that disagreement. Before releasing a financial recommendation, the workflow
must state the decision-time information that excludes a mechanism, or carry
the ambiguity into the recommendation. Graph proposals, retrieval and flexible
conditional models remain candidate components. Their integration deserves
evaluation against an expert-reviewed graph, a numerical discovery route and
an ambiguity-preserving analysis, with withheld mechanisms, interval coverage,
decision loss and complete review cost assessed separately.
~~~


## Edit 165
docs/release/2026-10-29/source/docs/writing-repair/2026-10-29/assistance.tex

Reconcile new primary reading and explicit quarantine with the recommendation and candidate census.

Substantive destination: Preserve the questionnaire comparator, earlier readings, cutoff limitations and live-evaluation requirements; add precise disposition and distinguish inspection from evidential admission.

Before:
~~~text
The preferred first assisted task is therefore question clarification
with evidence retrieval and explicit alternatives, evaluated against
the questionnaire. Identification and numerical results remain delegated
to defined procedures. Expansion to autonomous discovery, variable
construction or recommendations requires task-specific evidence. The
frontier evidence includes the three additional inspected originals discussed
in Section~\ref{gap29:frontier}. The preceding inventory recorded 85 inspected
and 28 unread entries. The financing-and-exports and epistemology originals
now developed in Section~\ref{closure30:formulation} bring this edition to
87 inspected and 26 unread. The financing study supports a bounded extraction
and explanation comparison; its scores do not identify a lending effect.
The epistemology discussion supplies no separate intervention estimate. These
readings sharpen the limits of assistance without establishing live banking
benefit. The remaining omissions still limit broad claims. A live comparison
requires a fixed model version, held-out questions, matched information,
independent adjudication and a budget.~\citep{rel18li,rel18mugleston}
~~~

After:
~~~text
The preferred first assisted task is therefore question clarification
with evidence retrieval and explicit alternatives, evaluated against
the questionnaire. Identification and numerical results remain delegated
to defined procedures. Expansion to autonomous discovery, variable
construction or recommendations requires task-specific evidence. The
frontier evidence includes the three additional inspected originals discussed
in Section~\ref{gap29:frontier}. The preceding inventory recorded 85 inspected
and 28 unread entries. The financing-and-exports and epistemology originals
now developed in Section~\ref{closure30:formulation} brought the preceding edition to
87 inspected and 26 unread. The financing study supports a bounded extraction
and explanation comparison; its scores do not identify a lending effect.
The epistemology discussion supplies no separate intervention estimate. These
readings sharpen the limits of assistance without establishing live banking
benefit. The remaining omissions still limit broad claims. A live comparison
requires a fixed model version, held-out questions, matched information,
independent adjudication and a budget.~\citep{rel18li,rel18mugleston}

The end-to-end workflow in Section~
ef{rel64:graph-confidence} adds a
further primary comparison. It turns a prompt-based risk score into sampled
graphs and fitted effect intervals, but its uncertainty interpretation needs
the additional conditions developed there. A separate thesis by Ait Lhouss
has been inspected only for a source-quality disposition. Section~4.1 gives
ten included studies, whereas its abstract and Tables~5--6 give eighty; its
selected-study table lists ten. Those unresolved denominators prevent using
its reported review coverage or productivity results here. This is an
exclusion of those claims, not an allegation of intent or formal retraction.
The current inventory therefore contains 89 inspected entries, including
this one explicit quarantine, and 24 unread entries. Inspection does not
mean that every inspected work supplies positive evidence.citep{rel64molero,rel64aitlhouss}
~~~

## Edit 168
docs/release/2026-10-29/source/docs/writing-repair/2026-10-29/src/5e1bb6d4d921349d.tex

Restore four spaces before source citations identified in rendered-text review.

Substantive destination: All source comparison, equations, limitations, counts and economic interpretation remain verbatim apart from citation spacing.

Before:
~~~text
\section{When an end-to-end graph workflow still lacks calibrated effects}
\label{rel64:graph-confidence}

The preceding example leaves a practical question. Can an assistant quantify
its uncertainty about each arrow, draw many graphs and pass those graphs to a
flexible generative model? Such a workflow would be useful if its final
intervals retained the mechanisms relevant to the bank's decision. Each step,
however, changes the object whose uncertainty is being described. A probability
assigned to a sentence, a distribution over graphs and a sampling interval for
an intervention effect require different justifications.

Molero P{\'e}rez's February 2026 thesis develops a concrete instance of this
workflow. Pairwise language judgments, optionally augmented by retrieval,
produce proposed causal relations. The study compares self-reported
confidence, selected-answer token probabilities and a HallBayes refinement
based on weakened prompts. Its chosen procedure converts the refinement's
risk score into an edge probability, averages three runs, samples 100 graphs
and fits causal neural spline flows. This is a substantive implementation
proposal with reported biomedical comparisons. Its availability permits a
more precise assessment than either accepting the integration as calibrated
or dismissing it merely because language models propose the graphs. The
following assessment concerns the printed method and results; it does not
reproduce the upstream software or its model calls.\citep{rel64molero}

\subsection{A success ceiling gives an error floor}

Write the Bernoulli relative entropy, in nats, as
\[
 d(p,q)=p\log\frac pq+(1-p)\log\frac{1-p}{1-q},
 \qquad 0<q<1.
\]
The thesis's equations (14)--(16) average clipped positive log-probability
lifts for the selected answer under original and masked prompts. It then uses
this quantity as an information budget in a Bernoulli inequality and defines
the largest compatible success probability. For any stipulated nonnegative
budget $\Delta$, the logical implication of that inequality is
\begin{equation}
 \begin{split}
 d(p,q)\leq\Delta,\qquad
 p_{\max}(\Delta,q)&=\sup\{u\in[0,1]:d(u,q)\leq\Delta\},\\
 p\leq p_{\max}(\Delta,q)&\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 1-p\geq1-p_{\max}(\Delta,q).
 \end{split}
 \label{rel64:error-floor}
\end{equation}
The complement is therefore a lower bound on error under this premise. The
thesis calls it an upper bound and subsequently uses it to set graph-sampling
probabilities. That interpretation does not follow from the displayed
inequality. With $q=.1$ and $\Delta=.5$, the upper endpoint is approximately
$.49505$, giving complement $.50495$. Yet $p=.1$ satisfies the inequality
and has error $.9$. This exact counterexample concerns the direction of the
bound; it does not assert that every estimated graph in the thesis is wrong.
\citep{rel64molero}

There is a further distinction between the score and the premise. Evaluating
a clipped log-lift at one selected answer does not generally evaluate an
expectation over the full answer distribution. Nor does masking a prompt
establish the probability that its causal assertion is true in the intended
bank population. A separate calibration argument is needed before the score
can stand for the information budget or the causal correctness probability
in~\eqref{rel64:error-floor}. Averaging repeated calls reduces some
Monte Carlo variation conditional on the response mechanism; it cannot by
itself supply either missing interpretation.

\subsection{The information inequality remains useful in its stated direction}

The thesis cites the 2025 version of Chlon and colleagues' information-budget
paper. Its Theorem 5 follows from convexity and reduction to an event. Let
$P$ be a reference distribution, $S_k$ predictive distributions, and
$\bar S=\mathbb E_k S_k$ their mixture. For an event $A$, put
$p=P(A)$ and $q=\bar S(A)$. Under absolute continuity and finite expected
relative entropy,
\begin{equation}
 \mathbb E_k\operatorname{KL}(P\Vert S_k)
 \geq\operatorname{KL}(P\Vert\bar S)
 \geq d(p,q).
 \label{rel64:necessary-information}
\end{equation}
The first step is Jensen's inequality in the second distribution; the second
is the information lost when the outcome is reduced to membership of $A$.
Equality for the entire chain requires equality in both steps. Sufficiency
of the event for distinguishing $P$ from the mixture addresses the second
step; it does not eliminate a positive mixture Jensen gap.
\citep{rel64chlonone,rel64chlonthree}

A necessary information requirement is not a success guarantee. Consider the
project's four-outcome construction
\[
 S=(.05,.05,.001,.899),\qquad
 P=(.05,.05,.899,.001),\qquad A=\{1,2\}.
\]
Use the same $S$ for every prompt. Both event probabilities are $.1$, but
\[
 \operatorname{KL}(P\Vert S)=.898\log899\simeq6.10755
 >d(.95,.1)\simeq1.99421.
\]
The extra divergence lies within the failure event. Exceeding the information
requirement associated with a target success probability of $.95$ has not
raised actual reference-event probability above $.1$. This is a failure of a
sufficiency interpretation, not a counterexample to
inequality~\eqref{rel64:necessary-information}.

The June 2026 version changes the title, author list and deployment
description. It explicitly requires a specified Bernoulli predicate and a
reference supplied by gold labels, a deterministic verification primitive or
an audited calibrated verifier. Unrestricted generation without such a
reference is outside its stated regime. Its reported held-out audit remains
one particular evaluation, not an intervention-coverage result. These
qualifications improve the interpretation of the proposal, but they do not
turn the necessary inequality into a sufficient guarantee. Even with a
binary reference, $d(.05,.5)=d(.95,.5)$: without a justified branch
restriction, inversion does not distinguish low from high event probability.
With constant predictive probability $.5$ and target $.95$, the printed
budget ratio is one for either reference probability, before clipping matters.
The event, reference and direction of the bound must therefore remain visible
when adapting the planner. A later version's verifier assumptions also cannot
be silently attributed to the thesis's earlier masking procedure.
\citep{rel64chlonthree}

\subsection{Independent edge draws need a coherent graph law}

For an accepted edge the thesis uses one minus the risk score; for a rejected
edge it uses the risk score. It then samples edges independently and applies
topological sorting. Sorting represents an already acyclic graph in an
appropriate order; it cannot make every independently drawn graph acyclic.
The treatment of a failed sort is part of the probability law. The following
example diagnoses the general construction and does not claim that the
thesis's particular draws contained a cycle.\citep{rel64molero}

Suppose the only possible arrows are $A\to B$, $B\to C$ and $C\to A$,
each assigned probability $4/5$. Under independent draws,
\[
 \Pr(\text{cycle})=(4/5)^3=64/125,\qquad
 \Pr(A\to B\mid\text{acyclic})=\frac{36}{61}\simeq.59016.
\]
The conditional numerator is $(4/5)[1-(4/5)^2]$ and the denominator
is $1-(4/5)^3$. Rejecting cyclic draws changes the edge probabilities;
deleting an edge instead creates another distribution determined by the
deletion rule. Indeed, no distribution supported on DAGs can retain all
three proposed marginals: a DAG contains at most two of these arrows, whereas
their proposed expected count is $12/5$. This strengthens the majority-cycle
example in Section~\ref{exec20:expert-votes}: structural validity and
marginal probability interpretation cannot be checked independently.

A usable alternative defines a joint law on an explicitly admissible model
class, records its prior and likelihood interpretation, and tests sensitivity
to that class. When those probabilities cannot be justified, retain compatible
graphs and propagate their effect sets instead. Neither route assumes that a
linguistic probability score is already a posterior over banking mechanisms.

\subsection{An interval comparison needs an intervention reference}

The thesis trains four groups of 100 flow models: an expert reference graph,
the sampled language-derived graphs, the expert graph's ordering without its
adjacency restrictions, and random orderings without those restrictions.
Within each group, the 2.5th and 97.5th percentiles of the fitted effect
estimates define an interval. The reported interval-overlap means are .723,
.712 and .301 for the last three groups compared with the fitted expert-graph
group. The first two differ by .011. That contrast describes these fitted
model intervals; the table does not provide a paired uncertainty interval for
the difference. It is not evidence that language assistance generally
outperforms knowledge of the causal ordering.\citep{rel64molero}

Agreement with a fitted expert reference is useful when the task is to
reconstruct that reference. It is not observed intervention truth. If two
methods return the identical interval $[.5,.7]$ while the true effect is zero,
their interval overlap is one and both miss the effect. More generally,
percentiles across graph choices and training runs describe variation under
that procedure. A 95\% repeated-sampling coverage claim needs a defined
sampling experiment, valid treatment of graph uncertainty and an identified
target. A calibrated Bayesian interpretation instead needs a justified joint
posterior and corresponding model assumptions. Repetition alone supplies
neither interpretation.

The numerical comparator also needs matched information and a suitable test.
The thesis specifies chi-square conditional-independence tests for its PC
baseline while prioritizing continuous clinical variables. Its language
workflow additionally draws on outside knowledge. A fair follow-up would
resolve the continuous-variable testing and coding choices, compare methods
given the same admissible knowledge, and keep a numerical-only arm. The
thesis itself acknowledges calibration problems, lost valid edges and the
absence of hidden-confounder handling. Those limitations belong alongside
the favorable reported scores.\citep{rel64molero}

For the bank, the investment conclusion follows from the retained example in
Section~\ref{exec20:expert-identification}. Its observationally identical
mechanisms imply margins of USD~20 and USD~$-40$ under the same stated
valuation and cost. A narrow distribution of repeated fits cannot resolve
that disagreement. Before releasing a financial recommendation, the workflow
must state the decision-time information that excludes a mechanism, or carry
the ambiguity into the recommendation. Graph proposals, retrieval and flexible
conditional models remain candidate components. Their integration deserves
evaluation against an expert-reviewed graph, a numerical discovery route and
an ambiguity-preserving analysis, with withheld mechanisms, interval coverage,
decision loss and complete review cost assessed separately.

~~~
