## Edit 169
docs/release/2026-10-29/source/docs/writing-repair/2026-10-29/src/ef6e4a66d3e6badf.tex

Develop the newly inspected text-signal methods and constructive embedding alternative, with explicit identification, score and target-weight distinctions.

Substantive destination: All prior linguistic measurement, perception-error derivations, public complaint study, longitudinal policy and component alternatives remain verbatim. Added exact examples make the monetary consequences reconstructible.

Before:
~~~text
\subsection{Public complaints as a prototype resource}
~~~
After:
~~~text

\subsection{A useful text signal still needs an intervention argument}
\label{rel66:text-signals}

The distinction between a label and an action also governs claims that a
language model has discovered causes inside case notes. A bank may find that
notes mentioning a payment reminder have higher predicted repayment. Such a
signal can help an analyst search records or formulate a hypothesis. To decide
whether to send a reminder, however, the bank needs the repayment contrast
under an implementable reminder policy and its alternative, using information
available before that choice. A word recorded after the action cannot silently
become a baseline treatment assignment or an admissible adjustment variable.

Three pharmacovigilance studies provide a concrete comparison.
\citet{rel66inferbert} trains a classifier on sentences constructed from
commercially curated FAERS fields. Its two reported tasks concern acute liver
failure and tramadol-related death. Equations 2--6 form groups according to
whether an observed field contains a term, then compare the classifier outputs
in those groups. The paper calls this operation do-calculus. In the inspected
\texttt{causal\_tree} and \texttt{compute\_gap} routines, the predictions are
read from a saved file and partitioned by feature membership; the procedure
does not replace a structural equation or evaluate both actions for the same
unit. Recursive restriction to records containing an earlier term does not
itself supply an adjustment argument. \citep{rel66inferbertcode}

To state what this computes, let $W_i$ denote the recorded text or fields,
$H_i=1$ indicate a selected term's presence, and $f(W_i)$ be a fixed predicted
endpoint probability. On the retained records, the root comparison is
\begin{equation}
 \widehat\Delta_H=
 \frac{\sum_i H_i f(W_i)}{\sum_i H_i}
 -\frac{\sum_i(1-H_i)f(W_i)}{\sum_i(1-H_i)}.
 \label{eq:rel66-score-gap}
\end{equation}
This expression assumes nonempty groups; the code further restricts to
nonmissing fields and groups exceeding its support threshold. Its normal-score
denominator uses the sum of the two within-group sample variances divided by
their respective counts. That calculation describes uncertainty about a
particular score contrast only under its own sampling and fitting assumptions.
It does not establish exchangeability, resolve reporting selection or account
automatically for learning and repeatedly selecting the highest-scoring terms.
The published paper describes adjusted significance levels, while the selected
routine visibly applies an unadjusted one-sided threshold of 0.05. The local
source inspection therefore cannot certify the paper's complete multiplicity
procedure or reconstruct its reported results. \citep{rel66inferbert,rel66inferbertcode}

\citet{rel66deepcausality} extends this design to free text from LiverTox,
using named-entity recognition to propose terms. Its label is inherited from a
drug-level likelihood category: the extracted sentences receive that drug's
positive or negative label. The reported split places 14,361 sentences into
training and test sets in a 9:1 ratio; the account selects its checkpoint using
the test-set classification results. This does not supply an untouched
drug-level evaluation. It leaves both selection optimism and within-drug
dependence to be assessed; no numerical penalty is inferred here. The reported
18 clinically consistent terms out of 20 retained terms measure agreement with
domain knowledge. They do not measure intervention-effect bias or interval
coverage. The paper itself states that the method cannot classify the selected
factors into confounders and colliders. In the pinned notebook, the same score
partition is visible and the significance-filter lines are commented out.
These are reasons to specify the exact implementation and evaluation,
including useful hypothesis-ranking behavior, before adopting its causal
interpretation. \citep{rel66deepcausalitycode}

The subsequent comparison by \citet{rel66pharma} retains both the structured
and free-text tasks. Its domain-expert assessment asks whether enriched terms
are relevant to liver injury. The stated positive predictive quantity is
$\mathrm{TP}/(\mathrm{TP}+\mathrm{FP})$; the narrative reports 0.95 for the
task-adapted DILI-BERT and 0.20 for RoBERTa. Those numbers concern this selected
term-review task. They cannot be read as probabilities that a banking
intervention estimate is correct. The method paragraph cites references 9--10,
whereas the bibliography identifies InferBERT and DeepCausality as 17--18;
the comparison here follows the actual primary methods, rather than treating
the mismatched reference numbers as a new derivation. The source's model-size
and domain-adaptation comparisons remain useful experimental leads within
their reported task and cohort.

An exact authored example isolates the missing step without imitating any of
these experiments. Let unrecorded customer readiness $U$ be Bernoulli with
probability $1/2$, and let a historical action $A$ have probabilities
$\Pr(A=1\mid U=1)=4/5$ and $\Pr(A=1\mid U=0)=1/5$. Suppose repayment $Y$ has
conditional probability $1/10+3U/5$ and is independent of $A$ given $U$.
Independent uniform random variables can implement these two Bernoulli draws,
so intervening on $A$ leaves the repayment equation unchanged. A note records
$A$ exactly. Bayes' rule gives
\begin{equation}
 E[Y\mid A=1]=\frac{29}{50},\qquad
 E[Y\mid A=0]=\frac{11}{50},\qquad
 E[Y(1)-Y(0)]=0.
 \label{eq:rel66-confounding}
\end{equation}
Even the exactly calibrated predictor $f(A)=E[Y\mid A]$ therefore produces
a term-group gap of $9/25=0.36$. For a stipulated USD~100 value per repayment
and USD~10 action cost per account, treating the gap as an effect implies
USD~26 net value; the stipulated intervention law gives minus USD~10. This
is a finite probability construction, not a bank estimate or a replication of
the source studies. Better prediction of this same observational law cannot
choose the causal interpretation.

\subsection{A conditional route from text to adjustment}

Text can nevertheless contain useful adjustment information. The route in
\citet{rel66veitch} begins by assuming that the original text suffices for
the specified causal adjustment. It then asks whether a lower-dimensional
embedding retains the outcome or propensity information needed for estimation.
This addresses loss of already sufficient information during compression; it
does not establish that an arbitrary case note captured all common causes.
Its semi-synthetic experiments test the proposed representations under stated
generators. Its consistency result also requires sufficiently accurate nuisance
models and a well-behaved estimator, with bounded outcomes and overlap given
as sufficient conditions in the discussion. Predictive language performance
alone does not verify these requirements in a bank.

For a single-stage banking use, take $W$ to be text available before a defined
action $A$, let $Z=\lambda(W)$ be its representation, and let $Y$ be the later
outcome. State consistency, conditional exchangeability given $W$, and overlap.
If $Z$ preserves the required adjustment information, define
$m_a(z)=E[Y\mid A=a,Z=z]$ and $e(z)=\Pr(A=1\mid Z=z)$. The target population
then determines the averaging law:
\begin{align}
 \tau_{\mathrm{ATT}}
 &=E[m_1(Z)-m_0(Z)\mid A=1]
   =\frac{E[e(Z)\{m_1(Z)-m_0(Z)\}]}{\Pr(A=1)},\\
 \tau_{\mathrm{ATE}}
 &=E[m_1(Z)-m_0(Z)].
 \label{eq:rel66-target-weights}
\end{align}
The equality for ATT follows from Bayes' rule after identification. It shows
why a representation model and a correct conditional outcome contrast are
insufficient if the final estimator averages over the wrong population.
In the inspected proceedings version, equation 2.1 conditions the outer
expectation on treatment, and equations 2.2--2.3 retain propensity or treated
weights. The later displayed equation 3.1 and the final display in Theorem 3.1
omit those weights or that conditioning while discussing the same target.
We retain the explicit ATT definition and distinguish the displayed formulas;
we do not treat this notation mismatch as a theorem that ATT equals ATE.
The separate appendix proof is not contained in the inspected ten-page
proceedings copy. \citep{rel66veitch}

The distinction can reverse a decision even when all confounding is observed.
Let $Z$ have two equally prevalent strata, with propensities $1/5$ and $4/5$,
and conditional repayment effects zero and $3/10$. One feasible law sets
$m_0(z)=1/5$ in both strata and $m_1(z)=1/5$ and $1/2$, respectively.
Then $\Pr(A=1)=1/2$, the treated population places weights $1/5$ and $4/5$
on the two strata, and
\begin{equation}
 \tau_{\mathrm{ATE}}=\frac{3}{20}=0.15,\qquad
 \tau_{\mathrm{ATT}}=\frac{6}{25}=0.24.
\end{equation}
At USD~100 per repayment and USD~20 per action, the respective net contrasts
are minus USD~5 and plus USD~4 per account in their respective populations.
Neither is a universal policy value: expanding eligibility beyond historical
recipients changes the population and may change support. The example holds
the conditional effects fixed so that the consequence of weighting is visible.

The proposed service should consequently distinguish three outputs: a
term-ranking diagnostic, a measured pretreatment variable, and an identified
effect under a declared adjustment argument. A credible text-assisted study
retains source spans and availability times, evaluates extraction on held-out
customers and periods, and propagates measurement and nuisance-estimation
uncertainty using a sampling design appropriate to repeated notes. It compares
the text representation with structured covariates and simpler text summaries,
reports overlap and sensitivity to omitted information, and uses an untouched
evaluation set after model selection. If the adjustment argument fails,
hypothesis ranking or a randomized prospective action comparison remains a
substantive alternative. These requirements preserve the useful text-processing
contribution without turning its classifier score into a causal assurance.

\subsection{Public complaints as a prototype resource}
~~~

## Edit 177
docs/release/2026-10-29/source/docs/writing-repair/2026-10-29/assistance.tex

Replace instruction sequences or acquisition chronology with connected scientific and economic explanation.

Substantive destination: Formulation fields, unresolved choices, Kıcıman and causal-learn+ scope, questionnaire comparator, correction effort, deposit counterexample, text-adjustment limits, coding-rule and labelled-example baselines, missing/ambiguous cases and independent validation remain explicit.

Before:
~~~text
For formulation, the useful output is a precise intervention, alternative,
population, outcome, horizon and information date, together with unresolved
choices. The retained Kıcıman and causal-learn+ discussions motivate
question clarification and graph critique. Their descriptions support
a prototype candidate, not the truth of bank-specific assumptions.
\citep{v12_la_2305_00050,v12_la_2606_23608}
The strongest elementary comparator is a structured questionnaire completed
by an economist. Measure material omissions found and the time required
to correct the final question. The deposit distinction among assignment,
receipt, acceptance and price is an explicit test case. An eloquent
answer that silently chooses one is a failure.

Variable construction requires a separate assessment. A model can suggest
a record field or an extraction rule, but measurement validity depends
on the relation between that field and the scientific variable. The
retained text-and-causal-inference review discusses text used for adjustment;
it does not prove that a modern embedding removes confounding.
\citep{exec13_census_text_review_arxiv}
Compare any proposed extraction with a documented coding rule and
independently labelled examples, preserving missing and ambiguous cases.
The observation journey shows why high predictive accuracy or apparent
completeness is insufficient. Begin with auditable field definitions and
retrieval assistance; an autonomous scientific measurement claim remains
unsupported without independent validation.


~~~
After:
~~~text
A useful formulation states the intervention and its alternative, the
population, outcome, horizon and information date, and the choices that remain
unresolved. The retained Kıcıman and causal-learn+ discussions motivate a
prototype for question clarification and graph critique; they cannot establish
the truth of a bank's assumptions. A structured questionnaire completed by an
economist is therefore a serious comparator. The relevant outcomes are material
omissions in the final question and the time needed to correct them. In the
deposit example, assignment, receipt, acceptance and price define different
questions. A fluent response that silently substitutes one for another fails
this comparison. \citep{v12_la_2305_00050,v12_la_2606_23608}

Variable construction introduces a further question: does an extracted field
measure the scientific variable? The retained review of text-based adjustment
explains relevant uses of text, but supplies no general guarantee that an
embedding removes confounding. A documented coding rule and independently
labelled examples provide a comparison for the proposed extraction, including
missing and ambiguous cases. The observation journey explains why predictive
accuracy and apparent completeness alone cannot establish measurement validity.
Auditable field definitions and retrieval assistance are justified starting
points; autonomous scientific measurement requires independent validation of
the relation between the field and the variable. \citep{exec13_census_text_review_arxiv}


~~~

docs/release/2026-10-29/source/docs/writing-repair/2026-10-29/assistance.tex

Replace instruction sequences or acquisition chronology with connected scientific and economic explanation.

Before:
~~~text
For diagram work, require evidence and timing for each proposed relationship
and retain competing explanations. LACR's reported retrieval effects vary
across its examples. The retained inspection records both improvement and
deterioration, including the ASIA skeleton comparison and the SACHS
comparison when PC output is added. This is evidence for evaluating the
retrieval choice, rather than assuming that more context improves the graph.
\citep{v12_la_2402_15301}
Compare the assistant with ordinary search plus an economist and, where
its assumptions fit, a numerical discovery procedure. The inputs must
be matched. Assess false orientations, missed common causes and the
resulting effect or action, rather than relying solely on edge counts.

The first assistant should propose a diagram and competing explanations
for review, then call a named discovery or identification method under
explicit assumptions. Numerical discovery contributes its own evidence;
textual plausibility cannot turn an unresolved equivalence class into
a validated graph. The three-model pricing example is a useful adverse
case because one unjustified orientation changes the financial sign.


~~~
After:
~~~text
A proposed diagram is useful when each relationship has an interpretation,
an information date and supporting evidence, and competing explanations remain
visible. LACR's retrieval results show why the choice of evidence matters: the
retained study reports both improvement and deterioration, including the ASIA
skeleton comparison and the SACHS comparison after adding PC output. More
context is therefore a candidate design choice whose consequences need
measurement. \citep{v12_la_2402_15301}

Ordinary search by an economist and an applicable numerical discovery method
are substantive alternatives. A comparison with matched inputs should follow
false orientations and missed common causes through to the estimated effect
or chosen action. Edge counts alone conceal that consequence. The first
assisted workflow can propose a diagram and its alternatives for review, then
invoke a named discovery or identification method under explicit assumptions.
Numerical discovery contributes evidence within those assumptions; textual
plausibility cannot resolve directions that the observations leave equivalent.
In the three-model pricing example, one unjustified orientation is enough to
reverse the financial sign.
~~~

## Edit 179
docs/release/2026-10-29/source/docs/writing-repair/2026-10-29/assistance.tex

Replace instruction sequences or acquisition chronology with connected scientific and economic explanation.

Substantive destination: CLadder supplied-mechanism scope, formal identification comparator, justified refusal, failures and uncertainty denominator, four-case interface scope, scripted baseline, request/correction costs, deterministic reporting baseline, zero-crossing adverse case and absence of measured live-model benefit remain.

Before:
~~~text
CLadder evaluates formal causal questions under constructed mechanisms;
its reported results concern historical models and that supplied task.
It does not measure elicitation of a new bank mechanism.
\citep{v12_la_2312_04350}
For identification, compare assistance with a defined formal procedure,
requiring the assistant to preserve both the answer and its conditions.
An appropriate refusal must explain the missing restriction, not simply
return an empty result. The benchmark literature and the retained
mechanism-clustering critiques give reasons to keep failed cases and
uncertainty denominators in the evaluation.

For execution, the existing local interface study shows equality for four
constructed requests, not model-driven analysis quality. A scripted
workflow is the credible baseline. Compare any assistant on request
construction, faithful tool use, errors detected and correction effort.
Do not give it credit for a calculation performed by the formal tool
while omitting the costs of choosing or correcting the request.

For reporting, a deterministic template is a strong comparator because
it can already state the estimand, assumptions, interval and scope.
The assistant's proposed value is explaining why the result changes a
decision or why it cannot yet support one. Check that explanation
against the actual result and the retained sources. A report that turns
a set crossing zero into a positive recommendation fails even if its
prose is persuasive. No local live-model evaluation establishes an
incremental benefit for this reporting task.


~~~
After:
~~~text
CLadder evaluates formal causal questions under constructed mechanisms. Its
historical model results concern a supplied task rather than the elicitation
of a new bank mechanism. A defined formal procedure provides the identification
comparator: an assisted answer must preserve both the conclusion and the
conditions under which it follows. When identification fails, an explanation
of the missing restriction is useful; an unexplained empty response is not.
The benchmark literature and retained mechanism-clustering critiques also
justify retaining failed cases and the full uncertainty denominator.
\citep{v12_la_2312_04350}

For execution, the local interface study establishes equality for four
constructed requests. Assessing assistance requires a further comparison with
a scripted workflow, because choosing the request and correcting mistakes are
part of the analytical work. Request construction, faithful tool use, detected
errors and correction effort are the relevant outcomes. The formal tool's
calculation cannot by itself establish the assistant's contribution.

A deterministic reporting template is another strong comparator. It already
states the estimand, assumptions, interval and scope. The proposed additional
value is an explanation of why the result changes a decision, or why it
cannot yet support one, that remains faithful to the result and its sources.
An explanation that promotes a set crossing zero into a positive recommendation
is substantively wrong. No local live-model evaluation yet establishes an
incremental benefit for this reporting task.


~~~

## Edit 184
docs/release/2026-10-29/source/docs/discovery-repair/2026-10-29/chapter.tex

Integrate completed finite-sample discovery and information-clock follow-up, with all90 result cells and exact original figure.

Substantive destination: Existing exact-oracle treatment remains complete. Its final observation bridge moves after the new sampling treatment; all prior scientific content and negative findings survive.

Before:
~~~text
 The next chapter asks a prior question for any such search: whether the recorded variables and customer identities faithfully represent the economic observations whose relationships are being learned.
~~~
After:
~~~text
 The following sampling comparison addresses one of these separate questions.

\section{A complete search can still give an incomplete lending comparison}
\label{closure30:sampling}

A bank can remove an unsound computational shortcut and still obtain an
unreliable effect estimate. The preceding exact-oracle comparison establishes
what the search does when every independence answer is correct. Actual records
offer estimates of those answers. A relationship that is weak after adjustment
may disappear from an estimated graph even when its economic contribution is
substantial. If that disappearance then removes a confounder from a lending
comparison, a narrow confidence interval can surround the wrong quantity.

This distinction motivates a second, deliberately limited study. It asks how
sampling error in the repaired search can affect the controls used to compare
rate concessions and balance responses. It also asks whether the bank's
information clock helps: should a score produced after the balance response
enter the discovery exercise in the first place? The examples are constructed
Gaussian economies with known equations. Their role is to expose a failure
mechanism and evaluate one response, not to estimate its prevalence in banks.

\subsection{An independence test is not a confidence statement about the graph}

For centred Gaussian variables, conditional independence between $X_i$ and
$X_j$ given $X_S$ is equivalent to a zero partial correlation
$\rho_{ij\mid S}$. The sample test used here follows the Fisher transformation
in \citet[Section 2.2.2, equation (3)]{kalisch2007}:
\begin{equation}
 Z_{ij\mid S}=\frac12\log\frac{1+\widehat\rho_{ij\mid S}}
                                  {1-\widehat\rho_{ij\mid S}},\qquad
 T_{ij\mid S}=\sqrt{n-|S|-3}\,|Z_{ij\mid S}|.
 \label{closure30:fisher}
\end{equation}
The search treats the pair as independent when
$T_{ij\mid S}\leq\Phi^{-1}(1-\alpha/2)$, where $n$ is the number of
observations, $|S|$ is the number of conditioning variables and $\Phi$ is the
standard normal distribution function. Failure to reject a zero correlation
is operationally used to delete the candidate link. It is not proof that the
population correlation is zero.

The same source's consistency result requires more than a large sample. Its
assumptions include a faithful Gaussian law, graph sparsity and bounds on
nonzero partial correlations; the asymptotic significance sequence depends on
an unknown lower signal bound. These conditions do not supply a finite-sample
guarantee at a fixed threshold. \citep[Assumptions A1--A4 and Theorem 1,
pp.~619--620]{kalisch2007}

The stable search stores each variable's neighbours at the beginning of a
conditioning depth, so deleting one link does not change the other candidate
sets examined at that depth. This is the central skeleton modification in
\citet[Section 4.1, Algorithm 4.1 and Theorem 3]{exec13_latentr_colombo2014}. It removes
one source of dependence on variable order. It does not make a noisy test
correct. The project comparison retains this level-frozen search and evaluates
all distinct candidate sets, rather than claiming a byte-for-byte reproduction
of a released software package.

For a small nonzero $\rho$, the approximate signal is
\[
 \sqrt{n-|S|-3}|\operatorname{atanh}(\rho)|.
\]
At a five per cent two-sided threshold, equating that scale to 1.96 gives
\begin{equation}
 n\approx |S|+3+
 \left\{\frac{1.96}{|\operatorname{atanh}(\rho)|}\right\}^{2}.
 \label{closure30:signal-scale}
\end{equation}
This is a signal-to-threshold calculation, not a power formula or a sample-size
recommendation. A correlation of about 0.00433 makes its last term approximately
205,000; a correlation of about 0.0289 makes it about 4,595. Large coefficients
elsewhere in a system do not ensure that every conditional comparison has a
large signal.

\subsection{Three economies with known intervention effects}

Let $Z=(Z_1,Z_2,Z_3)^\top$ denote independent baseline characteristics,
$X$ a rate concession and $Y$ the subsequent balance response. Let $C$ be a
later summary. The first two mechanisms are
\begin{align}
 X&=s\left(Z_1+\frac45Z_2+\frac65Z_3\right)+\varepsilon_X,\\
 Y&=s\left(\frac7{10}Z_1+\frac{11}{10}Z_2+
                 \frac9{10}Z_3\right)+\varepsilon_Y,\\
 C&=\frac{11}{10}X+\frac35Y+\varepsilon_C,
 \label{closure30:sampling-economy}
\end{align}
where the six exogenous inputs are mutually independent standard normal
variables. The larger-coefficient case has $s=1$; the smaller-coefficient case
has $s=1/8$. These are standardized model units. Neither coefficient choice is
an estimate for an institution. Changing $X$ leaves the structural equation
for $Y$ unchanged, so the intervention effect is zero in both cases.

The unadjusted population slope is
\begin{equation}
 \frac{\operatorname{Cov}(X,Y)}{\operatorname{Var}(X)}
 =\frac{(133/50)s^2}{1+(77/25)s^2}.
 \label{closure30:omitted-slope}
\end{equation}
It equals $133/204\simeq0.652$ when $s=1$ and
$133/3354\simeq0.0397$ when $s=1/8$. Correct adjustment for all three baseline
variables makes the slope zero. Including $C$ after that adjustment introduces
a different error. The residuals satisfy
$C^*=(11/10)\varepsilon_X+(3/5)\varepsilon_Y+\varepsilon_C$, giving the
overadjusted slope
\begin{equation}
 -\frac{(11/10)(3/5)}{1+(3/5)^2}=-\frac{33}{68}\simeq-0.485.
 \label{closure30:later-slope}
\end{equation}
These population calculations explain the directions of the simulation errors.
They also show why extra precision cannot compensate for an invalid comparison.

Both six-variable distributions are faithful to their stipulated graphs:
exact rational conditional covariances agree with graph separation for every
pair and conditioning set, 240 queries per mechanism. Faithfulness nevertheless
allows very small nonzero correlations. In the larger-coefficient case,
$\rho_{Z_1,Y\mid Z_3,C}\simeq-0.00433$, although $Z_1$ directly enters the
equation for $Y$. The later score nearly cancels the remaining association.
The larger coefficients therefore do not imply uniform strength of every
discovery query.

The third mechanism makes the distinction exact rather than approximate:
\begin{equation}
 Z=\varepsilon_Z,\qquad X=Z+\varepsilon_X,\qquad
 Y=X-Z+\varepsilon_Y=\varepsilon_X+\varepsilon_Y.
 \label{closure30:cancellation}
\end{equation}
Its intervention effect is one, but $Z$ and $Y$ are marginally independent.
The direct and indirect contributions cancel. Its unadjusted slope is $1/2$,
while adjustment for $Z$ recovers one. This distribution is unfaithful to the
three-arrow graph. It is included as an explicit assumption failure, not as
evidence against a theorem that assumes faithfulness.

\subsection{What is compared on the same observations}

For each mechanism and each sample size $n\in\{100,500,2000\}$, the study
generates 200 independent samples. It applies thresholds $\alpha=0.01$ and
$0.05$ to three searches: full stable subsets, the all-neighbour dependence
screen and a depth-two cap. This gives 1,800 datasets and 10,800 search results.
The methods receive identical observations within each comparison. The depth
cap keeps its unfinished-search flag whenever relevant larger sets remain.

To reveal the consequence of graph errors, an explicitly naive rule chooses
baseline variables that are adjacent to both $X$ and $Y$ in the estimated
skeleton. It then regresses $Y$ on $X$ and those selected variables using the
same observations, reporting an ordinary 95 per cent Student-$t$ interval for
the coefficient on $X$. The later score $C$ is always excluded from this
regression by its information date. This rule is a diagnostic comparator;
common estimated neighbours are not asserted to be a valid general adjustment
criterion. The reference regression uses every stipulated baseline cause.

Coverage means the proportion of the 200 intervals that contain the known
intervention effect. It is distinct from graph recovery. A graph can be wrong
in an economically irrelevant edge while the selected adjustment is correct,
or it can lose exactly the variable needed to remove confounding. The study
does not isolate the pure penalty from reusing observations after selection:
its interval failures combine selection, omitted confounders and inappropriate
confidence in the resulting regression. Splitting the sample would not by
itself make an invalid adjustment set valid.

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering\small
\begin{tabular}{lrrrrr}
\toprule
Mechanism & $n$ & Wrong graphs & Selected coverage & Baseline coverage & Bias\\
\midrule
Larger coefficients &100&200/200&0.0\%&93.0\%&0.650\\
 &500&200/200&0.0\%&94.0\%&0.644\\
 &2000&200/200&0.0\%&95.5\%&0.635\\
Smaller coefficients &100&200/200&93.0\%&95.5\%&0.032\\
 &500&200/200&86.0\%&93.0\%&0.034\\
 &2000&191/200&81.5\%&97.0\%&0.023\\
Exact cancellation &100&187/200&6.5\%&94.5\%&$-0.468$\\
 &500&188/200&6.0\%&95.5\%&$-0.470$\\
 &2000&189/200&5.5\%&93.5\%&$-0.471$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{Full stable search at $\alpha=0.05$. Wrong graphs compares the
undirected skeleton with the stipulated graph; the cancellation case violates
faithfulness. Selected coverage uses the naive estimated-common-neighbour
rule. Baseline coverage uses all stipulated baseline causes. Bias is the
mean selected estimate minus the true effect, in model units. These are
descriptive simulation results, not bank performance estimates.}
\label{closure30:sampling-results}
\end{table}

The smaller-coefficient case illustrates how more observations can expose
rather than remove a comparison error. The selected estimator's bias remains
positive while its intervals narrow. Coverage falls from 93 to 81.5 per cent.
For the latter proportion, a descriptive 95 per cent Wilson Monte Carlo
interval is 75.5--86.3 per cent. It quantifies variation over the declared
independent simulation draws; it is not an interval for performance across
unknown banking environments.

In the larger-coefficient case, the selected rule almost always omits important
causes. At $n=2000$ the full search selects no baseline variable in 145 of 200
samples and selects only $Z_3$ in another 54. No interval contains the zero
effect. The ordinary all-baseline regression covers zero in 191 of those same
200 samples. The failure is therefore consequential for the lending contrast,
not simply a disagreement over an incidental arrow.

The screen and depth cap do not solve this sampling problem. At $n=2000$ in
the larger-coefficient case, full search retains the spurious $X$--$Y$ link in
193 samples; the screened and capped searches retain it in all 200. The depth
cap labels every one of these searches unfinished. Full search has completed
its declared tests, but sampling error still prevents correct recovery.
Completion and correctness are separate statements.

The stricter threshold does not provide reassurance. At $\alpha=0.01$, full
search in the smaller-coefficient case with $n=2000$ has only 67.0 per cent
interval coverage, compared with 81.5 per cent at $\alpha=0.05$. A smaller
significance level makes it easier to fail to reject a zero partial correlation and
delete a true link. The complete comparison below retains both thresholds;
neither should be chosen retrospectively to make this example look favorable.

\subsection{The information clock helps, but does not guarantee recovery}

After inspecting those results, a separately specified follow-up changes one
thing: it excludes $C$ from the discovery exercise as well as from the outcome
regression. Coefficients, sample sizes, thresholds, seeds and observations are
unchanged. Removing this particular node does not conceal a common cause:
it is only a later descendant of $X$ and $Y$. The reduced five-variable
covariances are verified against the corresponding graph. The cancellation
case contains no such node and is not rerun.

For the smaller-coefficient case at $n=2000$ and $\alpha=0.05$, full-search
coverage rises from 81.5 to 97.0 per cent. The paired difference is 15.5
percentage points, with a Monte Carlo standard error of 2.66 points. The
reference set of all three causes is selected in 188 samples. Exact skeleton
recovery rises from nine out of 200 for the six-variable task to 173 out of
200 for the five-variable task. The graph tasks differ because the latter
omits $C$; retained paired records also compare the common five-variable edges.

The larger-coefficient case improves much less. At $n=2000$, interval coverage
is still only 3.5 per cent and the reduced skeleton is wrong in 194 samples.
Even after removing $C$, conditioning on other variables leaves some direct-edge
correlations close to zero. Correct timing is useful economic information; it
does not guarantee a favorable statistical signal or establish faithfulness.

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=.78\textwidth]{twelve-hour-closure/2026-10-29/sampling-figures/coverage.pdf}
\caption{Coverage of ordinary 95 per cent effect intervals under full search,
$\alpha=0.05$, and 200 samples per point. Error bars are descriptive 95 per cent
Wilson Monte Carlo intervals. The green reference uses all stipulated baseline
causes; the blue follow-up excludes the later score from discovery. The dashed
line marks nominal coverage. These examples demonstrate specific failure and
repair mechanisms, not a general ranking of discovery methods.}
\label{closure30:coverage-figure}
\end{figure}

\subsection{What the proposal should require before valuing a graph}

The study supports a conditional recommendation. Keep full subset search as a
logical reference and report unfinished searches honestly. Also retain the
economically defended information clock, assess the signals required by the
discovery procedure, and justify the adjustment used for the particular lending
contrast. A graph can help formulate questions without supplying a qualified
effect estimate. A significance setting such as 0.05 should never be presented
as a 95 per cent probability that the entire graph is correct.

The numerical checks target the study itself. Exact rational covariance and
independently implemented graph/path tests agree on every declared population
query in the faithful cases. Precision-matrix and residual-regression partial
correlations agree within $5\times10^{-15}$ in 486 sample checks. Independent
QR residualization reproduces 270 retained interval calculations within
$7\times10^{-16}$; reversing variable order preserves the checked full-search
skeletons. These checks reduce implementation error in the examples. They
cannot establish a universal calibration guarantee.

All 54 first-study method/threshold cells and all 36 timing-follow-up cells
are retained, including adverse results. The follow-up was specified after
the first study and is described as such. Neither experiment evaluates native
R or Java software, arrow orientation, latent confounding, a live language
model or an actual bank. Those questions retain their own tests. For the
proposal, the scientific advance is narrower and useful: repairing the search
does not complete the causal comparison, and timing information can change
its reliability without making it universally reliable.

percentage of ordinary selected-regression intervals containing the stipulated
~~~

## Edit 185
docs/release/2026-10-29/source/docs/writing-repair/2026-10-29/src/5e1bb6d4d921349d.tex

Before:
~~~text
For the bank, the investment conclusion follows from the retained example in
~~~
After:
~~~text
\subsection{Retaining the alternative changes interval coverage}

The two Gaussian mechanisms in Section~\ref{exec20:expert-identification}
make it possible to examine this distinction with a known observation law.
The forward mechanism has intervention effect $3/5$ and the reverse mechanism
has effect zero, while both generate the same standardized joint distribution.
Their ordering is deliberately unresolved; this construction does not reverse
an action and an outcome whose temporal order is known.

A retained project experiment draws 2,000 independent datasets of 500
observations from that common Gaussian law. In each dataset, regression of
the outcome on an intercept and the candidate cause gives an ordinary 95\%
Student interval for the slope. Gaussian independent observations and the
correct linear conditional mean justify that slope interval. It is also an
intervention interval in the forward mechanism. Under the reverse mechanism,
the same calculation estimates a conditional association rather than the
intervention effect.

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering\small
\begin{tabular}{lrr}
\toprule
Returned set & Forward-world coverage & Reverse-world coverage\\
\midrule
Slope interval & 95.7\% & 0.0\%\\
Slope interval together with zero & 95.7\% & 100.0\%\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{One observation law and two causal interpretations. Each percentage
uses all 2,000 retained replications, each with 500 observations. The second
set retains both declared mechanisms. This is a generated example, not a
reproduction of neural-flow training or measured banking performance.}
\label{rel69:graph-coverage}
\end{table}

The slope interval covers the forward effect in 1,914 of the 2,000 datasets
and covers the reverse effect in none. Its mean width is 0.1411. The Monte
Carlo standard error of the 95.7\% coverage estimate is 0.45 percentage points;
the observed proportion does not establish improvement over nominal 95\%
coverage. First, middle and last interval calculations were independently
checked by QR decomposition in the original study. Reaggregating all retained
intervals reproduces the counts and width. This checks the stated experiment;
it supplies no calibration result for a fitted neural ensemble.

Adding zero to each slope interval produces a confidence set for the fixed
two-mechanism class. Its forward-world coverage is unchanged, and its
reverse-world coverage is one by construction. The set need not be a single
interval. Taking its convex hull would preserve coverage but obscure the
gap between the two retained causal possibilities.

This is a concrete instance of the graph-retention decomposition in
equation~\eqref{eq:la03-coverage-decomposition}. The true member is retained
with certainty because the example fixes the class to contain both mechanisms.
The forward mechanism contributes its justified regression interval; the
reverse contributes the exact zero effect. For a learned graph class, the
probability of discarding the true member must instead be controlled, and
effect inference must remain valid under the actual selection procedure.
Simultaneous inference over a fixed candidate family or an independent
estimation sample can address particular selection problems. Neither repairs
a false model or supplies missing identification. An unidentified retained
mechanism must contribute justified bounds or an uninformative set; removing
it because it widens the answer introduces an additional error.

The monetary consequence is already visible in the stated USD~100 valuation
and USD~40 intervention cost: the compatible net values are USD~20 and
minus USD~40. More precise estimation of their shared observational law does
not choose between them. A verified information clock, suitable assignment
evidence or a defensible structural restriction can change that conclusion.
The experiment therefore supports an ambiguity-preserving comparison while
leaving general graph-class coverage and bank validation open.

For the bank, the investment conclusion follows from the retained example in
~~~

## Edit 187
docs/release/2026-10-29/source/docs/writing-repair/2026-10-29/src/1de93e537048440f.tex

Integrate completed missing-history recovery, independent-pilot and equal-resource studies, preserving all adverse findings and mathematical conditions.

Substantive destination: All prior chapter14 source survives verbatim. New section develops the previously proposed outcome-audit response and retains its full theory, experiments, costs and limits. Five imported decimal transcriptions are corrected against retained records and documented separately.

Before:
~~~text
sampling argument and measurement evidence.

~~~
After:
~~~text
sampling argument and measurement evidence.

\section{Buying the information that missing customers take away}
\label{closure30:outcome-audit}

A bank cannot recover the experience of customers who disappear merely by
fitting a more elaborate model to those who remain. It can sometimes change
the evidence. A sample of incomplete customer histories may be recovered from
archived servicing records, linked payments or carefully conducted follow-up.
The economic question is how much reliable information that extra work buys.

The preceding analysis shows why resampling a fitted model does not correct
outcome-dependent dropout. This section changes the observation design while
retaining its three-decision servicing example.
Random recovery repairs the missing-information problem under a clear
condition: the histories actually recovered must form the specified random
sample. Random invitations are insufficient if successful recovery remains
selective. Even a correct estimate can be too imprecise to support a decision.
Here a separately fitted outcome model reduces interval width substantially,
but the additional pilot information is costly and a positive policy
improvement often remains statistically unresolved.

\subsection{A known design, applied to a sequential decision}
Following up a subsample with missing outcomes is usually called double
sampling. Levis and colleagues show how noninformative successful second-stage
recovery identifies the full-data distribution despite informative original
missingness. Their Proposition~1 treats a missing outcome; Appendix~A extends
the argument to general coarsening of a record. Their mean-effect estimators
also use regression to improve efficiency under explicit conditions
\citep{levis2023double}.

This changes the information without exempting the analysis from selection
assumptions. Levis and colleagues distinguish invitations from obtaining the
missing information, and discuss nonresponse and recall error. Sun and
colleagues develop a related construction for weighted quantile treatment
effects, with conditions appropriate to quantiles \citep{sun2025double}.
A mean-reward comparison does not thereby qualify a tail-loss estimate.
Averaging conditional quantile effects does not in general recover a
population quantile effect.

The present contribution is a worked composition: recover missing pieces of
a sequential policy-value score, derive the precision consequence, and test
it in the servicing law. The two-phase idea and sequential regression
assistance are inherited methods. This does not establish an efficient
estimator for every longitudinal missing-data model.

\subsection{What must be recovered}
There are three decisions, $t=0,1,2$. Each generated customer has a fixed
characteristic $X\sim N(0,1)$ and initial state
$L_0=0.4X+\varepsilon_0$. With independent standard-normal innovations,
\begin{align*}
L_{t+1}&=0.6L_t+0.25X-0.5A_t+\varepsilon_{t+1},\\
R_t&=10-2L_{t+1}^{2}-A_t+0.5A_tX.
\end{align*}
The historical probability of action is
\[
p_t=\operatorname{clip}\{\operatorname{expit}(0.3X-0.5L_t+0.2A_{t-1}),
                       0.2,0.8\},\qquad A_{-1}=0.
\]
These are known probabilities in a generated example. Clipping belongs to
the assignment law, rather than modifying an estimated bank propensity
score. A fixed policy acting when $L_t>0.5$ is compared with never acting.
Neither is selected using evaluation outcomes.

For history $H_t$ available before action $t$, define
\[
W_t(\pi)=\prod_{s=0}^{t}
 \frac{\mathbf1\{A_s=\pi(H_s)\}}{p_s(A_s\mid H_s)},
\qquad F(\pi)=\sum_{t=0}^{2}W_t(\pi)R_t.
\]
Sequential exchangeability, consistency and positive action probabilities
give $E\{F(\pi)\}=V(\pi)$, where
$V(\pi)=E_\pi(\sum_t R_t)$. The simulation satisfies these conditions by
construction. Recovering missing records cannot make them true in a bank.

Let $C_t$ indicate that reward $t$ and its required history were routinely
observed. After dropout, later $C_t$ are zero. Split the full score:
\[
K(\pi)=\sum_t C_tW_t(\pi)R_t,\qquad
M(\pi)=\sum_t(1-C_t)W_t(\pi)R_t,\qquad F=K+M.
\]
The missing contribution may depend on unobserved outcomes. Among incomplete
records, let $B$ indicate completed recovery of the missing history, with
known probability $q>0$, independently of that history and its original
observation pattern. Complete records need no recovery and have $M=0$.
Average the following score over all initially eligible customers:
\begin{equation}
Z(\pi)=K(\pi)+\frac{B}{q}M(\pi).
\label{closure30:audit-score}
\end{equation}
Each routinely observed contribution has weight one; each recovered missing
contribution represents $1/q$ similar incomplete histories. There is no
division by the questionable routine observation probability.

Condition on the full history and routine observation pattern.
$E(B\mid\text{history},C)=q$ for incomplete records, so
$E(Z\mid\text{history},C)=F$ and $E(Z)=V(\pi)$. This requires recovery of
the quantities entering $M$: actions, states, rewards and the information
needed for assignment probabilities. A terminal balance alone may not
supply them. Recovery must measure historical outcomes faithfully and must
not change them. A follow-up intervention that changes repayment would
answer a different question.

\subsection{Identification does not imply useful precision}
Under constant independent Bernoulli recovery,
\begin{equation}
\operatorname{Var}(Z)=\operatorname{Var}(F)
                   +(q^{-1}-1)E(M^2).
\label{closure30:audit-variance}
\end{equation}
Indeed $Z-F=(B/q-1)M$ has conditional mean zero and conditional variance
$(q^{-1}-1)M^2$, so its covariance with $F$ is zero. For policy improvement,
use paired differences $F_\Delta=F(\pi)-F(0)$ and
$M_\Delta=M(\pi)-M(0)$. Evaluating both policies on the same customers
retains this pairing.

Low recovery probabilities magnify rare contributions. Incomplete customers
who match several consecutive policy actions can receive large weights.
More recovery reduces the extra variance; recovering every history still
leaves the variance of the full-history policy score.

The first routine observation mechanism continues with probability
$g_t=0.65+0.30\operatorname{expit}(0.3L_t-0.2A_t)$, independently of the
next innovation conditional on history and action. The adverse mechanism
uses $\operatorname{expit}\{\operatorname{logit}(g_t)+1.5\varepsilon_{t+1}\}$.
Weighting by the original $g_t$ in that case assumes away selection by the
unseen shock.

Each mechanism has 500 independent replications with 2,000 customers.
Recovery rates are 5\%, 10\%, 25\%, 50\% and 100\% of incomplete records,
with shared recovery draws across rates within a replication. Estimators
receive full histories only where routine observation or designated recovery
permits. The reference policy improvement is 0.75258 reward units, with
numerical Monte Carlo standard error 0.00432, from the retained
million-trajectory intervention calculation. Reward units are illustrative,
not dollars.

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering\small
\begin{tabular}{lrrrr}
\toprule
Adverse dropout & Bias & Coverage & Width & Follow-ups\\
\midrule
Assumed routine $g_t$ & 0.755 & 0.908 & 4.506 & 0\\
Random recovery, 5\% & -0.129 & 0.944 & 11.809 & 62\\
Random recovery, 25\% & -0.052 & 0.948 & 6.047 & 308\\
Random recovery, 50\% & 0.016 & 0.930 & 4.772 & 618\\
Random recovery, 100\% & 0.029 & 0.942 & 3.933 & 1,238\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{Fixed-policy improvement under outcome-dependent dropout;
500 replications, 2,000 evaluation customers. Width is the mean width of a
normal 95\% interval. Follow-ups are completed missing-history recoveries.
No pilot is used. Coverage is against the numerical reference, not a
finite-sample guarantee.}
\end{table}

The estimated bias at 25\% recovery is small relative to its Monte Carlo
standard error, 0.070. Yet mean interval width is about eight times the
improvement. Even full recovery yields a positive lower bound in only
11.6\% of replications. Under conditionally independent routine dropout,
25\% recovery gives width 5.679 and coverage 93.0\%. These results do not
establish uniform attainment of nominal coverage.

A third study makes actual recovery depend on the unseen first-missed
innovation, using
$\operatorname{expit}\{\operatorname{logit}(0.25)+0.8\varepsilon_{\rm miss}\}$,
but analyses it as constant 25\% recovery. Across 500 replications, upward
bias is 0.278 with Monte Carlo standard error 0.0625. Coverage nevertheless
appears to be 95.2\%, because mean width is 5.219. A nominal-looking
coverage fraction alone does not validate the design or exclude bias.

\subsection{Use prediction to economise on information}
Repeated weighting expends information on action randomness as well as
reward uncertainty. A model of future reward can remove part of this
variation. The sequential doubly robust construction of
\citet{jiang2016}, equation~(10) and Theorem~1, makes this distinction
precise. Here the model is fitted on a separate pilot and action
probabilities remain known. Unbiasedness does not require the fitted
outcome model to be correct.

For fixed pilot regressions $\widehat Q_t(H_t,A_t)$, put
$\widehat V_t(H_t)=\widehat Q_t\{H_t,\pi(H_t)\}$ and
$\widehat V_3=0$. The full-history score is
\begin{equation}
D(\pi)=\widehat V_0(H_0)+
 \sum_{t=0}^{2}W_t(\pi)
 \{R_t+\widehat V_{t+1}(H_{t+1})-\widehat Q_t(H_t,A_t)\}.
\label{closure30:sequential-score}
\end{equation}
Condition on the pilot. With $W_{-1}=1$, random assignment gives
\[
E\{W_t\widehat Q_t(H_t,A_t)\}
  =E\{W_{t-1}\widehat V_t(H_t)\}.
\]
Model terms cancel in expectation, leaving
$E(D\mid\text{pilot})=V(\pi)$. This telescoping argument covers the present
reward and transition dependence without importing a variance formula under
unstated assumptions. Setting every $\widehat Q_t$ to zero returns the
reward-weighted score.

Apply recovery to the residual contributions. Initial $\widehat V_0$ is
observed for everyone and belongs in $K_D$; split the residual sum into
routinely observed $K_D$ and missing $M_D$. Then $Z_D=K_D+B M_D/q$ has
conditional mean $D$ and
\[
\operatorname{Var}(Z_D\mid\text{pilot})
=\operatorname{Var}(D\mid\text{pilot})
 +(q^{-1}-1)E(M_D^2\mid\text{pilot}).
\]
Good predictions can reduce both terms. A wrong but fixed square-integrable
model does not invalidate the mean identity when the probabilities are
correct. Reusing evaluation outcomes for fitting would require a different
argument, such as a justified sample-splitting procedure.

\subsection{The precision improvement and its price}
A second experiment uses 5,000 independent pilot customers per
replication and random 25\% recovery of incomplete pilot histories.
Working backward, separate regressions for each policy use the fixed basis
$(1,L,X,A,L^2,X^2,LX,AL,AX)$. Routine transitions receive weight one and
recovered transitions weight four. Another 2,000 customers evaluate the
scores. There are 500 replications per observation mechanism, with fresh
seeds. The model basis is fixed before final evaluation.

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering\small
\begin{tabular}{lrrrr}
\toprule
Adverse dropout & Bias & Coverage & Width & Positive lower\\
\midrule
Assumed $g_t$, assisted & 0.925 & 0.590 & 2.194 & 84.4\%\\
5\% recovery, assisted & 0.082 & 0.958 & 4.769 & 15.8\%\\
25\%, reward weighting & -0.079 & 0.962 & 6.094 & 4.4\%\\
25\%, constant model & -0.025 & 0.968 & 3.229 & 14.4\%\\
25\%, quadratic model & -0.003 & 0.964 & 2.773 & 24.0\%\\
50\%, quadratic model & 0.016 & 0.950 & 2.181 & 33.2\%\\
Full, quadratic model & 0.001 & 0.952 & 1.827 & 41.4\%\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{Fixed-policy improvement with an independent pilot. Rows share
evaluation samples and recovery draws. Assisted and constant-model rows
also use the 5,000-customer pilot. Positive lower denotes a positive
interval endpoint, not adoption. Reward weighting needs no pilot.}
\end{table}

At 25\% evaluation recovery, the quadratic model reduces width from 6.094
to 2.773, or 54.5\%. The paired difference is $-3.320$, with Monte Carlo
standard error 0.038. Bias is $-0.0026$ with Monte Carlo standard error
0.0323. Coverage of 96.4\% has Monte Carlo standard error 0.83 percentage
points. Under conditionally independent routine dropout, coverage is 95.2\%
and width 2.607. The crude constant model also remains centred within
simulation error, while its wider intervals show the cost of weaker
prediction.

The pilot is additional information. Under adverse dropout it needs about
775 completed recoveries on average, besides roughly 309 evaluation
recoveries at 25\%. The unassisted comparator needs only evaluation
recoveries. This is not an equal-total-budget comparison. Reusing a pilot
across policies might spread its cost, but that benefit must be measured.

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{twelve-hour-closure/2026-10-29/outcome-figures/precision.pdf}
\caption{More recovery narrows intervals, but positive evidence for a modest
gain remains scarce. Outcome-dependent dropout; 500 replications per study.
The assisted curve needs about 775 extra pilot recoveries, excluded from the
horizontal evaluation count. Curves use separate studies; the
paired 25\% comparison is in the text.}
\end{figure}

The false routine observation model with the assisted score produces a more
dangerous result: bias 0.925 and coverage 59.0\%, while 84.4\% of intervals
have a positive lower endpoint. Prediction sharpens a selected view of the
customers without correcting the observation mechanism.

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{twelve-hour-closure/2026-10-29/outcome-figures/bias-coverage.pdf}
\caption{The observation design controls bias; regression controls part of
the variance. Bars are ordinary 95\% Monte Carlo intervals across 500
replications, not simultaneous qualification bounds. Reference uncertainty
is separate. Full history is a diagnostic benchmark, not bank data.}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Compare the same collection budget}
The preceding precision gain uses extra pilot information. A fair resource
comparison gives every method the same 7,000 initial records and the exact
same randomly recovered histories, at a 25\% recovery rate. In each of 500
new replications, one method weights all records directly. Three others
reserve 5,000, 2,000 or 1,000 customers for fitting and use the remaining
customers for evaluation. The partitions are fixed before outcomes are
examined. All use the same quadratic regression basis and fixed policies.

A fifth method divides the cohort into two equal halves. It fits a model
on each half, evaluates that model on the other half, and averages all
held-out scores. Each customer contributes to evaluation, but its own
outcome is never used to fit the model applied to it. This cross-fitting
has a scientific condition, not just a computational advantage.

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering\small
\begin{tabular}{lrrrr}
\toprule
Adverse dropout & Bias & Coverage & Width & Positive lower\\
\midrule
Weight all 7,000 & 0.049 & 0.960 & 3.272 & 14.0\%\\
Pilot 5,000; evaluate 2,000 & 0.034 & 0.968 & 2.713 & 25.4\%\\
Pilot 2,000; evaluate 5,000 & -0.011 & 0.946 & 1.781 & 41.4\%\\
Pilot 1,000; evaluate 6,000 & 0.003 & 0.952 & 1.654 & 47.6\%\\
Two-fold cross-fitting & -0.010 & 0.944 & 1.512 & 53.8\%\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{Equal realised collection resources: every method receives the same
7,000 initial records and completed recoveries, averaging 1,086 histories
under adverse dropout. There are 500 new replications. These are selected
fixed allocations, not a search proving a globally optimal split.}
\end{table}

Reducing the pilot to 1,000 customers improves precision because more
customers then evaluate a model that is already useful. Cross-fitting has
the narrowest average interval among these declared alternatives. Relative
to weighting all records, its paired reduction in width is 1.761, with
Monte Carlo standard error 0.013. Estimated bias is $-0.0103$ with Monte
Carlo standard error 0.0181. Under conditionally independent routine
dropout, its width is 1.441 and coverage is 96.6\%; every method then uses
about 895 recovered histories on average.

The adverse-case coverage of 94.4\% has Monte Carlo standard error about
1.03 percentage points. Estimated variance averages 0.154, compared with
the empirical variance of estimates, 0.163. These finite-sample findings
remain visible rather than being rounded into a calibration guarantee.
Equal collection resources also do not mean identical operating costs:
fitting, administration and recovery quality still require measurement.

\subsection{Why cross-fitting has a conditional justification here}
Let $Z_Q$ be the recovered score for any fixed outcome regressions $Q$.
Known action and recovery probabilities give $E(Z_Q)=V(\pi)$ for every
such square-integrable $Q$. Suppose each fold's fitted model approaches a
common deterministic limit $Q^*$ in the sense
\[
\|Z_{\widehat Q_{-k}}-Z_{Q^*}\|_{L_2(P)}=o_p(1).
\]
There are a fixed number of folds, each containing a nonvanishing fraction
of the independent customers. Conditional on the other folds, the mean
difference on fold $k$ has mean zero and variance
$\|Z_{\widehat Q_{-k}}-Z_{Q^*}\|_{L_2(P)}^2/n_k$.
Conditional Chebyshev's inequality makes that difference
$o_p(n^{-1/2})$. The finitely many fold remainders need not be independent
for their weighted sum to be negligible. Thus
\[
\widehat V_{\rm cf}(\pi)
=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n} Z_{Q^*}(O_i)+o_p(n^{-1/2}).
\]
If the limiting score has finite, positive variance, the ordinary independent
sample central limit theorem applies. The same conditional argument makes
the empirical squared score differences vanish; Cauchy--Schwarz then shows
that pooled-score variance consistently estimates the limiting variance.
This justifies the normal approximation asymptotically under the stated
conditions, without claiming exact coverage in a finite sample.

The declared finite-dimensional regressions have a route to these conditions.
Action and recovery probabilities are bounded away from zero. The fixed
horizon, Gaussian states and polynomial rewards have the needed moments.
Positive state densities and both possible actions give nonsingular
population regression matrices. Weighted least squares converges backward
through the three decisions to deterministic projection coefficients, even
when the quadratic class is misspecified. Coefficient convergence and the
finite feature moments imply the score convergence above. A sample rank
check alone would not establish this population argument.

The exact mean identity is what makes this route relatively simple. If
treatment or recovery probabilities must be estimated, it no longer removes
all model error automatically. Products of estimation errors, dependent
customers, policy learning and selective completion require additional
arguments. Cross-fitting does not itself solve those problems.

\subsection{A collection budget is a design choice}
Let $r$ be the incomplete-record probability, $c_0$ the cost of an initial
record and $c_A$ the additional cost of a recovered history. Expected
evaluation cost is $n(c_0+rqc_A)$, plus pilot cost. For a fixed score let
$V_F=\operatorname{Var}(F_\Delta)$ and $B_M=E(M_\Delta^2)$. Estimator
variance is $(V_F-B_M+B_M/q)/n$.

With fixed $n$, more recovery always reduces the missing-information term.
With a fixed monetary budget, it can mean fewer initial customers. If
$A=V_F-B_M>0$, differentiating
$(A+B_M/q)(c_0+rqc_A)$ gives the unconstrained optimum
\[
q^*=\sqrt{\frac{B_Mc_0}{A r c_A}},
\]
restricted to $[q_{\min},1]$. If $A\leq0$, that expression decreases with
$q$ and favours $q=1$ within this cost model. This assumes constant costs
and a fixed trained score, not an optimal joint pilot/evaluation design.
No bank prices are supplied, so no numerical recovery fraction is recommended.

Another route should be separated from the tested regression. For information
$O$ available before recovery and a separately trained predictor $m(O)$ of
the missing contribution,
\[
Z_m=K+m(O)+\frac{B}{q(O)}\{M-m(O)\}
\]
is unbiased under the known conditional recovery design even when $m$ is
wrong. Set $m=0$ and $q=1$ for complete records, which need no recovery; the design
and allocation calculations concern incomplete records. Its extra variance is
$E[\{q(O)^{-1}-1\}\{M-m(O)\}^2]$.
The best squared-error predictor is $E(M\mid O)$. With known residual
second moment $a(O)=E[\{M-m(O)\}^2\mid O]$ and cost $c(O)>0$, minimising
this term at a fixed expected recovery budget yields
$q(O)\propto\sqrt{a(O)/c(O)}$, subject to positivity and the ceiling one.
This is a derivation, not a tested allocation algorithm. A separate pilot
must estimate the quantities; fresh data must test the design. Unseen
future outcomes cannot determine which customer is recovered.

\subsection{What this changes in the proposal}
A prototype can now be proposed around a specific design: accept declared
recovery probabilities, retain the initial customer denominator, estimate
paired policy improvements, and display the extra information burden and
remaining uncertainty. It should distinguish dropout weights from recovery
weights and show when an outcome model improves only precision. Where
recoverable histories do not exist, sensitivity or partial-identification
analysis is the appropriate next step.

These results concern independent generated customers, known action
probabilities and fixed policies. They do not settle calendar dependence,
unmeasured treatment confounding, erroneous or partially recovered histories,
estimated treatment probabilities, or uncertainty after policy learning.
Normal intervals are assessed rather than declared exact. The simulation
keeps every unaudited missing value unavailable to fitting and estimation.
The generated evidence does not establish that real histories can be
recovered accurately, or that the collection expense is worthwhile.

The remaining question is whether a feasible collection design can deliver
accurate histories in sufficient numbers to separate the economic
alternatives at an acceptable total cost.

~~~

docs/release/2026-10-29/source/docs/writing-repair/2026-10-29/investment.tex
