Substantive destination: Every prior chapter101 paragraph and nested input remains. Complete scientific treatment added, with generated-arrival wording corrected; source-specific exclusions and operating limits preserved.

Before:
~~~text
\section{A spending sequence with real alternatives}
~~~
After:
~~~text
\section{The cost of a useful answer}
\label{closure:energy-study}

A bank does not buy words by the thousand because it needs more words. It
commissions a study to decide whether a cheaper funding mix, a different
servicing policy or an additional piece of information is worth having.
The relevant unit of production is therefore a useful, independently checked
answer to a specified question. An answer may recommend no change. It may
also conclude, usefully, that the available evidence cannot distinguish the
alternatives. A fluent report that misses the question is wasted production,
however little electricity it consumes.

This distinction changes the investment calculation. A small model may
generate text cheaply but require more correction. A large batch may lower
energy per request while making the analyst wait. A power meter may report a
precise number without measuring the intended period or attributing the
consumption to the intended study. We examine these three problems in turn.
The argument uses inspected public sources, exact calculations and generated
arrival times. It does not report measured energy use by the proposed bank
service.

\subsection{What the public comparisons establish}

\citet{closureArgerich2024} measure CPU, memory, graphics processor and
storage consumption on a specified Linux machine. Their experiments compare
model families, model sizes, batching and numerical precision. The machine
has an Intel i9-9900K processor, 32\,GB of memory and an NVIDIA RTX\,4090
graphics processor. Their short-prompt experiment repeats the same input ten
times; their question-answering application separately examines preparation
and querying. These choices make the measurement boundaries visible. They
do not resemble a complete causal investigation with document retrieval,
alternative diagrams, identification checks, numerical estimation and
human review.

Two qualifications matter for a purchaser. First, the paper explicitly sets
accuracy aside in Section~VII. Its reported energy reductions therefore
cannot establish a lower cost per correct causal conclusion. Second, the
quantization experiments depend on the software used to execute the model.
Reducing numerical precision can save memory without proportionately reducing
latency or energy. The paper's findings do not warrant choosing a bank model
from its parameter count or nominal precision alone.

A seemingly closer comparator is the joint accuracy, energy and latency
study by \citet{closureHusom2025}. We inspected its April 2025 preprint and
public supporting code. The experiment uses a Raspberry Pi~4, a hardware
power meter and five general-purpose task collections. The prompts request
answers without explanations. This is already a different product from a
causal study whose assumptions and reasoning must be examined. More seriously,
the publicly supplied code introduces an unresolved correctness problem.
We exclude the source's accuracy and joint quality--energy rankings from our
recommendation pending reconstruction; the citation here explains that
exclusion.

The code for the 164 programming tasks passes each stored test to a general
evaluation routine. Every stored test defines a function named
\texttt{check(candidate)}, but none invokes it. The caller supplies the test
definition without adding the call for the task's named entry point. The
inspected evaluation routine concatenates the candidate program and the
supplied reference, then reports whether this combined program terminates
without an error. Defining a test function does not execute its assertions.
The relevant evaluator and task files are identical in the inspected
September 2026 revision and a February 2025 revision preceding the preprint
\citep{closureEdgeCode2026,closureCodeEval2022}.

An authored example makes the consequence concrete. Suppose a function must
return principal times an interest rate. One candidate performs the
multiplication; another always returns zero. Both terminate normally when
followed only by the definition of two test assertions. Calling the test
function then accepts the first and rejects the second. This checks the
logical defect without running downloaded code or any model-generated
program. A future reconstruction must invoke the assertions, include
deliberately wrong controls, and recompute each task's result from preserved
outputs. The static evidence does not establish which unrecorded manual
changes, if any, were used for the published results, nor does it show that
separately recorded power measurements are false.

The distinction between a named benchmark and its actual execution is
financially consequential. If an acceptance test silently rewards an answer
that was never checked, the measured success rate is too weak a basis for
pricing useful output. A bank should settle the definition and execution of
acceptance before comparing energy per accepted study. Public benchmarks
can inform that design. They cannot supply the bank's success probability
by substitution.

\subsection{What exactly did the meter measure?}

Power is a rate, measured in watts; energy is its integral over time,
measured in joules. A watt-hour is 3,600 joules. For observations $P_j$ at
strictly increasing times $t_j$, linear interpolation gives the familiar
trapezoidal calculation
\begin{equation}
 \widehat E=\sum_{j=1}^{m-1}
        (t_{j+1}-t_j)\frac{P_j+P_{j+1}}2.
 \label{closure:energy-trapezoid}
\end{equation}
Its units and time boundary are part of the claim. The formula is exact for
the chosen interpolation, not necessarily for the unobserved path of power
between samples. Missing readings must remain missing information.

The original EnergyMeter paper describes a mean-power calculation with a
default half-second GPU sampling interval. The inspected February 2026
software, version~2.0.2, instead uses timestamp-based trapezoidal integration
and a default interval of one tenth of a second. These are different
versions. We examine the later code as a possible measurement component;
we do not retrospectively apply its behavior to the earlier experiments
\citep{closureEnergyMeter2026}.

In the later code, excluding idle GPU observations removes samples whose
reported utilization is zero and then integrates across the timestamps that
remain. This can join active observations on either side of an idle period.
The elapsed gap remains in the integral even though its observations have
been removed. Consider the declared artificial trace in
Table~\ref{closure:energy-trace}. Linear interpolation over every observation
gives 330 joules. Setting idle observations to zero while retaining their
times gives 300 joules. Removing the idle observations before integrating
gives 600 joules: the calculation bridges from second~1 to second~5 at
100 watts.

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering\small
\caption{An authored trace exposes the effect of removing idle observations}
\label{closure:energy-trace}
\begin{tabular}{lrrrrrrr}
\toprule
Elapsed seconds & 0&1&2&3&4&5&6\\
Power, watts &100&100&10&10&10&100&100\\
Utilization, per cent &50&50&0&0&0&50&50\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

Nor is setting idle observations to zero the same as subtracting a measured
idle baseline. Subtracting an illustrative constant 10 watts throughout this
six-second trace gives $330-10\times6=270$ joules. The alternatives answer
different questions: total observed device consumption, consumption assigned
only to active samples under an interpolation rule, and consumption above
a stated baseline. None proves the incremental electricity attributable to
one study sharing a device with other work. Such attribution requires an
allocation rule or an appropriate comparison of operating conditions.

There are further boundary problems to resolve before integration. The
inspected software returns zero, with a warning, for certain unavailable
CPU or memory readings. No GPU readings, or a single retained GPU sample,
can also produce zero. These outcomes cannot all be treated as verified
zero consumption. Device activity and process-name-based disk tracking are
not unique study identities. Component measurement windows must also be
aligned before their totals are added. A small numerical result can thus
mean either low consumption or insufficient observation.

EnergyMeter has an inspected MIT licence and is a candidate for conditional
integration. The proposal is to retain raw timestamped readings, preserve
unavailable components explicitly, identify the common measurement window,
and calculate the declared total and baseline-adjusted quantities separately.
Any active-period calculation must state how interval boundaries are handled.
A later prototype must compare these quantities with an appropriate external
reference on its actual hardware. The present arithmetic supports these
requirements; it does not qualify a repaired native implementation.

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{twelve-hour-closure/2026-10-29/energy-figures/energy-waiting.pdf}
\caption{A cheaper reported unit can conceal a different measurement boundary.
Left: removing observations bridges an idle interval in an authored power
trace. Right: exact average collection delay for Poisson requests grouped
in fixed counts. Service time and queue backlog are excluded.}
\label{closure:energy-waiting}
\end{figure}

\subsection{The waiting cost of batching}

A batch of questions prepared overnight can be available before computation
begins. An analyst asking the first question of the morning is in a different
position. If the service waits for enough further requests to fill a batch,
an improvement in processing efficiency can be purchased with the analyst's
time. Energy per processed token does not record that price.

An exact calculation separates collection delay from processing time.
Assume requests arrive as a Poisson process of rate $\lambda$ per second.
Group consecutive arrivals in batches of $b$, and release a batch as soon as
its last request arrives. Ignore service capacity and any backlog for this
calculation. Let $X_j$ be the time between the $j$th and $(j+1)$st requests
within a batch. The $X_j$ are independent exponentials with mean
$1/\lambda$ and variance $1/\lambda^2$. The average waiting time in a batch
is
\begin{equation}
 \overline W_b=\frac1b\sum_{j=1}^{b-1}jX_j,
 \qquad
 E\overline W_b=\frac{b-1}{2\lambda},\qquad
 \operatorname{Var}(\overline W_b)=
 \frac{(b-1)(2b-1)}{6b\lambda^2}.
 \label{closure:batch-wait}
\end{equation}
Each gap contributes to the $j$ earlier requests that are waiting through
it. Summing these contributions proves the formula. The first request waits
$(b-1)/\lambda$ on average, twice the average across the whole batch;
the last waits zero. These are collection delays alone.

For illustration, at one request per minute a batch of 20 takes an average
of 9.5 minutes of collection waiting per customer, and 19 minutes for its
first request. At 60 requests per minute the corresponding averages are
9.5 and 19 seconds. The same batch size therefore has quite different
commercial implications for a quiet specialist service and a heavily used
shared service.

We checked the calculation with 100,000 independently generated batches in
each of nine combinations: batch sizes 1, 4 and 20, and rates 1, 10 and
60 requests per minute. The raw interarrival times are retained. An
independent reconstruction weights those gaps directly instead of
reconstructing every customer's arrival time. Table~\ref{closure:batch-results}
shows the six nontrivial combinations. The error of the estimated mean is
calculated across independent batches; customers within the same batch are
not incorrectly counted as independent observations.

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering\small
\caption{Generated arrival times reproduce the collection-delay calculation}
\label{closure:batch-results}
\begin{tabular}{rrrrrr}
\toprule
Batch & Requests/min & Exact mean & Simulated mean & MC error & 95th percentile\\
\midrule
4&1&90.000&89.945&0.177&287.06\\
4&10&9.000&9.018&0.018&28.78\\
4&60&1.500&1.501&0.003&4.80\\
20&1&570.000&569.729&0.471&1,252.65\\
20&10&57.000&57.053&0.047&125.41\\
20&60&9.500&9.494&0.008&20.89\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\par\smallskip\footnotesize All times are seconds. MC error is the standard
error of the simulated mean. Percentiles describe individual customer waits,
not uncertainty intervals for the mean. No bank arrival process is fitted.
\end{table}

A waiting-time limit is a natural alternative to waiting indefinitely for a
fixed count. Offline studies can be grouped deliberately; interactive
requests need a delay allowance appropriate to the decision. Neither
calculation describes congestion: even a promptly collected batch can wait
behind earlier work. Evaluating a proposed service therefore requires the
arrival pattern, collection rule, processing time, capacity and cancellation
behavior. The inexpensive generated-arrival exercise establishes the relevant
tradeoff before undertaking that more costly prototype measurement.

\subsection{Price the whole attempt, then divide by useful output}

Define an attempt as a complete, bounded study under a declared procedure.
Its energy $E_i$ includes preparation, retrieval, estimation, verification,
internal retries, abandoned paths and an explicitly allocated share of
idle consumption. Let $A_i$ equal one when independent assessment finds a
useful answer to the intended question, and zero otherwise. The acceptance
rule is fixed before comparing configurations. Under comparable repeated
tasks and a law of large numbers, if $p=E(A_i)>0$ and $e=E(E_i)<\infty$,
\begin{equation}
 \frac{\sum_{i=1}^n E_i}{\sum_{i=1}^n A_i}
       \longrightarrow \frac{e}{p}.
 \label{closure:energy-useful}
\end{equation}
Energy and success may be correlated within an attempt; independence
between these two quantities is not required for this ratio of averages.
This is not the expectation of a finite-sample ratio, which can be undefined
when no useful answer is produced. A configuration with no accepted result
must not acquire an attractive unit cost by dropping its failures.

For configurations $A$ and $B$, configuration $B$ uses less energy per useful
answer precisely when
\begin{equation}
 \frac{p_B}{p_A}>\frac{e_B}{e_A},
 \qquad p_A,p_B>0,\quad e_A>0.
 \label{closure:energy-break-even}
\end{equation}
The acceptance probability must improve, or fall by less than the energy
cost falls. In an exact invented illustration, $e_A=100$ and $e_B=60$
abstract energy units, while $p_A=0.8$ and $p_B=0.4$. The second configuration
uses 40 per cent less energy per attempt but needs 150 units per useful
answer against 125 for the first: 20 per cent more. These numbers are an
arithmetic example, not measurements of models or estimates of bank
performance.

Financial expense requires a further calculation. Electricity is only one
component. With $r_E$ the applicable price per energy unit, analyst time
$H_i$, its relevant cost $r_H$, and other nonduplicated expense $K_i$, define
$C_i=r_E E_i+r_H H_i+K_i$. Then $E(C_i)/p$ is the corresponding long-run
expense per useful answer under the same assumptions. Capacity already
purchased, cloud charges and electricity must be treated consistently to
avoid counting the same cost twice. Waiting is an opportunity cost only
when it delays useful work or a decision; elapsed seconds alone are not a
salary bill. No tariff, staff price, model price or return on investment is
imputed here.

Useful answers also differ in consequence. A justified refusal to estimate
an unidentified effect is valuable protection, but should be reported
separately from a delivered effect estimate. An unsupported estimate that
passes a superficial format check should count as neither. A later trial
should therefore retain both the frequencies and decision consequences of
correct answers, useful refusals, correction, abandonment and misleading
answers. Averaging them into a single convenient success rate would conceal
the very risk the service is supposed to reduce.

\subsection{A bounded investment recommendation}

The available evidence supports a proposal for measurement and comparison,
not a claim that a particular model minimizes a bank's cost. EnergyMeter is
a plausible component after its time boundaries, missing readings and
attribution rules are repaired and tested. The inspected edge-benchmark
scorer should not be adopted unchanged; its missing test invocation is a
specific repairable defect, and redistribution rights for that repository
also need clarification. The comparator's published accuracy rankings
remain excluded until its actual results can be reconstructed.

For the proposed causal service, the first matched comparison should use
the same questions, evidence cutoff, task mix and independent acceptance
criteria for the shared service and the established analyst alternative.
Record every complete attempt and its corrections, including justified
negative findings. Measure full-workflow expense and energy, and show
waiting separately. This makes the eventual prototype experiment worth
funding: it can test whether a saving survives the costs of producing an
answer that a decision-maker can actually use.

\section{A spending sequence with real alternatives}
~~~

After:
~~~text
The software comparison reinforces the separation between evidence retrieval,
graph construction and intervention calculation. A quotation check can test
whether a cited passage supports a claim under a declared predicate without
establishing that claim's causal truth. Similarly, an information ceiling does
not supply calibrated edge probabilities, and the spread of fitted-model
effects does not acquire confidence-interval coverage from repetition alone.
A prototype could support stronger claims only with an authentic complete
implementation, a declared joint graph law or retention procedure, relevant
calibration evidence, and public or synthetic experiments with known
interventions and matched information across comparators. Those experiments
would determine whether the prospective gain warrants bank validation.
~~~

## Edit 196
docs/release/2026-10-29/source/docs/writing-repair/2026-10-29/src/1de93e537048440f.tex

Close the selective-completion and historical-outcome stability gap with a bounded-outcome derivation, retained study and substantive alternatives.

Substantive destination: All inherited chapter14 mathematics, Gaussian servicing study, negative results and full-history recovery argument remain verbatim. New section makes the distinct binary target, confidence-set scope, empty intervals, stability violation and cost assumptions explicit.

Before:
~~~text
alternatives at an acceptable total cost.
~~~
After:
~~~text
alternatives at an acceptable total cost.

\section{When a random invitation still leaves selective recovery}
\label{rel73:partial-recovery}

The full-history design answers the preceding question only when a selected
history can actually be recovered. A bank may instead invite a random sample
of customers whose records are incomplete and receive usable evidence from
only some of them. Customers with a resolved dispute may reply more readily
than those still in difficulty; a collections call can itself alter a later
repayment. Randomizing the invitation does not make the completed responses
representative, nor does it make a later outcome equal to the historical
outcome the bank intended to recover. Both distinctions change the conclusion
that the bank can draw from the follow-up expense.

Consider a simpler decision with a genuinely bounded endpoint. A servicing
offer is randomized before a fixed repayment date. The endpoint is whether
the specified payment was made by that date, and the target is the average
effect of the offer in the experimental customer population. The bank later
tries to recover the historical payment status from incomplete records. This
example concerns a binary historical outcome and a single treatment. It does
not impose artificial finite bounds on the earlier Gaussian servicing rewards
or claim to recover a longitudinal policy value from a terminal indicator.

\subsection{The information supplied by an invitation}

Let $A\in\{0,1\}$ denote randomized treatment and $Y(a)\in\{0,1\}$ the
historical outcome under assignment $a$. Consistency and no interference give
$Y=Y(A)$; random assignment identifies the population contrast
$\tau=E\{Y(1)-Y(0)\}$ from the two full arm means if those outcomes were
observed. Let $R$ indicate that routine records contain $Y$. For customers
with $R=0$, let $B$ indicate an invitation and let $S$ indicate whether the
specified follow-up would recover an outcome if that customer were invited.
An uninvited customer's $S$ is not observed. The design requires
\[
 B\mathbin{\perp}(Y,S)\mid A=a,R=0,\qquad
 \Pr(B=1\mid A=a,R=0)=q_a>0.
\]
The invitation probabilities are known. The outcome among completed
follow-ups must be the same historical $Y$, not a new measurement of a
changed endpoint. Neither routine response nor follow-up completion is
assumed independent of $Y$.

For each arm define the routine success mass, recovered success mass and
remaining mass by
\[
 v_a=E(RY\mid A=a),\qquad
 z_a=E\{(1-R)SY\mid A=a\},\qquad
 m_a=E\{(1-R)(1-S)\mid A=a\}.
\]
Random invitation identifies $z_a$ and $m_a$ through the observed conditional
distribution of follow-up response and outcomes among invitees. It does not
identify the outcome mean in the remaining mass. Binary support consequently
gives arm bounds $\ell_a=v_a+z_a$ and $u_a=\ell_a+m_a$, and effect bounds
\begin{equation}
 I=[\tau_L,\tau_U]=[\ell_1-u_0,\ u_1-\ell_0],\qquad
 \tau_U-\tau_L=m_1+m_0.
 \label{rel73:identified-set}
\end{equation}
This is the binary specialization of the double-sampling bounds developed
by \citet{rel73coppock2017}. Their outcome-stability assumption permits
arbitrary differences between initial respondents and nonrespondents. It
restricts the meaning of the recovered outcome, not the selectivity of the
initial response.

The bounds are attainable within the declared model. Assign outcome zero
to every unresolved treated customer and one to every unresolved control
customer to obtain the lower endpoint; reverse those assignments for the
upper endpoint. These assignments leave the routine observations,
invitation law, completion probabilities and observed follow-up outcomes
unchanged. The joint coupling of the two potential outcomes is otherwise
unrestricted, so the arm completions can coexist in a population. This
establishes sharpness relative to these observed data and assumptions; extra
restrictions on outcomes or selection would define a different model.

Holding the follow-up procedure fixed, changing a positive invitation
probability does not change $m_a$ or the population identified set. It
changes how precisely the available follow-up law can be estimated. A more
effective recovery procedure can reduce $m_a$ and hence identification width.
At $q_a=0$, the follow-up law is not identified in that arm, so the conclusion
cannot be extrapolated to no invitations. If increasing invitations changes
staff attention, response behavior or outcomes, it also changes the procedure;
the fixed-law comparison no longer applies.

\subsection{Estimating a set and covering it are separate operations}

For independent customer observations across and within the two arms,
and invitations drawn independently across customers, known Bernoulli
invitation permits
the following observable scores, with missing products evaluated only for
records whose outcomes are observed:
\begin{equation}
 L=RY+\frac{(1-R)BSY}{q_a},\qquad
 U=RY+(1-R)\left\{1-\frac{BS(1-Y)}{q_a}\right\}.
 \label{rel73:endpoint-scores}
\end{equation}
Conditional expectation over $B$ gives $E(L\mid A=a)=\ell_a$ and
$E(U\mid A=a)=u_a$. Thus
$\widehat\tau_L=\overline L_1-\overline U_0$ and
$\widehat\tau_U=\overline U_1-\overline L_0$ are unbiased endpoint
estimators. These are authored inverse-invitation estimators under Bernoulli
sampling. They are not a native execution of the published package's
sample-ratio estimators or its fixed-follow-up-size variance calculation.

Inverse weighting can give an individual score outside the outcome support.
Here $L\in[0,1/q_a]$ and $U\in[1-1/q_a,1]$, so each range has length
$1/q_a$. In a finite sample the two estimated endpoints can cross. Sorting
them or concealing such a sample would change the declared procedure. The
raw estimates therefore remain in the results, along with any empty
confidence set.

A conservative finite-sample guarantee follows directly from the score
ranges. Let $n_a$ be the number of independent customers in arm $a$, and put
\begin{equation}
 D=\frac{1}{n_1q_1^2}+\frac{1}{n_0q_0^2},\qquad
 t_\alpha=\sqrt{\frac{D}{2}\log\frac{2}{\alpha}},\qquad
 C_\alpha=[\widehat\tau_L-t_\alpha,
            \widehat\tau_U+t_\alpha]\cap[-1,1].
 \label{rel73:confidence-set}
\end{equation}
Under the stated independence, design and stability assumptions,
$\Pr(I\subseteq C_\alpha)\geq1-\alpha$. The guarantee covers the entire
population identified interval, and consequently the true effect within it.
It does not say that the reported set is narrow enough to support an action.

For completeness, the argument needs no estimated variance. If a random
variable has range length $c$, the second derivative of its centered
log-moment-generating function is its variance under exponential tilting.
That variance is at most $c^2/4$: the squared distance from the range
midpoint is bounded by $c^2/4$, and the variance is no larger. Integrating
twice from zero gives a log-moment bound of $s^2c^2/8$. Independence then
bounds either centered endpoint estimate's log moment by $s^2D/8$.
Exponential Markov inequality, minimized at $s=4t/D$, bounds either adverse
tail by $\exp(-2t^2/D)$. Applying this to overestimation of the lower endpoint
and underestimation of the upper endpoint, and summing the two failure
probabilities, proves~\eqref{rel73:confidence-set}. Dependence between the
two endpoint estimates is allowed; independence of the customers used in
the product argument is not optional. Intersection with $[-1,1]$ preserves
coverage because the population identified interval is inside that support.

The comparison also reports normal endpoint bands using sample variances of
the scores and a 1.96 multiplier on each outward standard error. Their
behavior is empirical here; the preceding finite-sample guarantee does not
transfer to them. The original article's Imbens--Manski construction instead
targets asymptotic coverage of the true effect, which is a different
criterion from simultaneous containment of both endpoints. A software label
such as \emph{confidence interval around the region} does not erase this
distinction. \citep{rel73coppock2017,rel73attrition}

\subsection{Selective completion can reverse a point conclusion}

The retained generated experiment has historical arm means .45 and .55,
so the true effect is .10. Routine observation probabilities, indexed by
outcome zero and one, are $(.8,.4)$ in control and $(.4,.8)$ in treatment.
Both arms therefore have routine response probability .62, but their observed
success means differ sharply. Equal response rates do not imply equal
selection. The routine-only complete-case contrast is approximately .4194,
while routine-only worst-case bounds are $[-.12,.64]$.

Three stable recovery procedures vary successful completion among invited
nonresponders. The standard procedure has probabilities $(.1,.9)$ in control
and $(.9,.2)$ in treatment; the intensive procedure has $(.8,.98)$ and
$(.98,.8)$ respectively; complete recovery has probability one in both
arms. These probabilities define the generated truth. The estimator is not
given the outcome-dependent selection law or the true arm means.

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering\small
\begin{tabular}{lrrr}
\toprule
Recovery procedure & Population effect set & Width & Naive limit\\
\midrule
Routine records only & $[-.1200,.6400]$ & .7600 & .4194\\
Standard & $[-.0870,.1540]$ & .2410 & $-.0720$\\
Intensive & $[.0560,.1108]$ & .0548 & .0697\\
Complete & $[.1000,.1000]$ & .0000 & .1000\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{Exact generated population quantities. The routine-only naive
contrast uses complete cases. The other naive limits replace every routine
nonrespondent's outcome by the mean among completed follow-ups in that arm.
All stable sets contain the true effect .10.}
\label{rel73:population}
\end{table}

Under standard recovery, successful control follow-ups overwhelmingly reveal
historical successes, whereas successful treated follow-ups disproportionately
reveal failures. Treating those replies as representative produces the
negative limiting contrast in Table~\ref{rel73:population}. A random
invitation has not repaired selective completion. The valid set retains
both signs; the intensive procedure supplies enough extra outcome information
to exclude zero at the population level.

Each procedure is evaluated in 500 independent datasets with 2,000 customers
per arm. A fourth, deliberately unstable procedure is discussed below.
Within a dataset, shared invitation uniforms compare probabilities
.10, .25, .50 and 1.00. There are 2,000 independent generated datasets and
8,000 procedure--invitation-rate cells, not 8,000 independent datasets.
All cells remain in the denominator. Reaggregation checks every retained
endpoint, interval indicator, resource total and summary; direct observed-cell
sums and squares reconstruct endpoint estimates and standard errors from
the first, middle and last retained raw datasets in each procedure.

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering\small
\begin{tabular}{llrrrr}
\toprule
Procedure & Invitation & \multicolumn{2}{c}{Whole-set coverage}
 & \multicolumn{2}{c}{Mean set width}\\
 & probability & Bounded & Normal & Bounded & Normal\\
\midrule
Standard & .10 & 100.0\% & 97.6\% & 1.100 & .376\\
Standard & 1.00 & 99.2\% & 96.0\% & .327 & .302\\
Intensive & .10 & 100.0\% & 96.6\% & .915 & .220\\
Intensive & 1.00 & 99.4\% & 94.8\% & .141 & .117\\
Complete & .10 & 100.0\% & 98.0\% & .860 & .172\\
Complete & 1.00 & 99.2\% & 95.4\% & .086 & .062\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{Selected endpoints of the retained invitation comparison, with
500 replications per row and nominal 95\% confidence. Bounded refers to
equation~\eqref{rel73:confidence-set}; Normal is the separately assessed
normal band. Coverage requires containment of the entire population set.
An empty confidence set has width zero and fails coverage.}
\label{rel73:coverage}
\end{table}

The bounded construction buys a finite-sample statement with substantial
conservatism. Under intensive recovery at invitation probability .10 it
declares no positive effects, even though the population lower endpoint is
.056. At probability one it returns a nonempty strictly positive set in
401 of 500 datasets. The corresponding normal-band counts are 193 and
469. Mean invitations rise from about 152 to 1,521, and mean completed
recoveries from about 141 to 1,411. These counts describe information burden,
not bank collection prices or the superiority of either interval rule.

At 94.8\% coverage the Monte Carlo standard error is approximately one
percentage point. The difference from nominal 95\% therefore does not by
itself establish miscalibration; the theorem and its absence for the normal
band remain separate from these empirical proportions. Across all four
invitation rates, raw endpoint crossings occur in 108 intensive-recovery
cells and 712 complete-recovery cells. One complete-recovery normal band is
empty. None is removed from coverage counts, and no empty set is treated
as a supported positive decision. The original signed endpoint separations
remain available alongside the nonnegative set widths used here.

\subsection{A recovered record can concern the wrong outcome}

The fourth procedure completes every invited follow-up but changes the
recorded endpoint for half of the originally unsuccessful, routinely missing
treated customers. It leaves control outcomes unchanged. This deliberately
violates historical-outcome stability. The apparent identified point becomes
.235 although the historical treatment effect remains .10. Inviting more
customers now estimates the wrong contrast more precisely.

At invitation probabilities .10, .25, .50 and 1.00, the bounded sets cover
the historical .10 in 100.0\%, 90.4\%, 0.2\% and 0.0\% of the 500
datasets. The normal bands cover it in 7.0\%, 0.2\%, 0.0\% and 0.0\%.
The wide first band's accidental coverage is not evidence that the stability
assumption is dispensable. At full invitation the bounded construction
covers the different recorded-outcome point .235 in all 500 datasets,
while the normal band covers it in 472. Neither statement validates
inference for the historical target.

A sensitivity analysis must restrict the error that actually matters.
Let $k_a=E\{(1-R)S\mid A=a\}$ and, for $k_a>0$, let
$\widetilde\mu_a$ be the mean recorded outcome among recoverable customers.
A zero recovered mass contributes zero without defining that mean.
Suppose external information
justifies an absolute bound $\gamma_a$ on the difference between that mean
and their historical-outcome mean. The recovered contribution then lies in
\[
 k_a\max(0,\widetilde\mu_a-\gamma_a)
 \ \leq\ E\{(1-R)SY\mid A=a\}\ \leq\
 k_a\min(1,\widetilde\mu_a+\gamma_a).
\]
Adding the routine contribution and unresolved mass gives a sensitivity set
by the same arm subtraction as~\eqref{rel73:identified-set}. This is a
population sensitivity calculation. Estimating its endpoints would require
an additional sampling argument; the original confidence formula cannot be
attached without checking the altered scores and assumptions.

In the unstable construction, $k_1=.38$, the recovered treated success mass
is .245, and its historical value is .11. If control recovery is stable,
the smallest treated mean-drift bound that admits the truth is
$\gamma_1=.135/.38=27/76$, approximately .3553. At $\gamma_1=.30$ the
effect sensitivity set is $[.121,.349]$ and still excludes .10; at .40 it
is $[.083,.370]$. These are stipulated limits on outcome drift, not estimates
learned from the incomplete data.

The distinction matters when choosing software. The inspected
\texttt{attrition} 1.0.0 source at its 21 September 2026 revision implements
the paper's double-sampling bounds, analytic variance and normal critical-value
calculation. Its package description calls the sensitivity analysis a check
on outcome stability, but the function documentation and
\texttt{gen\_mean} implement a different restriction: a fraction $1-\delta$
of follow-up nonresponders has the responder mean, while the remaining
fraction $\delta$ receives worst-case outcomes. That is the paper's
nonresponse-mixture sensitivity, not a bound on historical-outcome drift.
When recovery is complete, there are no follow-up nonresponders and changing
$\delta$ cannot alter the erroneous .235 contrast. Static inspection
establishes this distinction; it is not a native package trial.
\citep{rel73attrition,rel73coppock2017}

\subsection{The information purchase and its alternatives}

The available implementation is a serious comparator for bounded randomized
experiments with the declared follow-up design. Its sample-ratio estimator,
asymptotic variance and poststratification should be compared under matched
sampling rules and targets. The proposed inverse-invitation calculation makes
known design probabilities and a conservative finite-sample guarantee explicit,
but its crossed endpoints and wide intervals are costs of that choice. Neither
route validates the other merely because their population limits agree.
The package declares GPL-3; a proposed integration must retain its version,
applicable terms and dependency record. No enabled integration or measured
runtime advantage is claimed. \citep{rel73attrition}

Other options change the information or assumptions. A reliable administrative
linkage may recover the fixed historical outcome more credibly than a later
customer report. An intensive protocol can reduce the unresolved mass, while
more invitations to the same protocol mainly improve precision. A model that
assumes completion is ignorable conditional on recorded covariates can support
a point estimate, but it must justify that assumption and diagnose overlap.
The package's trimming option instead targets customers whose outcomes would
be observed under either treatment, with selection assumptions explicitly
chosen. That always-observed target can be useful, but it is not the full
experimental-population effect in this section. Equal observed response rates
alone do not establish individual response monotonicity or justify changing
the target without saying so. \citep{rel73attrition}

To connect identification with a financial decision, stipulate a benefit of
USD~100 for each additional historical repayment and an offer cost of USD~5
per customer. These invented values define net benefit $100\tau-5$, not
observed bank returns. The standard-recovery population set permits net
values from USD~$-13.70$ to USD~10.40. Intensive recovery narrows this to
USD~0.60 through USD~6.08; complete stable recovery gives USD~5.00. Those
are population implications. A finite-sample spending recommendation needs
its own nonempty net-benefit confidence set above zero, and follow-up costs
must also be counted. A positive effect alone is insufficient to clear the
USD~5 threshold.

The resulting design choice is concrete. The bank should establish whether
follow-up recovers the fixed historical endpoint, record every invitation and
completion, and compare the cost of reducing the unresolved mass with the
cost of estimating a fixed set more precisely. A workflow that cannot defend
stability should expose an appropriately justified sensitivity range or
withhold the historical-effect recommendation. These generated results close
the missing selective-completion argument for a bounded single-treatment
example. They do not establish feasible bank recovery, a general solution
for unbounded longitudinal rewards, or independent operating acceptance.
~~~
